\chapter{Changes Since Fortress 1.0 Specifications}
\applabel{spec-changes}

\note{Changes Since
http://projectfortress.sun.com/Projects/Community/changeset/1444}

\section{Changes Made by the 2026 Revival to the Working Draft}
\seclabel{revival-changes}
\sloppy

This section lists the changes made to this working draft by the 2026
revival of the Fortress project (\url{https://github.com/palimondo/fortress}).
The revival works to finish what the designers intended, judged by this
working draft, and changes the text only where a decision it has taken for
the implementation would otherwise leave the text and the implementation in
open disagreement.
Each changed passage carries a box headed
``Revised by the 2026 revival'' that points to its entry here.

Each entry gives the affected sections, the change, its rationale, its
effect, the original text, and what reversing to the alternative not taken
would require.
The original text is that of the \emph{Working Draft of February 2011},
the unrevised copy of this document kept in the revival's repository under
the directory \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/}, cited by path and line
there.
The directory's name is misleading: its sources are the working draft of
2~February 2011, and only the file \nolinkurl{fortress.1.0.pdf} in it is the
Fortress~1.0 release.
The full reasoning behind every entry, with each original passage and each
new one, is in the revival's decision records,
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-route-a/decision-record.md}
and, for the number chapters,
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-numbers/decision-record.md},
in the same repository.

Each change below follows a decision of the revival.
The first of those decisions, taken on
24~September 2026 and called \emph{route~A}, is that the compiled type checker keeps
the type group's rule that no type is a subtype of two different
instantiations of one parameterized trait, a rule this working draft never
stated, and that the library's numeric types are reorganized so that they
satisfy it.
An entry whose change does not follow from route~A says so, and names the
decision it follows or gives its reason.
The revision of the number chapters (\secref{revival-numbers} to
\secref{revival-reductions}) also carries out three decisions of
26~September 2026 that follow from route~A: on the refined number types, on
conversions between number types, and on sums and products.
The alternative not taken, \emph{route~C}, is described once, in
\secref{revival-routec}.

\subsection{Instantiation exclusion}
\seclabel{revival-rule}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{trait-types}; \secref{trait-decls}.
\item[Change] A property of the exclusion relation is added:
two instantiations of the same parameterized trait exclude each other
unless every static argument other than an operator argument is the same.
The rule it imposes on declarations is added beside the example of
\TYP{InclusiveMolecule}: a trait or object may not extend two such
instantiations, directly or through its supertraits.
\item[Rationale] Without the rule a value may belong to two instantiations
of one parameterized trait, and code specialised for one of them can be
chosen for a caller that was promised the other; with the rule relaxed, the
compiled implementation accepts such a program and fails at run time.
The type group adopted the rule as \emph{multiple instantiation exclusion}
(Allen, Hilburn, Kilpatrick, Luchangco, Ryu, Chase, and Steele, ``Type
Checking Modular Multiple Dispatch with Parametric Polymorphism and
Multiple Inheritance'', OOPSLA 2011), and the compiled type checker has
enforced it since 2010.
The team's later, unfinished Types chapter (Luchangco, 2012) states it in
the two parts used here, under the name \emph{instantiation exclusion}.
The type group's paper of 2019 states it as its rule [Anc-Same-Trait], which
it credits to Steele and Chase in 2012: a class that extends two instances of
a trait extends an instance below both, which for an invariant static
parameter is the same instance (Park, Hong, Steele and Ryu, ``Polymorphic
Symmetric Multiple Dispatch with Variance'', POPL 2019, Figure~4).
Operator arguments do not count: an operator argument only names the
operator a subtrait inherits (\secref{opr-ident}), neither implementation
distinguishes instantiations by it at run time, and the algebra of the
library chapters depends on types that are several instantiations of one
trait differing only in operator arguments.
\KWD{nat}, \KWD{int} and \KWD{bool} arguments count: they are values at
run time (\secref{natparams}, \secref{boolparams}).
\item[Effect] Programs in which a type is a subtype of two instantiations
of one parameterized trait that differ in any other static argument are no
longer valid. The compiled type checker refuses them where the arguments
that differ are types, or numerals given as \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int}
arguments (row~402 of the revival's gap ledger). It does not yet refuse
them where those arguments are \KWD{bool} arguments, though it accepts boolean parameters written out (row~406),
or where one of them is a static parameter of the trait or object that extends both (row~414).
The interpreter does not check the rule.
The number chapters are revised to the reorganized numeric types
(\secref{revival-numbers}).
\item[Original text] The Working Draft of February 2011 states no such
property; its relations are ``the smallest ones that satisfy all the
properties given in those sections (and this one)''
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/types-vals-vars.tex}, lines
185--189), and the properties it gives for trait types are these (lines
212--216):
\begin{quote}
A trait declaration may also include an \KWD{excludes} clause,
in which case the defined trait type
excludes every type that appears in that clause.
Every trait type also excludes every arrow type and every tuple type,
and the special types \TYP{()} and \TYP{BottomType}.
\end{quote}
Its rule on declarations is only that of exclusive traits,
``no trait can extend them both''
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/traits.tex}, lines 218--222).
\item[Route C] Replaces this rule by the rule of \secref{revival-routec}.
\end{description}

\subsection{Where-clause variables in extends clauses}
\seclabel{revival-where}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{where-clauses}; \secref{trait-decls}.
\item[Change] One sentence is added: a \KWD{where}-clause variable may not
appear as a static argument in an \KWD{extends} clause.
The three declarations that followed it are kept, marked
``Not allowed'', each with the way to write it
(\secref{revival-covariance}, \secref{revival-every},
\secref{revival-empty}).
A box after Victor Luchangco's two draft notes in \secref{trait-decls},
which say that a trait can explicitly extend itself through hidden type
variables and that ``we could just rule this a special case'', answers
them.
\item[Rationale] Such a variable makes the declared type a subtype of one
instantiation for each type the variable may stand for, and instantiation
exclusion forbids any two of them.
\item[Effect] None of the three declarations ran before the revision:
both implementations stop at name resolution, because a
\KWD{where}-clause variable is not in scope in an \KWD{extends} clause.
The revision makes that the rule.
The calculus of \appref{where-calculus} still admits such
declarations (\secref{revival-calculi}).
\item[Original text] No such sentence; the draft notes are unchanged.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C, which places no type below
infinitely many instantiations of one trait.
\end{description}

\subsection{Conditional extension}
\seclabel{revival-conditional}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{where-clauses}; \secref{partial-total-orders};
the overview of operators in \chapref{operators}.
\item[Change] A paragraph and an example are added, with a box: where the
\KWD{where} clause of an entry of an \KWD{extends} clause introduces no
\KWD{where}-clause variable and all its constraints are trait constraints,
the declared trait or object extends the entry's type exactly where the
constraints hold, and the constraints hold while the entry itself, the
declaration's value parameters and its members are checked, and not
while the rest of the declaration is, which holds at every instance: its
static parameters, its own \KWD{where} clause, its \KWD{throws} clause
and contract, its \KWD{excludes} and \KWD{comprises} clauses and the
other entries of its \KWD{extends} clause;
the example is
\library's list, totally ordered exactly where its elements are.
Callouts at the lexicographic order of \secref{partial-total-orders} and at
the comparison of lists in the overview of operators say that a list is
ordered only where its element type is totally ordered, and that the
interpreter still orders every list.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival
decided on 16~October 2026 to build the forms of \KWD{where} clause the team
wrote down, among them that a trait that extends another under a
\KWD{where} condition is that type only where the condition holds and that
its members are checked under the condition, as Haskell checks an
instance's context, Rust a conditional implementation and Swift a
conditional conformance.
The grammar gives each entry of an \KWD{extends} clause its own optional
\KWD{where} clause, and the text orders lists ``whose elements support these
same comparison operators''
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/operators/opr-overview.tex},
lines 284--285), which no
unconditional declaration of the list type can state:
\library\ declared their list type as lexicographically ordered at every
element type, so the compiled type checker refused the comparison of two
elements of an unbounded type in the order's own body.
The team's internal notes foresaw conditional subtyping and its cost,
that ``we may also not be able to tell whether a trait has a particular
method'' (\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/appendices/internal-document.tex},
lines 538--543, in its subsection ``No Wrapped Fields Whose Types Are Naked
Type Variables'').
A clause that introduces a \KWD{where}-clause variable, or whose
constraints are not all trait constraints, keeps the reading it had: the
entry is extended at every instance.
\library's list keeps an element-wise \EXP{=} of its own outside the
condition, so that a list whose elements are not ordered, a list of pairs
among them, is still compared element by element.
The alternative not taken is to read the clause as limiting nothing, the
extension holding at every instance, under which the clause has no effect
and the list's order needs another construct.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-checker-conditional-extension/REPORT.md}
in the revival's repository, and for the two callouts in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-slices-list-equality/REPORT.md}.
\item[Effect] The compiled type checker reads such a clause as this
section says: a value of an instance whose static arguments satisfy the
constraints is of the entry's type, one whose static arguments do not is
refused where that type is expected, and the entry, the declaration's
value parameters and its members are checked with the constraints
holding, the rest of the declaration without them.
Of that rest, the compiled type checker never sees a trait's or an
object's \KWD{throws} clause or contract: the disambiguator drops both, so
neither is checked, and neither path evaluates an object's contract
(row~781).
\library's \TYP{LexicographicOrder} bounds its element type by
\TYP{StandardTotalOrder}, and its list extends it under the constraint
that its element type does, so the compiled type checker orders a list
exactly where its elements are totally ordered.
It gives no order to a list of partially ordered elements, of \TYP{RR64},
\TYP{QQ} or ranges among them, where \secref{partial-total-orders} says
that a lexicographic order ``may be partial or total depending on whether the
ordering of the elements is partial or total''
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/advanced-lib/algebraic-constraints.tex},
lines 520--522), and where the operators chapter's subsection
``Comparisons Operators'' compares lists ``whose elements support these same
comparison operators'', as floating-point numbers do: no partial
lexicographic order of lists is declared.
The compiled type checker still lets a member that it checked with the
constraints holding be called where they fail: it accepts a call of such
a member on an instance whose static arguments do not satisfy them
(row~773 of the revival's gap ledger).
Its other readers of an \KWD{extends} clause, the members a type inherits
and its checks of the type hierarchy, read the entry as extended at every
instance.
An entry under a clause that introduces a \KWD{where}-clause variable or
holds a constraint that is not a trait constraint, as the entries of the
team's sketch of conditional subtyping do
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/appendices/internal-document.tex},
lines 483--512, its subsection ``Conditional Subtyping''), is read as
extended at every instance on both paths.
The interpreter reads the extension as holding at every instance, and so
does the compiled run time: a type test of an instance whose static
arguments fail the constraints answers \VAR{true} on both
(row~774, gated for the interpreter by
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/XXXConditionalExtensionTypeTestWalk.fss}),
so the interpreter keeps the order of a list of tuples or of floating-point
numbers.
The code generator compiles a declaration whose \KWD{extends} clause has
such a clause, and one that carries a \KWD{where} clause of its own, for
whose constraints, which the type checker checks, it emits nothing; a compiled
call of a function or method whose own clause introduces a \KWD{where}-clause
variable links and dies when it runs (row~867 of the revival's gap ledger).
\item[Original text] No such paragraph. The grammar is unchanged:
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/traits.tex}, line 83,
\begin{quote}
\emph{TraitTypeWhere} ::= \emph{TraitType} \emph{Where}?
\end{quote}
and the front matter lists ``where clauses and conditional extension''
among the features not yet supported
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/fortress/preamble.tex}, line 82).
The two passages that order lists are unchanged
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/advanced-lib/algebraic-constraints.tex},
lines 519--522, and
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/operators/opr-overview.tex},
lines 284--285); each gains its callout.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The covariance example}
\seclabel{revival-covariance}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{where-clauses}.
\item[Change] The declaration is kept, marked ``Not allowed''; the claims
that trait declarations may extend other instantiations of themselves and
that the declaration expresses covariance are withdrawn; static
parameters are stated to be invariant, and a widening function with
bounded static parameters is given as the way to convert one
instantiation to another.
\item[Rationale] \EXP{C\llbracket{}S\rrbracket} would be a subtype of
\EXP{C\llbracket{}T\rrbracket} for every supertype \VAR{T} of \VAR{S}.
The widening function is how the library writes covariance, in the
conversions and the \OPR{APPCOV} operator of its covariant collections,
and it runs on both implementations.
\item[Effect] Covariance is written as an explicit conversion, with the
static arguments given at the call. The box at the example adds that the
type group's later design declares variance with a \KWD{covariant}
modifier, implemented on neither implementation; the decision record and
row~404 of the revival's gap ledger say what is known of it.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/trait-parameters.tex},
lines 339--351; the declaration is the one kept above, marked
``Not allowed'', and the text around it read:
\begin{quote}
Trait declarations are allowed to extend other instantiations of
themselves. For example, we can write:
[the declaration]
In this declaration, for every subtype \VAR{S} of \VAR{T}, \EXP{C\llbracket{}S\rrbracket}
is a subtype of \EXP{C\llbracket{}T\rrbracket}. Effectively, we have expressed
  the fact that the static parameter \VAR{S} of \VAR{C} is covariant.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{A subtrait of every instantiation}
\seclabel{revival-every}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{where-clauses}.
\item[Change] ``the following trait declaration is legal'' becomes
``is not allowed''; the paragraph after the declaration is kept, in the
conditional, and a closing sentence says why the declaration is not
allowed.
\item[Rationale] The paragraph is the team's own account of why such a
trait is dangerous, written before the rule; it becomes the reason for the
rule.
\item[Effect] None beyond \secref{revival-where}.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/trait-parameters.tex},
lines 354--380; the declaration is the one kept above, and the text around
it read:
\begin{quote}
Trait declarations need not have any static parameters in order
to have a \KWD{where} clause. For example, the following trait
declaration is legal:
[the declaration]
In this declaration, trait \VAR{C} is a subtrait of \emph{every}
instantiation of parametric trait \VAR{D}. Thus, trait \VAR{C} has all
of the methods of every instantiation of \VAR{D}. By thinking of the
declaration this way, we can see what restrictions we need to impose on
the trait \VAR{C} in order for it to be sensible. [The rest of the
paragraph is unchanged.]
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The empty list}
\seclabel{revival-empty}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{where-clauses}.
\item[Change] The declaration of \TYP{Empty} is kept, marked
``Not allowed''; \EXP{\TYP{Empty}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket}, one empty list
for each element type, is given beside it.
\item[Rationale] The original makes \TYP{Empty} a
\EXP{\TYP{List}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket} for every \VAR{T} at once.
The replacement is the library's own shape for its empty collections and
the same choice as for \TYP{Nothing} (\secref{revival-nothing}); a program
of this shape runs on both implementations.
\item[Effect] The element type is written at each use, as in
\EXP{\TYP{Empty}\llbracket\mathbb{Z}32\rrbracket}, until the static
arguments of a generic object can be inferred, which neither
implementation does today. The section no longer has a running example of
a \KWD{where} clause on an object.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/trait-parameters.tex},
lines 383--399; the declaration is the one kept above, introduced by:
\begin{quote}
Object or functional declarations may include \KWD{where} clauses.
Here is an example declaration of an \TYP{Empty} list:
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The empty optional value}
\seclabel{revival-nothing}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{convenience-types};
\secref{chained-exceptions}; \chapref{lib:exception}.
\item[Change] \TYP{Nothing} becomes
\EXP{\KWD{value}\;\;\KWD{object}\:\TYP{Nothing}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket}
extending \EXP{\TYP{Maybe}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket}, with no
\KWD{excludes} clause, and \TYP{Maybe} comprises
\EXP{\TYP{Nothing}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket} and
\EXP{\TYP{Just}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket}; the original declaration is kept,
marked ``Not allowed''. The defaults of an exception's message and chain
become \EXP{\TYP{Nothing}\llbracket\TYP{String}\rrbracket} and
\EXP{\TYP{Nothing}\llbracket\TYP{Exception}\rrbracket}.
\item[Rationale] The original makes \TYP{Nothing} a
\EXP{\TYP{Maybe}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket} for every \VAR{T} at once. Its
\KWD{excludes} clause is also one the grammar gives no object declaration
(\secref{object-decls}), and is redundant, an object trait type already
excluding every type that is not its supertype (\secref{object-traits}).
The replacement is the library's declaration.
\item[Effect] The empty case is written with its type, as in
\EXP{\TYP{Nothing}\llbracket\TYP{String}\rrbracket}, until the static
arguments of a generic object can be inferred from the type expected.
The compiled path's own library, used until the one library replaces it,
still declares a single \TYP{Nothing} with a coercion to every
\EXP{\TYP{Maybe}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket}, and refuses
\EXP{\TYP{Nothing}\llbracket\TYP{String}\rrbracket}
(row~331 of the revival's gap ledger).
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/convenience.tex},
lines 34--53: the declaration kept above, marked ``Not allowed'', together
with:
\begin{quote}
An optional value \VAR{v} is either \TYP{Nothing} or
\EXP{\TYP{Just}(v)} declared as follows:
[\ldots]
\begin{Fortress}
\(\KWD{trait} \TYP{Maybe}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket \KWD{comprises} \{\,\TYP{Nothing}, \TYP{Just}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket\,\}\)
\end{Fortress}
[\ldots]
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/exceptions.tex}, lines 111--130:
\begin{quote}
These fields are default to \TYP{Nothing} as follows: [\ldots]
\begin{Fortress}
{\tt~~}\pushtabs\=\+\(  \KWD{getter} \VAR{message}()\COLON \TYP{Maybe}\llbracket\TYP{String}\rrbracket = \TYP{Nothing}\)\\
\(  \ldots\)\\
\(  \KWD{getter} \VAR{chain}()\COLON \TYP{Maybe}\llbracket\TYP{Exception}\rrbracket = \TYP{Nothing}\)\-\\\poptabs
\end{Fortress}
[\ldots]
where an optional value \VAR{v} is either \TYP{Nothing} or
\EXP{\TYP{Just}(v)}.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/exception.tex}, lines 22--24:
\begin{quote}
These fields are default to \TYP{Nothing}
where an optional value \VAR{v} is either \TYP{Nothing} or
\EXP{\TYP{Just}(v)} as declared in \secref{convenience-types}.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The root's algebra}
\seclabel{revival-object}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \chapref{lib:object}.
\item[Change] \TYP{Object} extends \TYP{Any} alone and \TYP{Tuple} likewise,
each still excluding the other; the sentences saying that they describe
their operators with algebraic constraints are removed. The original
declaration of \TYP{Object} is kept, marked ``Not allowed''.
\item[Rationale] Every trait but \TYP{Any}, \TYP{Object} and \TYP{Index}
extends \TYP{Object}, and every commutative algebraic type \VAR{T} is an
\EXP{\TYP{EquivalenceRelation}\llbracket{}T,=\rrbracket}; with the
original declaration such a type is also an
\EXP{\TYP{EquivalenceRelation}\llbracket\TYP{Object},\sequiv\rrbracket},
and the two differ in a type argument. \TYP{Tuple}'s declaration is not
refused by the rule, since no tuple type extends a generic trait (a tuple
of indices is a subtype of the trait \TYP{Index}, which takes no static
parameter, \secref{revival-index}), but its text follows \TYP{Object}'s. The replacement is the library's declaration.
\item[Effect] The properties of \EXP{\sequiv} are those stated in trait
\TYP{Any}; they are no longer restated at \TYP{Object} through the
algebra chapter.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/objects.tex}
(whose lines are two fewer than this document's source), line 82:
\begin{quote}
In \TYP{Object} this property is asserted abstractly using the algebraic constraints of \chapref{lib:algebraic-constraints}.
\end{quote}
lines 125--139, the declaration kept above, marked ``Not allowed'', after:
\begin{quote}
The \TYP{Object} type does not
have any additional methods, but uses algebraic constraints (see
\chapref{lib:algebraic-constraints}) to describe the properties of the
existing operators more abstractly.
\end{quote}
and lines 145--156:
\begin{quote}
As with the type \TYP{Object}, the type \TYP{Tuple} uses algebraic constraints to describe its existing methods.
\begin{Fortress}
\(\KWD{trait}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{Tuple} \KWD{extends} \{\,\TYP{Any}, \TYP{EquivalenceRelation}\llbracket\TYP{Tuple},\sequiv\rrbracket, \TYP{IdentityOperator}\llbracket\TYP{Tuple}\rrbracket\,\}\)\\
{\tt~~~~}\pushtabs\=\+\(    \KWD{excludes} \{\,\TYP{Object}\,\}\)\-\\\poptabs
{\tt~~}\pushtabs\=\+\(  \VAR{toString}()\COLON \TYP{String}\)\-\\\poptabs
\(\KWD{end}\)
\end{Fortress}
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] By the revival's reading, not measured, the original
declaration of \TYP{Object} is one of the two passages that would read
differently under route~C, the numeric chapters being the other.
\end{description}

\subsection{The internal document}
\seclabel{revival-internal}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] In the appendix ``Internal Document'', which
follows this one in a draft build: ``Disjunctive Constraints'',
``Weirdness with Multiple Inheritance and Polymorphism'' and
``Conditional Subtyping''.
\item[Change] One box after each: the first program's premise is not
allowed; the second is the case the rule answers; the third declaration is
not allowed for the reason the covariance example is not.
\item[Rationale] Instantiation exclusion.
\item[Effect] None; the internal document is printed only in a draft
build.
\item[Original text] Unchanged.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The calculi}
\seclabel{revival-calculi}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] In \appref{calculi}: \basiccore\
(Section~\ref{basic-calculus}), \wherecore\ (\appref{where-calculus}),
\overloadingcore\ (\appref{overloading-calculus}) and \acffdcore\
(Section~\ref{acffd-calculus}).
\item[Change] One box at the opening of each calculus, saying that the
calculus predates instantiation exclusion and naming the shape it admits
that the rule refuses: in \basiccore, an object that extends two different
instantiations of a parameterized trait declaring no method, so that its
statement that all of its valid programs are valid Fortress programs no
longer holds for every program; in \wherecore, a \KWD{where}-clause
variable in a supertype, which its subtyping rule turns into one supertype
for each witness of the variable; in \overloadingcore, the same object as
in \basiccore, which its rule for valid method declarations admits because
the trait declares no method; in \acffdcore, a trait that extends an
instantiation of itself at its parameter's bound, which conditions (2)
and (3) of its rule for an acyclic type hierarchy admit.
The rules of the calculi and their soundness claims are not changed.
\item[Rationale] The calculi are the team's formal record of this working
draft as it stood, and the rule changes the language, not that record.
New premises in their rules would be new formal work, and would reopen
soundness claims that were proved for the rules as they are written.
The revival decided on this treatment on 26~September 2026.
\item[Effect] None on the language or on either implementation.
The four calculi admit programs, in the shapes named, that are not valid
Fortress programs.
\item[Original text] The calculi are unchanged. The statement that the box
in \basiccore\ qualifies is in
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/appendices/calculi/basic/calculus.tex},
lines 16--19:
\begin{quote}
Following the precedent set by prior core calculi such as Featherweight
Generic Java~\cite{fgj},
we have abided by the restriction that all valid \basiccore\ programs are
valid Fortress programs.
\end{quote}
The shapes the other three boxes name are admitted by
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/appendices/calculi/where/syntax.tex},
lines 82--91, and
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/appendices/calculi/where/static.tex},
lines 53--81 and 329--342, by
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/appendices/calculi/overloading/static.tex},
lines 52--82, 107--136 and 270--275, and by
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/appendices/calculi/acffd/static.tex},
lines 46--88.
Before this entry, \secref{revival-notrevised} said of the calculi:
\begin{quote}
The calculi of \appref{calculi} predate the rule and are not yet revised:
the calculus with \KWD{where} clauses (\appref{where-calculus}) lets a
\KWD{where}-clause variable occur in a supertype, the calculus with an
acyclic type hierarchy admits a trait that extends an instantiation of
itself at its parameter's bound, and the basic calculus states that all of
its valid programs are valid Fortress programs, while it admits an object
that extends two instantiations of a parameterized trait declaring no
method.
How they are to be revised awaits a decision of the revival.
\end{quote}
That paragraph did not name \overloadingcore, whose box was added after
the other three.
\item[Route C] By the revival's reading, not measured, the same under
route~C: none of the shapes named is a chain of instantiations of a
self-typed parameterized trait, the one case route~C relaxes.
\end{description}

\subsection{The number types}
\seclabel{revival-numbers}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{basic-rationals}; \secref{basic-integers}.
\item[Change] The eighteen rational types and the twelve integer types of the
original, with \EXP{\mathbb{N}} and \EXP{\mathbb{N}^*}, each a subtype of others,
are replaced by seven number types \library\ declare: \EXP{\mathbb{Z}},
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}32},
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}} and \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, siblings under \TYP{Number}, with the
coercions the library declares: \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} from \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and
\EXP{\mathbb{N}32}; \EXP{\mathbb{N}64} from \EXP{\mathbb{N}32}; \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}
from the four fixed-size types; \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} from the five integer types;
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} from \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and from integer numerals.
Every other conversion between them is explicit, the one into \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} written \EXP{\VAR{asFloat}(x)} with the method that each number type declares (\secref{revival-asfloat}).
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}} includes \EXP{+\infty}, \EXP{-\infty} and \EXP{0/0}; the integer
types hold no infinite value.
A subset such as the positive values is tested at run time, and the two checks
the library declares, \VAR{check} and \EXP{\VAR{check}^*} on \EXP{\mathbb{R}64},
returning \EXP{\TYP{Maybe}\llbracket\mathbb{R}64\rrbracket}, are stated.
The sentences that the type system tracks the refined types through arithmetic
are removed, and so are the sentences that \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} is a field and totally
ordered and that \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*} is not a ring; \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, which holds
\EXP{0/0}, is stated to be neither totally ordered nor a field.
\item[Rationale] Route~A makes the library's numeric types siblings under
\TYP{Number}, each with its own algebra, a wider type converting a narrower one
by coercion, and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} inside \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} by coercion.
The revival decided on 26~September 2026 that the refined types become the
library's own run-time checks, which return a \TYP{Maybe}, and that their
original design is kept as a superseded design (\secref{revival-superseded});
and that a conversion between number types is a coercion where it is exact and
stays explicit where it can lose precision, the coercion into
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} being declared from \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and the numerals only.
\Library\ never declared the refined types, and the trait that defines them is
refused by instantiation exclusion (\secref{revival-rule}).
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}} holds the values that \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}}
added, as the library's rational type does, and as \chapref{operators} already
says: ``For rational results, division by zero produces \EXP{1/0}.''
The type that holds \EXP{0/0} is ``neither totally ordered nor a field'' in the
original's own words.
The team's later Types chapter (Luchangco, 2012) keeps the statement that the
numeric types are mutually exclusive (\secref{types-libraries}).
\item[Effect] A program names \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} or \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} where it named a
refined type, and tests a sign or finiteness at run time.
A generic call over two different integer types writes its static
argument until the rule that infers the narrowest type both coerce into is
written into \chapref{type-inference} together with its implementation.
The conversion from \EXP{\mathbb{N}32} into \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, though exact,
stays explicit, since the decision names only \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and the
numerals.
The library's \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}, which this change left below
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, is made a sibling of the other number types by
\secref{revival-rr32}, and \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} coerces from it.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/numbers.tex},
lines 19--105:
\begin{quote}
The trait \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} (\STR{QQ}) encompasses all finite rational numbers,
the results of dividing any integer by any nonzero integer.
The trait \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}star}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} with
two extra elements, \EXP{+\infty} and \EXP{-\infty}.
The trait \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}splat}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} with
one additional element, the indefinite rational (written \EXP{0/0}), which is used as the result
of dividing zero by zero or of adding \EXP{-\infty} to \EXP{+\infty}.

Often it is desirable to indicate that a variable ranges over only a subset of
the rationals, such as only positive values or only nonnegative values or only
nonzero values.
Unfortunately, traditional notations such as \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^+} are not used consistently
in the literature; one author may use \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^+} to mean the set of strictly positive rationals
and another may use it to mean the set of nonnegative rationals.
Fortress therefore uses a notation that is novel but unambiguous:

\begin{tabbing}
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}} (\STR{QQ}) is the set of rationals
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{<}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}LT}) is the set of strictly negative rationals
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}_{<}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{<}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\leq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\neq}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\leq}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}LE}) is the set of nonpositive rationals, that is, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{<} \cup \{0\}}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}_{\leq}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\leq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\geq}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}GE}) is the set of nonnegative rationals, that is, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{>} \cup \{0\}}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}_{\geq}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\geq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{>}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}GT}) is the set of strictly positive rationals
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}_{>}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{>}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\geq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\neq}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\neq}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}NE}) is the set of strictly nonzero rationals (that is, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{<} \cup \mathbb{Q}_{>}})
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}_{\neq}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\neq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}star}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} with extra elements \EXP{+\infty} and \EXP{-\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^*} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{<}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}star{\char'137}LT}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{<}} with extra element \EXP{-\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^*_{<}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{<}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\leq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\neq}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\leq}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}star{\char'137}LE}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\leq}} with extra element \EXP{-\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^*_{\leq}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\leq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\geq}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}star{\char'137}GE}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\geq}} with extra element \EXP{+\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^*_{\geq}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\geq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{>}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}star{\char'137}GT}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{>}} with extra element \EXP{+\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^*_{>}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{>}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\geq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\neq}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\neq}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}star{\char'137}NE}) is
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\neq}} with extra elements \EXP{+\infty} and \EXP{-\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^*_{\neq}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\neq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}splat}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} with extra element \EXP{0/0}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^{\#}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{<}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}splat{\char'137}LT}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{<}} with extra element \EXP{0/0}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^{\#}_{<}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\leq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\neq}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\leq}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}splat{\char'137}LE}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\leq}} with extra element \EXP{0/0}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^{\#}_{\leq}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\geq}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}splat{\char'137}GE}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\geq}} with extra element \EXP{0/0}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^{\#}_{\geq}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{>}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}splat{\char'137}GT}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{>}} with extra element \EXP{0/0}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^{\#}_{>}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}},
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\geq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\neq}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\neq}} (\STR{QQ{\char'137}splat{\char'137}NE}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\neq}} with extra element \EXP{0/0}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{R}^{\#}_{\neq}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}}).
\end{tabbing}

The Fortress type system tracks these types closely through various arithmetic
operations; for example, adding two values of type \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{>}} produces a result of type \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{>}},
and adding a value of type \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{>}} and a value of type \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\geq}} produces a value
of type \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\geq}}.

Here we present only the trait \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} and its methods.
The other rational types have exactly the same methods and differ
only in the details of the types of method arguments and results
and exactly what traits are extended by each rational type.
For example, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} is a field and is totally ordered,
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} is totally ordered but is not a field,
and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}} is neither totally ordered nor a field.
For the exact details of how all this is implemented, \see{advanced-rationals}.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/basic-integers.tex}, lines 16--84:
\begin{quote}
The trait \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} (\STR{ZZ}) encompasses all finite integers,
the results of starting from \EXP{0} and repeatedly adding or subtracting \EXP{1} a finite number of times.
The trait \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}star}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} with
two extra elements, \EXP{+\infty} and \EXP{-\infty}.

Often it is desirable to indicate that a variable ranges over only a subset of
the integers, such as only positive values or only nonnegative values or only
nonzero values.
Fortress uses a system similar to that for rational numbers, but also
accommodates the traditional use of \EXP{\mathbb{N}} to denote the natural numbers:

\begin{tabbing}
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}} (\STR{ZZ}) is the set of integers
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{<}} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}LT}) is the set of strictly negative integers
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{<}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{<}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\leq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\neq}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\leq}} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}LE}) is the set of nonpositive integers, that is, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{<} \cup \{0\}}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\leq}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\leq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq}} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}GE}) is the set of nonnegative integers, that is, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{>} \cup \{0\}}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\geq}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\geq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{>}} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}GT}) is the set of strictly positive integers
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{>}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{>}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\neq}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\neq}} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}NE}) is the set of strictly nonzero integers (that is, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{<} \cup \mathbb{Z}_{>}})
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\neq}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\neq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}star}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} with extra elements \EXP{+\infty} and \EXP{-\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{<}} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}star{\char'137}LT}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{<}} with extra element \EXP{-\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{<}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\leq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\neq}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\leq}} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}star{\char'137}LE}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\leq}} with extra element \EXP{-\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\leq}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\geq}} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}star{\char'137}GE}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq}} with extra element \EXP{+\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\geq}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{>}} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}star{\char'137}GT}) is \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{>}} with extra element \EXP{+\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{>}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\geq}}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\neq}}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\neq}} (\STR{ZZ{\char'137}star{\char'137}NE}) is
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\neq}} with extra elements \EXP{+\infty} and \EXP{-\infty}
(it is a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*_{\neq}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*}).
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{N}} (\STR{NN}) is a synonym for \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq}}.
\\
\EXP{\mathbb{N}^*} (\STR{NN{\char'137}star}) is a synonym for \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{\geq}}.
\end{tabbing}

The Fortress type system tracks these types closely through various arithmetic
operations; for example, adding two values of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{>}} produces a result of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{>}},
and adding a value of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{>}} and a value of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq}} produces a value
of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*_{>}}.

Here we present only the trait \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} and its methods.
The other integer types have exactly the same methods and differ
only in the details of the types of method arguments and results
and exactly what traits are extended by each integer type.
For example, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} is a commutative ring and is totally ordered,
and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*} is totally ordered but is not a ring.
%% For the exact details of how all this is implemented,
%% \see{advanced-integers}.
\emph{Future versions of this specification will include
the exact details of how all this is implemented.}
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] By the revival's reading, not measured, route~C could keep the
nesting of the widths, each level a link in a chain of instantiations of a
self-typed trait, but not the refined types: \TYP{RationalQuantity} places
each type below a lattice of its own instantiations, not a chain, and names
them through \KWD{where}-clause variables in its \KWD{extends} clause
(\secref{revival-where}).
\end{description}

\subsection{The rational trait}
\seclabel{revival-rationals}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{basic-rationals}.
\item[Change] The listing of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} extends \TYP{Number} in place of the
real numbers, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}, three instantiations of \TYP{Field} with
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}_{\neq}} as the multiplicative group,
\TYP{TotalOrderOperators} and \TYP{PartialOrderAndLattice}, and declares the
coercions from the five integer types, with a method entry for them.
The reciprocal, division and exponentiation return \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, and so does
the absolute value; the four hyperfloor and hyperceiling operators return
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}}; the two comparisons by \OPR{CMP} become one, returning
\TYP{Comparison}.
The seventeen check methods leave the listing and the method entries.
Ten sentences of the method entries lose the words ``For types
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}},'' or ``For type
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}},'', the next word taking the capital (the sentence on
\OPR{MAXNUM} and \OPR{MINNUM} now begins ``The operators''); the parenthesis on the reciprocal's result type and
the sentence on \TYP{TotalComparison} are removed.
\item[Rationale] \secref{revival-numbers}.
A signature that named a refined type takes the type \library\ declare for the
method where they declare it (division and the absolute value return the
rational type, exponentiation by an integer returns it, and the comparison
\OPR{CMP} returns \TYP{Comparison}), and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} or \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} where
they do not.
The library declares \VAR{check} and \EXP{\VAR{check}^*} on \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, where
\secref{basic-rationals} states them, and no check by sign.
What the entries said of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}} holds of
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, which holds their values.
\item[Effect] As \secref{revival-numbers}.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/numbers.tex}:
the listing's header, lines 177--183, and its lines 198--200, 206--207, 212,
222--225 and 228--244:
\begin{quote}
\begin{Fortress}
\(\KWD{trait}\:\mathbb{Q}\)\\
{\tt~~~~}\(    \KWD{extends} \{\, \null\)\(\mathbb{R}, \mathbb{Q}^*,\)\\
{\tt~~~~~~~~~~~~~~}\(              \TYP{Field}\llbracket\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\neq},+,-,\cdot,/\rrbracket,\)\\
{\tt~~~~~~~~~~~~~~}\(              \TYP{Field}\llbracket\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\neq},+,-,\times,/\rrbracket,\)\\
{\tt~~~~~~~~~~~~~~}\(              \TYP{Field}\llbracket\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\neq},+,-,\KWD{juxtaposition},/\rrbracket,\)\\
{\tt~~~~~~~~~~~~~~}\(              \TYP{TotalOrderOperators}\llbracket\mathbb{Q},<,\leq,\geq,>,\OPR{CMP}\rrbracket,\)\\
{\tt~~~~~~~~~~~~~~}\(              \TYP{PartialOrderAndLattice}\llbracket\mathbb{Q},\leq,\OPR{MIN},\OPR{MAX}\rrbracket\,\}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} /(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^*\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} /(\KWD{self}, \VAR{other}\COLON \mathbb{Q})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^{\#}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \mathord{\hbox{\tt\char'136}}(\KWD{self}, \VAR{power}\COLON \mathbb{Z})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^{\#}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \mathord{\OPR{CMP}}(\KWD{self}, \VAR{other}\COLON \mathbb{Q}^*)\COLON \TYP{TotalComparison}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \mathord{\OPR{CMP}}(\KWD{self}, \VAR{other}\COLON \mathbb{Q}^{\#})\COLON \TYP{Comparison}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \left|\mathord{\KWD{self}}\right| \mathrel{\mathtt{:}}\:\mathbb{Q}_{\geq}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \lhfloor \mathord{\KWD{self}} \rhfloor\COLON \mathbb{N}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \lhceil \mathord{\KWD{self}} \rhceil\COLON \mathbb{N}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \lhhfloor \mathord{\KWD{self}} \rhhfloor\COLON \mathbb{N}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \lhhceil \mathord{\KWD{self}} \rhhceil\COLON \mathbb{N}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^* \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}_{<}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}_{<} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}_{\leq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}_{\leq} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}_{\geq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}_{\geq} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}_{>}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}_{>} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}_{\neq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}_{\neq} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*_{<}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^*_{<} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*_{\leq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^*_{\leq} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*_{\geq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^*_{\geq} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*_{>}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^*_{>} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*_{\neq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^*_{\neq} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^{\#}_{<}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{<} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^{\#}_{\leq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\leq} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^{\#}_{\geq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\geq} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^{\#}_{>}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{>} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^{\#}_{\neq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^{\#}_{\neq} \KWD{throws}\:\TYP{CastError}\)
\end{Fortress}
\end{quote}
The method entries at lines 327--328, 345--346, 388, 416--417, 488--493 and
546--562 repeated these signatures.
Two did not: line 339 gave division the result \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}, and line 387
gave \OPR{CMP} an argument of type \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} and the result
\TYP{TotalComparison}:
\begin{quote}
\renewcommand\Method[1]{#1\par}
\Method{\EXP{\KWD{opr} /(\KWD{self}, \VAR{other}\COLON \mathbb{Q})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^*}}
\Method{\EXP{\KWD{opr} \mathord{\OPR{CMP}}(\KWD{self}, \VAR{other}\COLON \mathbb{Q})\COLON \TYP{TotalComparison}}}
\end{quote}
In the chapter each of these two lines was the heading of its entry; here each
is set as a line.
The sentences of lines 289, 300, 320, 334, 374, 444 and 479 began
``For types \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}},'' and those of lines
410, 422 and 431 ``For type \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}},''; lines 331--332:
\begin{quote}
The reciprocal of zero is \EXP{+\infty} (and therefore the result type of \EXP{/}
when given an arguments of type \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} is necessarily \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}).
\end{quote}
lines 390--393:
\begin{quote}
The \OPR{CMP} operator compares the arguments and returns one of the four values
\TYP{LessThan}, \TYP{EqualTo}, \TYP{GreaterThan}, and \TYP{Unordered}.
If the argument types are such that the result cannot be \TYP{Unordered},
then the result has type \TYP{TotalComparison} rather than simply \TYP{Comparison}.
\end{quote}
and lines 564--566:
\begin{quote}
Each of these methods checks this rational number to see whether it belongs
to the result type of the method.  If, the number is returned; if not,
a \TYP{CastError} is thrown.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C, which rescues none of the refined types
(\secref{revival-numbers}).
\end{description}

\subsection{The integer trait}
\seclabel{revival-integers}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{basic-integers}.
\item[Change] The listing of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} extends \TYP{Number} in place of
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*}, keeping its algebra, and declares the
coercions from the four fixed-size integer types, with a method entry for them.
Division returns \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}; the exponent of the power, the results of the
factorial, of \OPR{CHOOSE} and of the four hyperfloor and hyperceiling
operators, the argument of \OPR{CMP}, the absolute value, and the result of
\VAR{lowBits} in its method entry are \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}.
The twelve check methods leave the listing and the method entries. The method entries of the three unary negations give the result \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, as the listing does.
The sentences on the infinities of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*} and \EXP{\mathbb{N}^*}
leave the method entries, and so do the words that made the strictly positive
integers a type extending a lattice trait; the lattice they describe stays.
Another revision gives the result of an integer raised to a negative power in
the method entry of the power: the bases \EXP{1} and \EXP{-1} give their exact
power, a base of \EXP{0} throws \TYP{DivisionByZero}, and any other base throws
\TYP{IntegerOverflow}, since the result is no integer; a fraction is written
with a floating-point or rational base. The box above the listing and a new box
after the method entry say so.
\item[Rationale] \secref{revival-numbers}.
\Library\ declare no infinite integer and no check on the integer types.
Division takes the type the library declares for it; the other signatures take
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, since the library declares none of those methods with a
natural-number type (its power on integers takes an exponent of any integer
type and returns the integer's own type). Route~A gives each number type its own algebra, and the listing's commutative ring over \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} negates within \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}; the method entries' \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} held of an integer only while \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} was a subtype of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}.
The revision of the negative power follows the revival's decision of
20~October 2026 that each integer type's power answers its own type at every
exponent, raising an error where the result is no integer: the type group's
later code types an integer power by its integer at a signed exponent; no type
carries the sign of an exponent or of a numeral, so that no overloading can give
\EXP{10^{16}} and \EXP{10^{-16}} different types, and the split is on the base,
with the sign tested at run time as \library\ test a sign elsewhere.
Its reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/integer-power-fable-judgement.md}, and its
measurement in \nolinkurl{explorations/perf-probes/integer-power/REPORT.md}, in
the revival's repository.
A reversal to the zero that truncation gives would change only the
interpreter's natives at a negative exponent and the sentences of the method
entry; one to a rational result, typed by the exponent as the Working Draft
typed it, would need the sign of a numeral exponent in its type, which neither
implementation gives.
\item[Effect] As \secref{revival-numbers}.
The integer types no longer hold infinities, so their total order is that of the
finite integers. Negating an integer gives an integer.
The exponent of the power, \EXP{\mathbb{N}} in the Working Draft, is now
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, so a negative power is admitted.
The Working Draft gave the result of a negative power through the
exponentiation of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, since \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} was a subtype of
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}}: \EXP{2^{-1}} was \EXP{1/2}
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/numbers.tex}, lines 344--355).
The revised text of \secref{basic-integers} gives it otherwise: \EXP{2^{-1}}
throws \TYP{IntegerOverflow}, and a program that wants \EXP{1/2} writes
\EXP{2.0^{-1}} or a rational base.
The interpreter does so at every integer type, at an exponent of any integer
type, and its integer powers answer the base's own type, as \library\ declare
them; the compiled type checker types them so.
The compiled path's own library does not: its \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} power answers
\EXP{0} at a negative exponent, and a Java exception that no \KWD{catch} sees
at a base of \EXP{0} (rows~441 and~523 of the revival's gap ledger).
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/basic-integers.tex}:
the listing's lines 196, 239, 246, 249--250, 256, 261, 271--274 and 290--301:
\begin{quote}
\begin{Fortress}
{\tt~~~~}\(    \KWD{extends} \{\, \null\)\(\mathbb{Q}, \mathbb{Z}^*, \TYP{IntegerLike}\llbracket\mathbb{Z}\rrbracket,\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} /(\KWD{self}, \VAR{other}\COLON \mathbb{Z})\COLON \mathbb{Q}^{\#}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \mathord{\hbox{\tt\char'137}}(\KWD{self}, \VAR{power}\COLON \mathbb{N})\COLON \mathbb{Z}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} (\KWD{self})! \mathrel{\mathtt{:}}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\mathbb{N}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \mathord{\OPR{CHOOSE}}(\KWD{self}, \VAR{other}\COLON \mathbb{Z})\COLON \mathbb{N}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \mathord{\OPR{CMP}}(\KWD{self}, \VAR{other}\COLON \mathbb{Z}^*)\COLON \TYP{TotalComparison}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \left|\mathord{\KWD{self}}\right| \mathrel{\mathtt{:}}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\mathbb{Z}_{\geq}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \lhfloor \mathord{\KWD{self}} \rhfloor\COLON \mathbb{N}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \lhceil \mathord{\KWD{self}} \rhceil\COLON \mathbb{N}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \lhhfloor \mathord{\KWD{self}} \rhhfloor\COLON \mathbb{N}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{opr} \lhhceil \mathord{\KWD{self}} \rhhceil\COLON \mathbb{N}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}^* \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}_{<}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}_{<} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}_{\leq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}_{\leq} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}_{\geq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}_{\geq} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}_{>}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}_{>} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}_{\neq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}_{\neq} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*_{<}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}^*_{<} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*_{\leq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}^*_{\leq} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*_{\geq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}^*_{\geq} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*_{>}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}^*_{>} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)\\
{\tt~~}\(  \VAR{check}^*_{\neq}(\KWD{self})\COLON \mathbb{Z}^*_{\neq} \KWD{throws}\mskip 4mu plus 4mu\TYP{CastError}\)

The method entries at lines 425, 507, 551, 600, 669--672 and 888--899 repeated
these signatures, line 507 setting the power operator as a caret where the
listing's line 246 set an underscore.
Two did not: line 622 gave the absolute value the result \EXP{\mathbb{N}}, where
the listing had \EXP{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq}}, of which \EXP{\mathbb{N}} was a synonym;
and line 816 gave \VAR{lowBits} the result \EXP{\mathbb{N}}, where the listing's
line 284, which the revision leaves as it was, already said \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}:

In the chapter each of these two lines was the heading of its entry; here each

Lines 380--382 gave the three unary negations the result \EXP{\mathbb{Q}},

Lines 372--374, 387--388 and 418--421:
\begin{quote}
For type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*}, the sum of an infinity
and either a finite integer or another infinity of the same sign is equal to the given infinity,
but attempting to sum infinities of differing sign throws an \TYP{IndeterminateInteger}.

For types \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^{\#}}, the negative of
\EXP{+\infty} is \EXP{-\infty}, and the negative of \EXP{-\infty} is \EXP{+\infty}.

For types \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^*} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}^{\#}}, attempting to multiply
zero and an infinity (regardless of sign) throws an \TYP{IndeterminateInteger}.
The product of an infinity and any nonzero integer (including an infinity)
is an infinity whose sign is positive if and only if the two arguments have the same sign.
\end{quote}

\subsection{The superseded chapter on rational quantities}
\seclabel{revival-superseded}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{advanced-rationals}; the box after
``Conditional Subtyping'' in the appendix ``Internal Document''.
\item[Change] One box at the head of the chapter says that it is a superseded
design, why, and where the way back is. No word of the chapter changes.
The box in the internal document (\secref{revival-internal}), which said
that the number chapters were not yet revised, now points here.
\item[Rationale] The revival decided on 26~September 2026 to keep the chapter
word for word as the record of the original design.
Instantiation exclusion refuses \TYP{RationalQuantity}, which extends its own
instantiations.
The compiled type checker refuses the team's own test of the declaration with
``Cyclic type hierarchy: Type RationalQuantity transitively extends itself''; the
interpreter runs that test only because nothing instantiates the trait, and an
instantiation fails there, on the trait's \KWD{where} clause or, without it, with
``Missing type'' for a flag of the conditional \KWD{extends} clause.
The team's later Types chapter comments out the section on type aliases by which
the chapter names the eighteen types.
The way back is declaration-site variance, the later chapter's
\KWD{covariant} static parameters, with each flag a covariant type argument: the
compiled type checker accepts that form, and neither implementation carries
covariance to run time yet (row~404 of the revival's gap ledger; item~12 of its
worklist).
\item[Effect] None on the language or on either implementation.
\item[Original text] Unchanged.
\item[Route C] By the revival's reading, not measured, the same under route~C
(\secref{revival-numbers}).
\end{description}

\subsection{Rational literals}
\seclabel{revival-literals}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{literals}.
\item[Change] \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}} become one
type that includes \EXP{1/0}, \EXP{-1/0} and \EXP{0/0}; the run-time error on
assigning a \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} to a \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, the two ASCII names and the
reference to \secref{advanced-rationals} are removed, and the team's margin note
is kept.
\item[Rationale] \secref{revival-numbers}.
\item[Effect] As \secref{revival-numbers}.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/literals.tex},
lines 188--209:
\begin{quote}
The type $\mathbb{Q}$ includes all such rational numbers.

The type $\mathbb{Q}^*$ relaxes the requirement $q>0$ to $q \geq 0$ and
includes two extra values, \EXP{1/0} and \EXP{-1/0} (sometimes called ``the
infinite rational'' and ``the indefinite rational'').
[The margin note of lines 193--197, kept unchanged in the revised text.]
If a value of type $\mathbb{Q}^*$ is assigned to a
variable of type $\mathbb{Q}$, a \TYP{DivisionByZero} is thrown
at run time if the value is \EXP{1/0} or \EXP{-1/0}.
The type \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}} includes all of \EXP{1/0}, \EXP{-1/0}, and
\EXP{0/0}.
The advantage of $\mathbb{Q}^{\#}$ is that it is closed under the rational
operations $+$, $-$, $\times$, and $/$.
In ASCII, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*}, and
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^\#} are written as \txt{QQ}, \txt{QQ\_star}, and
\txt{QQ\_splat}, respectively.
See \secref{advanced-rationals} for definitions of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*},
and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^\#}.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{Integers in floating-point expressions}
\seclabel{revival-int-float}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{coercion}.
\item[Change] The automatic conversion of integer values to floating-point values
becomes that of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} values and of integer numerals to
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64}; any other integer type converts to \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}
explicitly.
\item[Rationale] \secref{revival-numbers}: an exact conversion is a coercion and one
that can lose precision stays explicit, and the decision names \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}
and the numerals.
\item[Effect] The paragraph's examples convert numerals and are unchanged, and
Example~1 of \secref{coercion}, that a decimal integer literal argument may be
used for any floating-point parameter, stands as written.
A program converts a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}64} or
\EXP{\mathbb{N}32} value to \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} explicitly.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/conversions-coercions.tex},
lines 64--66:
\begin{quote}
type is not a subtype of any floating-point type.  Fortress supports
the automatic conversion of integer values to floating-point values (the
technical term for this kind of automatic conversion is coercion).  In
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C, which keeps the coercions.
\end{description}

\subsection{Reductions whose element type nothing fixes}
\seclabel{revival-reductions}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{reduction-expr}.
\item[Change] One box after the first sentence says that neither implementation
directs the desugaring of a reduction by type yet, so a reduction with a
generator clause list whose element type no argument fixes writes its static
argument; and that until the compiled path is built on the libraries this
specification describes, it compiles against a smaller library of its own,
whose big operators take no static argument and range over \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}
only, so that there the clause form runs without its static argument and a
written one is refused. The normative text is unchanged.
The box said at first that ``the interpreter, and the compiled type checker
when it checks the libraries this specification describes, each take
\TYP{BottomType} for it, and the reduction then accepts no element''; it now
says that the interpreter does, and that the compiled type checker takes the
bound of the big operator's static parameter, which gives the reduction the
type of a bound such as \TYP{Number} and no instance that applies where the
bound mentions the static parameter itself.
A revision of the interpreter of 9~October 2026 changes the box once more.
It said that ``When no argument of the big operator fixes the element type, the
interpreter takes \TYP{BottomType} for it, and the reduction then accepts no
element''; it now says that where the bound of the big operator's static
parameter mentions the static parameter itself the interpreter leaves that
parameter open and the reduction runs on the types of its elements, an empty
one taking the identity of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and that until the compiled
type checker directs the desugaring by type a program that both implementations
are to run writes the static argument.
A revision of the interpreter of 14~October 2026 changes the box once more.
It said that ``Neither implementation directs it by type yet'', and that where
the bound of the big operator's static parameter mentions the static parameter
itself ``the interpreter leaves that parameter open'', the reduction running on
the types of its elements ``except that an empty one takes the identity of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} whatever the type of the expression''; it now says that
neither implementation directs it by the type \VAR{N} of the expression yet,
that the interpreter, which has no static types, instantiates the big operator
by the declared type of the local variable, or the declared return type of the
top-level or local function, whose value the reduction is, otherwise by the
join of the run-time types of its elements, and where
there is none at the bound of its static parameter, or open where that bound
mentions the parameter itself, and that an empty reduction that no declared
type directs takes the identity of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}.
\item[Rationale] The revival decided on 26~September 2026 that the library's
\OPR{SUM} and \OPR{PROD} become generic reductions over the library's algebra,
each joined with its element type's own operator and taking that type's
identity, and that such a clause form writes its static argument, as the team's
own tests do.
Until 9~October 2026 the interpreter took \TYP{BottomType} for the static
parameter of a big operator that no argument fixes (row~424 of the revival's
gap ledger).
The revival decided on 8~October 2026 that the interpreter leaves open a type
parameter whose bound mentions the type parameter itself and that nothing at a
call fixes (\secref{revival-inference}): having no static types, the
interpreter cannot take the type \VAR{N} of the expression, and an open
parameter refuses no element where \TYP{BottomType} refused every one, so that
the reduction runs on the types of its elements, as the desugaring by type
would run it.
Reversing that revision takes one block out of the interpreter's
instantiation of a call and returns such a parameter to \TYP{BottomType}; the
specification's examples that write no static argument for such a reduction
would stop again at their first element, and refusing the call, as the compiled
type checker does, would stop them too.
The revival decided on 14~October 2026 that the interpreter instantiates the
big operator of a reduction or a comprehension that writes no static argument
as the desugaring by the type \VAR{N} would, with run-time types in place of
\VAR{N}: by the declared type of the local variable, or the declared return
type of the top-level or local function, whose value the reduction is, which
constrains the instance as a call's static return type
does (\secref{static-arg-inference}), and otherwise by the join of the
run-time types of its elements, which the interpreter collects before it
reduces them, as it already took the types of the elements for \VAR{N} where
it left the parameter open; with neither, the parameter takes its bound, or is
left open where the bound mentions it.
Until then the interpreter left a big operator's static parameter whose bound
does not mention it at \TYP{BottomType} (\secref{revival-inference}), and an
empty reduction, having no element, took the identity of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}.
Reversing the revision of 14~October 2026 takes the declared type and the
collection of the elements out of the interpreter's evaluation of such a
reduction and returns those static parameters to \TYP{BottomType}: an empty
reduction of \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} elements then takes the identity of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} again, the library's set reductions \EXP{\bigcup} and
\EXP{\bigcap} stop again at their first element, a comprehension is built
again at the run-time types of its elements wherever its type is declared, and
the library's \VAR{body} in the application of a big operator can no longer
declare the type of its parameter.
The reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/perf-probes/walk-plain-bound/REPORT.md} and
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-walk-elements/REPORT.md} in the
revival's repository.
The compiled type checker, when it checks the libraries this specification
describes, took \TYP{BottomType} too until it was revised to the rule of
\secref{static-arg-inference} for a type parameter that nothing at a call
fixes; it now takes the parameter's bound, which is not the element type, or,
since 14~October 2026, where the reduction's type at that bound does not fit the
reduction's expected type, that expected type, the big operator being a call
written as an argument of another call (\secref{revival-expected-contexts})
(row~425).
The compiled path's own library declares $\sum$ and
\EXP{\OPR{BIG}\:\OPR{MAX}} for \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} alone, with no static
parameter (\nolinkurl{Library/CompilerLibrary.fsi}, lines 180--184), and
refuses \EXP{\sum\llbracket\mathbb{Z}32\rrbracket [j \gets 0\#4]\: j}
(\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-numbers/probes/skeptic/SkSumClauseTyped.t1.txt}).
\item[Effect] None beyond the box: the forms without a generator clause list need
no static argument.
Since 9~October 2026, under the interpreter, a reduction with a generator clause
list whose element type nothing fixes, over a big operator whose bound mentions
its static parameter, runs where it stopped at its first element, the
specification's examples of that form among them.
Since 14~October 2026, under the interpreter, such a reduction or comprehension
is instantiated by the declared type of its value, by the join of its elements'
run-time types or at its bound: an empty sum that is the value of a function
declared \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} gives the \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} zero, where it gave
the identity of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} (row~645 of the revival's gap ledger), the
library's set reductions \EXP{\bigcup} and \EXP{\bigcap} run where they
stopped at their first element (row~662), and a comprehension that is the
value of a local variable or of a top-level or local function declared with its
type is built at that type.
An empty reduction that no declared type directs still gives the identity of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} whatever the type of the expression, and an empty
comprehension is built at its big operator's bound (row~716); a
comprehension that no declared type reaches, such as the value of a top-level
variable, a field or a method, or the body of another reduction, is built at
the run-time types of its elements (rows~20 and~717). Since 16~October 2026 a
reduction over a big operator whose declared result is a trait other than the
library's \TYP{BigReduction}, \TYP{Comprehension} and \TYP{BigOperator} is
instantiated through the one of those three that the trait extends, its static
arguments put for the trait's, as one declared with that supertype is (row~718).
The compiled type checker still refuses that form (row~425).
\item[Original text] Unchanged.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{Initializing an array from a function}
\seclabel{revival-tabulate}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{arrays}.
\item[Change] In \figref{arrayFactories}, the method that calls a function at
each index and initializes the corresponding element with the result is named
\VAR{tabulate}; \VAR{fill} keeps the method that initializes every element with
one value.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided
it on 26~September 2026.
The library declares the element type \VAR{E} of an array without a bound and
instantiates it at tuple types, which the implicit bound \TYP{Object} of
\secref{type-param} excludes; the interpreter reads such a parameter as bounded
by \TYP{Any}, and so does the compiled type checker when it checks the library.
\VAR{E} may then be \TYP{Any} or a function type, and a function passed to
\VAR{fill} is a value of type \VAR{E} as well as a function from \VAR{I} to
\VAR{E}: both declarations apply to the call and neither is more specific, which
the overloading rules forbid (\secref{incompatibility-rule}).
The compiled type checker refuses the pair; the interpreter chose between the two
by the argument's run-time type, so that on an array of element type \TYP{Any} a
function meant as the value was called at each index instead of being stored.
Where the library derived a second name, it gave it to a form that takes a
function (\VAR{ivmap} beside \VAR{map}); most languages that have both operations
name them differently, keeping \VAR{fill} for the value; and \VAR{tabulate} is
Scala's name for the other.
\item[Effect] A program initializes an array from a function with
\VAR{tabulate}. A call of \VAR{fill} with a function stores the function, on an
array whose element type admits it. The library's factories that build an array
from a function are renamed with the method; this specification does not list
them.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/advanced/parallelism-locality/arrays-distributed.tex},
line 55, a row of the lower table of the figure:
\begin{quote}
\begin{tabular}{|m{2.0in}|m{3.4in}|}
\hline
\EXP{\VAR{a}.\VAR{fill}(f\COLONOP{}I\rightarrow{}E)} & {Calls \VAR{f} at each index and initializes the corresponding element with the result of the call.} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The integer type of a range}
\seclabel{revival-ranges}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{ranges}; \secref{basic-integers};
\secref{revival-numbers}; \secref{revival-notrevised}.
\item[Change] The components of an explicit range are values of type
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, into which an integer numeral converts, and it is a static
error if a component has another integer type. A paragraph shows how a loop
whose counter must have a wider integer type is written: over a range of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} values, widening each element.
The box in \secref{basic-integers}, the effect of \secref{revival-numbers} and
\secref{revival-notrevised} no longer say that a range over two different
integer types infers the narrowest type that both coerce into; they keep that
rule for a generic call.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided
it on 27~September 2026.
The Working Draft named no integer type for a range, and \library\ declared
their ranges generic over an integer type named by a type parameter. The
compiled type checker refused that code wherever it converted a numeral to the
range's integer type, since a numeral does not convert to a type named by a
type parameter (\secref{using-coercion}).
The compiled path's own smaller library declares its ranges over
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} alone (\nolinkurl{Library/CompilerLibrary.fsi}, lines
173--174), and on the Java Virtual Machine, which runs Fortress, an array holds
at most $2^{31}-1$ elements, so a range of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} values indexes any
of them.
With every range over \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, no range is over two different
integer types, and the rule that infers the narrowest type that both coerce
into concerns a generic call alone.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-ranges/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] A program writes a range over \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} where it wrote
one over \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}64} or
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, and widens its elements where it needs a wider counter; a
range over another integer type is refused. Whether a \KWD{nat} parameter may
be a component of a range, which this change left open, is settled by
\secref{revival-inference}: it converts to \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} as an integer
numeral does.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/ranges.tex},
lines 42--44:
\begin{quote}
An \emph{explicit range}
is self-contained and completely describes a set of integers.  Assume that
$a$, $b$, and $c$ are expressions that produce integer values.
\end{quote}
The box in \secref{basic-integers} and two sentences of this appendix are the
revival's own text of 27~September 2026, not the Working Draft's.
The box said, in \nolinkurl{Specification/basic-lib/basic-integers.tex}, lines
61--65, before this revision:
\begin{quote}
A generic call or a range over two different integer types is to infer the
narrowest type that both coerce into, by a rule to be written into
\chapref{type-inference} together with its implementation; until then such a
call writes its static argument, as in
\EXP{f\llbracket\mathbb{Z}64\rrbracket(a, b)}.
\end{quote}
The effect of \secref{revival-numbers} said:
\begin{quote}
A generic call or a range over two different integer types writes its static
argument until the rule that infers the narrowest type both coerce into is
written into \chapref{type-inference} together with its implementation.
\end{quote}
and \secref{revival-notrevised} said:
\begin{quote}
The rule by which a generic call or a range over two different integer types
infers the narrowest type that both coerce into is to be written into
\chapref{type-inference} together with its implementation
(\secref{revival-numbers}).
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{Examples and a property with ranges over wider integer types}
\seclabel{revival-range-examples}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{block-expr}; \secref{defining-generators},
\figref{generatorDefn} and the generator adapted to a commutative monoid;
\secref{basic-integers}.
\item[Change] The function \VAR{mySum} of \secref{block-expr} takes a parameter
of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and keeps its sum of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}.
The generator of \figref{generatorDefn}, and the generator of
\secref{defining-generators} that adapts it to a commutative monoid, are
declared over \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and the figure's midpoint is
\EXP{\VAR{lo} + (\VAR{hi} - \VAR{lo}) \div 2}.
The factorial property of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} is stated by the value at \EXP{0} and
a recurrence, without a range.
