\item[Rationale] Each built a range over an integer type other than
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} (\secref{revival-ranges}): the loop of \VAR{mySum} over
\EXP{0\COLONOP{}i} with \VAR{i} of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, the method
\VAR{seq} of the figure's generator over \EXP{\VAR{lo}\COLONOP\VAR{hi}} with
bounds of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, and the property's product over
\EXP{1\COLONOP{}m} with \VAR{m} of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}. The adapted generator
builds no range in its own text, but its ellipsis stands for the figure's
members.
The figure's midpoint \EXP{\left\lfloor (\VAR{lo}+\VAR{hi})/2 \right\rfloor} is a
value of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} (\secref{basic-rationals}), which converts to
neither \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} nor \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} (\secref{basic-integers}), so
the recursive construction of the generator did not accept it; the new
midpoint is a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} value equal to it whenever \EXP{\VAR{lo} \leq \VAR{hi}}
and the generator's size \EXP{\VAR{hi}-\VAR{lo}+1} is a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} value, and
it does not form the sum \EXP{\VAR{lo}+\VAR{hi}}, which exceeds \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}
when both bounds are large.
The property of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} holds for every natural \VAR{m}; over a range
of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} values it would hold only below $2^{31}$ and need two
conversions, so it is stated by a recurrence, as \secref{basic-integers} states
the binomial coefficient by Pascal's rule.
\item[Effect] None beyond the examples and the property.
\item[Original text] The two examples are typeset from the files
\nolinkurl{SpecData/examples/basic/Expr.Do.mySum.fss} and
\nolinkurl{SpecData/examples/advanced/Generators.GeneratorDefn.fss} of the
revival's repository, identical in the commit of 2~February 2011 of which
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/} is a copy and unchanged until this
revision;
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/blocks.tex}, line 72,
and \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/advanced/parallelism-locality/defining-generators.tex},
line 50, input them. As typeset from those files:
\begin{quote}
\begin{FortressCode}
\VAR{mySum}(i\COLON\mathbb{Z}64)\COLON\mathbb{Z}64 =\; \KWD{do} \\
\2\+\VAR{acc}\COLON\mathbb{Z}64 \ASSIGN 0 \\
  \KWD{for} j \leftarrow 0\COLONOP{}i \KWD{do} \\
  \2\+\VAR{acc} \ASSIGN \VAR{acc} + j\- \\
  \KWD{end} \\
  \VAR{acc}\- \\
\KWD{end}
\end{FortressCode}
\end{quote}
and
\begin{quote}
\begin{FortressCode}
\KWD{object} \TYP{BlockedRange}(\VAR{lo}\COLON \mathbb{Z}64, \VAR{hi}\COLON \mathbb{Z}64, b\COLON \mathbb{Z}64) \KWD{extends} \TYP{Generator}\llbracket\mathbb{Z}64\rrbracket \\
\2\+\VAR{size} \COLONOP \mathbb{Z}64 = \VAR{hi} - \VAR{lo} + 1 \\
  \VAR{seq}(\KWDVAR{self})\COLON \TYP{SequentialGenerator}\llbracket\mathbb{Z}64\rrbracket = \VAR{seq}(\VAR{lo}\COLONOP\VAR{hi}) \\
  \VAR{generate}\llbracket{}R\rrbracket\bigl(\VAR{reduction}\COLON \TYP{Reduction}\llbracket{}R\rrbracket, \VAR{body}\COLON \mathbb{Z}64\rightarrow{}R\bigr)\COLON R = \\
  \2\+\KWD{if} \VAR{size} \leq (b \OPR{MAX} 1) \KWD{then} \\
    \2\+\fortressonelinecomment{(*}{ Blocks smaller than b run sequentially }{*)} \\
      r \COLONOP R \ASSIGN \VAR{reduction}.\VAR{empty}(\ultrathin) \\
      i \COLONOP \mathbb{Z}64 \ASSIGN \VAR{lo} \\
      \KWD{while} i \leq \VAR{hi} \KWD{do} \\
      \2\+v \COLONOP R = \VAR{body}(i) \\
        r \ASSIGN \VAR{reduction}.\VAR{join}(r,v) \\
        i \mathrel{\mathrel{+}=} 1\- \\
      \KWD{end} \\
      r\- \\
    \KWD{else} \\
    \2\+\fortressonelinecomment{(*}{ Blocks larger than b are split in half and generated in parallel. }{*)} \\
      \VAR{mid} = \left\lfloor\,{\textstyle\VAR{lo}+\VAR{hi}\over\textstyle2}\,\right\rfloor \\
      \VAR{reduction}.\VAR{join}(\+\TYP{BlockedRange}(\VAR{lo},\VAR{mid},b).\VAR{generate}\llbracket{}R\rrbracket(\VAR{reduction},\VAR{body}), \\
                     \TYP{BlockedRange}(\VAR{mid}+1,\VAR{hi},b).\VAR{generate}\llbracket{}R\rrbracket(\VAR{reduction},\VAR{body}))\-\- \\
    \KWD{end}\-\- \\
\KWD{end} \\
 \\
f(\ultrathin) = \bigl\langle\,2\, x \bigm| x \leftarrow \TYP{BlockedRange}(1,10,3)\,\bigr\rangle
\end{FortressCode}
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/advanced/parallelism-locality/defining-generators.tex},
lines 405, 407 and 411, the three lines of the adapted generator that named
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, quoted without their indentation and alignment commands:
\begin{quote}
\begin{Fortress}
\( \KWD{value}\;\;\KWD{object} \TYP{BlockedRange}(\VAR{lo}\COLON \mathbb{Z}64, \VAR{hi}\COLON \mathbb{Z}64, b\COLON \mathbb{Z}64) \KWD{extends} \TYP{Generator}\llbracket\mathbb{Z}64\rrbracket\)\\
\(   \VAR{generate}\llbracket{}R \KWD{extends} \TYP{CommutativeMonoid}\llbracket\,R, \oplus\rrbracket, \KWD{opr} \mathord{\oplus}\rrbracket(\VAR{body} \mathrel{\mathtt{:}} \mathbb{Z}64 \rightarrow R) \mathrel{\mathtt{:}} R = \;\KWD{do}\)\\
\(         i \mathrel{\mathtt{:}} \mathbb{Z}64 = l\)\\
\end{Fortress}
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/basic-integers.tex}, lines 559--561:
\begin{quote}
\begin{Fortress}
{\tt~~}\pushtabs\=\+\(  \KWD{property} \forall (m)\; m! = \prod\limits_{k\leftarrow{}1\COLONOP{}m} k\)\-\\\poptabs
\end{Fortress}
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The traits that extend a closed trait}
\seclabel{revival-comprises}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{trait-decls}; \secref{natparams}, by the
amendment of 9~October 2026.
\item[Change] The first sentence of a draft note in \secref{trait-decls},
which said that the traits listed in a \KWD{comprises} clause are exactly
the traits that immediately extend the declared trait and that they must
explicitly extend it, is replaced by normative text: every listed type
extends the declared trait \VAR{T}, and every value of type \VAR{T} is a
value of one of the listed types; a listed type may be an instantiation of
a parameterized trait whose static arguments contain no static variable
other than the static parameters of \VAR{T}; and a trait or object that explicitly extends
\VAR{T} is a subtype of a listed type, or a trait with a \KWD{comprises}
clause of its own each of whose listed types meets the same requirement, or
a trait with static parameters that at least one trait or object extends
and every trait or object extending it is a subtype of a listed type.
The note's second sentence, on a clause that includes ``\EXP{\ldots}'', is
kept in its note, and the example of \TYP{Molecule} is unchanged.
The new text answers the third question of Victor Luchangco's draft note
on trait identifiers earlier in the section, whether an instantiation of a
parameterized trait may be listed: it may.
An amendment of 9~October 2026 lets the static arguments of a listed type
contain a \KWD{where}-clause variable of the declaration of \VAR{T} as well,
the listed type then holding at every value of the variable, and adds one
sentence to \secref{natparams}: an argument whose type is a trait whose clause
lists a type at a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} \KWD{where}-clause variable, passed
where that listed type is expected with a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} parameter in
the variable's place, instantiates that parameter, for that call, with the
size of the value itself, a size known to equal only itself.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival
decided it on 28~September 2026.
The team's later Types chapter (Luchangco, 2012) reads a \KWD{comprises}
clause as coverage: the clause ``specifies a set of types and generic types
determined by the generic type of the declaration'', and an instantiation of
the declared generic type ``is covered by the union of the types in this set
and the corresponding instantiations of the generic type in this set'', one
generic type $G$ determining another $G'$ ``if every parameter of $G'$ is a
parameter of $G$''
(\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/types.tick}, lines
277--283 and 384--390, in the revival's repository).
Welterweight Fortress, the calculus of the type group's paper of 2012
(Chase, Hilburn, Luchangco, Naden, Ryu, Steele, and Tristan, ``Dynamic
Dispatch and Type Inference Semipredicates''), reads it at the level of
values: ``no value can belong to the trait unless it also belongs to one of
the comprised types''; its rule for a trait declaration asks nothing of the
traits that extend a trait with such a clause
(\nolinkurl{Papers/Welterweight/grammar.tick}, lines 21--22;
\nolinkurl{Papers/Welterweight/fig-wellformeddecls.tick}, lines 38--62).
The draft note restated the rule of the compiled type checker of 2009,
under which \library\ could not close their marker of the integer types as
the team meant to: \TYP{AnyIntegral} lists the five integer types, and the
parameterized trait \EXP{\TYP{Integral}\llbracket{}I\rrbracket}, which each
of them extends at itself, stands between them unlisted, as the team's
comment on the declaration records (``not yet: comprises
\EXP{\TYP{Integral}\llbracket{}I\rrbracket} where
\EXP{\llbracket{}I\rrbracket}'', \nolinkurl{Library/FortressLibrary.fsi},
line 434).
\TYP{Integral} has a static parameter that \TYP{AnyIntegral} does not have,
so it can be listed only at particular static arguments, and every value of it is a value of one of the five.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-comprises/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
The amendment follows the revival's default for the third question of its
array design, a size known only at run time, built on 9~October 2026 and
listed for the curator's review.
\library\ have carried on their trait \TYP{NatParam}, since 2007, the clause
``\EXP{\KWD{comprises}\;\{N\llbracket{}n\rrbracket\}\;\KWD{where}\;\llbracket\KWD{nat}\;n\rrbracket}''
as a comment, with the reading that a function taking an
\EXP{N\llbracket{}n\rrbracket} may be passed a \TYP{NatParam}, and ``within
that function n becomes a static nat parameter''
(\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/LibraryBuiltin/NatReflect.fss}, lines 15--26, in
the revival's repository).
The sentence of 28~September followed the Types chapter's rule by which one
generic type determines another, which does not consider \KWD{where}-clause
variables; \secref{where-clauses} allows a declaration to introduce them.
A size so opened is bound for the call, as inference binds a type parameter
to an argument's type, and since no program can write its name it cannot be
equal to a size written elsewhere.
The team wrote the same form at a type \KWD{where}-clause variable as a comment
on \TYP{AnyList} (``Not yet: comprises
\EXP{\TYP{List}\llbracket{}E\rrbracket} where \EXP{\llbracket{}E\rrbracket}'',
\nolinkurl{Library/List.fsi}, line 56, in the revival's repository); the
revival decided on 16~October 2026 to build the forms of \KWD{where} clause the
team wrote down, that comment among them.
The full reasoning of the amendment is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-size-expressions/REPORT.md} and
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/array-fork3-judgement.md} in the revival's
repository.
\item[Effect] The compiled type checker accepts the declaration of
\TYP{AnyIntegral} in \library\ as written
(\nolinkurl{Library/FortressLibrary.fsi}, lines 433--436).
No example of this specification changes: \TYP{ExclusiveMolecule}, which
has no static parameters, is still not allowed.
The compiled type checker enforces the requirement on a trait with static
parameters over the declarations and object expressions it sees: a trait or object declared in
another component, below such a trait and a subtype of no listed type, is
not refused (row~487 of the revival's gap ledger). It compares the declarations it sees by their names: for a parameterized \VAR{T} its verdict can depend on how an extender spells its static parameters, and a trait or object that extends a trait of the same simple name in another API counts as an extender (rows~489 and~490).
It refuses an object expression that extends a trait with a \KWD{comprises}
clause, explicitly or through the traits it extends, and is a subtype of no
listed type, as the requirement on the trait's extenders together demands of
it, an object expression having neither static parameters nor a clause of its
own; before 3~October 2026 it read no object expression against a clause
(row~597).
The interpreter checks at load the traits and objects that explicitly extend a
trait with a \KWD{comprises} clause declared in the component it executes, and
refuses one that is neither a subtype of a listed type nor another of the
extenders that \secref{trait-decls} allows.
It does not yet check a clause declared in another component, of the program
or of \library\ (rows~22, 487, 551, 595 and~596), an object expression that
extends such a trait (row~597), or that each listed type extends \VAR{T}
(row~598).
By the amendment, the compiled type checker reads a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int}
\KWD{where}-clause variable as a size, reads a listed type at every value of
such a variable when it checks the traits and objects that extend \VAR{T}, and
opens an argument as the new sentence of \secref{natparams} says, so that the
value of \VAR{reflect} in \library\ is accepted where an
\EXP{N\llbracket{}n\rrbracket} is expected; it refuses an opened size that
reaches a type naming another size, and gives two values opened in one call
two sizes, so it refuses a call that needs them equal where the interpreter
runs it when they are.
It reads a declaration whose parameter type is such a trait and a declaration
with a \KWD{nat} parameter whose parameter type is the listed type at it as
applicable to the same calls, and refuses the pair as duplicates, where the
interpreter runs it and calls the second; the text does not say whether the
opening counts for the rules of \secref{overloading-static-params}
(row~682 of the revival's gap ledger).
Since 17~October 2026 it opens a type \KWD{where}-clause variable in the same
way, as in the clause of \TYP{AnyList} in \library\ (row~636): an argument whose
type is such a trait, passed where the listed type is expected, takes the listed
type at a type of the value's own for that call, bounded as the variable's
binding bounds it and by each trait constraint of the clause on the variable
(\secref{where-clauses}), and known to equal only itself; before 18~October 2026
it did not bound that type by such a constraint, and refused a call that needs
the constraint's bound, a call the interpreter runs (row~771); it refuses an
opened type that
reaches a type naming another type, gives two values opened in one call two
types, and reads a listed type at the types below the variable's bounds, its
binding's and its constraints', when it checks the traits and objects that
extend \VAR{T}, so that it refuses an extender that the clause lists only below
a bound its static argument does not meet; before 18~October 2026 it read a
listed type at every type of the variable, whatever its bound, and took such an
extender (row~753).
It does not open a trait whose clause lists a type at a type
\KWD{where}-clause variable where it reaches that trait as a supertype of
another type, so that a value of a trait that extends it, and is not itself
listed, is not taken where a listed type is expected (row~752); it opens a
trait whose clause lists a type at a size there, as before.
It opens such a trait, of either kind, also where it is the result type of a
function type, once for all the function's results, so it takes a function
whose results are values of the trait where a function whose results share
one listed type is expected, a call the interpreter refuses (row~751).
The code generator compiles such a trait, but does not yet read a size off an
argument at a call: a compiled call that opens a value of the trait to its
size fails the JVM's verification (row~307).
The interpreter binds a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} \KWD{where}-clause variable as a
size, and a type \KWD{where}-clause variable as a type that stands for every
type, when it loads the trait, reads a listed type at every value of either in
its check at load, a type whatever its bound (row~754), and binds the size
of an argument when it dispatches.
It binds the type \KWD{where}-clause variable of a trait without static
parameters in its component's environment, so that a static parameter of
another declaration that has the variable's name and whose bound names it
is read as the variable, and a call of that declaration fails (row~778).
It refuses at load such a trait one of whose members has a declared type that
names the variable, of either kind (row~755); the compiled type checker
accepts that declaration, and stops with an internal error at a call, outside
the trait, of such a member whose type names a type \KWD{where}-clause variable
(row~756).
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/traits.tex},
lines 230--241, a draft note:
\begin{quote}
If a trait declaration of \VAR{T} includes a \KWD{comprises} clause
then the traits listed in its \KWD{comprises} clause
are exactly the traits that immediately extend \VAR{T}
and they must explicitly extend \VAR{T}
(i.e., list \VAR{T} in their \KWD{extends} clause).
If the \KWD{comprises} clause of a trait \VAR{T} includes ``\EXP{\ldots}''
(and must therefore be in an API),
then in a component exporting the enclosing API,
other traits may explicitly extend \VAR{T},
but these traits may not be declared or imported by the API
(see \secref{apis}).
\end{quote}
Its second sentence is kept, in its note.
The passage of \secref{natparams} that the amendment adds to is unchanged:
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/trait-parameters.tex}, lines 76--90.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C, under which the self-typed traits
carry \KWD{comprises} clauses of their own (\secref{revival-routec}): a
parameterized trait between a closed trait and its listed types then meets
the requirement through its own clause, and the last case of the
requirement is not needed.
The amendment is the same under route~C.
A return to the text before it would put \TYP{NatParam}'s clause back into a
comment, as \library\ had it, and leave the compiled type checker refusing
each call that passes the value of \VAR{reflect} where an
\EXP{N\llbracket{}n\rrbracket} is expected (row~664).
Not opening a type \KWD{where}-clause variable would put \TYP{AnyList}'s
clause back into a comment, as \library\ had it, and leave the compiled type
checker refusing the call in \TYP{AnyList}'s concatenation operator that reads
its two arguments as lists (row~636).
\end{description}

\subsection{Shifts and narrowing on the integer types}
\seclabel{revival-shifts}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{basic-integers}; the overview of operators in
\chapref{operators}.
\item[Change] Two paragraphs after the coercions of \secref{basic-integers} state
the method \VAR{narrow} and the operators \OPR{LSHIFT} and \OPR{RSHIFT}, which this
working draft did not name: \VAR{narrow} keeps the low 32 bits of a value of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} or \EXP{\mathbb{N}64}, and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} has no \VAR{narrow}
(\secref{revival-int-conversions}); on a fixed-size type the
two operators lose the bits shifted out, give \EXP{0}, or \EXP{-1} for a negative
value shifted right, at a count at or beyond the width, and shift the other way for
a negative count; on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} they are \VAR{shift}; the count may be of any
integer type and is read by its value.
The entry of \VAR{shift} adds that the result is exact for a count of any size and
that a result too large for the implementation throws \TYP{IntegerOverflow}, and
that \OPR{LSHIFT} and \OPR{RSHIFT} compute the same shift on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}.
The overview of operators names \OPR{LSHIFT} and \OPR{RSHIFT} beside the bitwise
operators.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided it on
22~September 2026, with two further decisions on 24~September: that the signed
\VAR{narrow} truncates too, and that \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}'s shifts take a count of any
integer type.
\Library\ declare \OPR{LSHIFT} and \OPR{RSHIFT} on every integer type with a count of
any integer type (\nolinkurl{Library/FortressLibrary.fsi}, lines 459--460), and
\VAR{narrow} on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}64} and, until
\secref{revival-int-conversions} removed it, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}
(lines 506, 594 and 608), and this working draft said nothing of them.
In the languages the revival compared a shift is an operation on bits whose bits
shifted out are lost, and the revival kept it one on the fixed-size types, with an
exact arithmetic shift on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} under the name \VAR{shift}, which this
working draft already defines.
For a count at or beyond the width the rule is the one the team's interpreter has
followed since 2007 and three of its gated tests assert
(\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/BitTwiddle.fss}, lines 33, 36 and 39;
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/UnsignedTest.fss}, lines 30, 32, 135 and 137;
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/QuickCheckTest.fss}, lines 97--103).
Nothing pinned a negative count; it shifts the other way, and the count is read by
its value, so that a large unsigned count is not read as a negative one.
\VAR{narrow} keeps the low 32 bits as the team's own test asserts at the values
\EXP{2^{64}-1} and \EXP{2^{63}} (\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/UnsignedTest.fss},
lines 210--211), on the signed types as on the unsigned.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-integer-rules/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] A count at or beyond the width, or a negative count, has a specified
result, and so has \VAR{narrow} of a value that does not fit.
Both implementations of the revival compute these results on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} for a count of the shifted value's own type, and the interpreter
on all five types for a count of any integer type; the compiled path, which until it
is built on \library\ compiles against a smaller library of its own, declares
\OPR{LSHIFT} and \OPR{RSHIFT} on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} only, with
a count of the shifted value's own type, and no \VAR{shift} (rows~381, 383 and~442
of the revival's gap ledger).
\item[Original text] None for the two paragraphs and for the sentence of the
overview: no chapter of this working draft names \OPR{LSHIFT}, \OPR{RSHIFT} or
\VAR{narrow}.
The entry of \VAR{shift},
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/basic-integers.tex}, lines 698--702,
which the revision keeps and extends:
\begin{quote}
The result of \EXP{\VAR{shift}(x,k)} is \EXP{\lfloor x \cdot 2^k \rfloor}.
This corresponds to what is sometimes called an ``arithmetic shift''
on the two's-complement representation of the integer;
positive values of \VAR{k} shifts the bits to the left,
and negative values of \VAR{k} shifts the bits to the right.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The greatest common divisor and the least common multiple}
\seclabel{revival-gcd}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{basic-integers}; the overview of operators in
\chapref{operators}.
\item[Change] In the entry of \OPR{GCD} and \OPR{LCM}, the result for an argument
\EXP{0} (of \OPR{GCD}) and for an argument \EXP{1} (of \OPR{LCM}) is the absolute
value of the other argument, where it was the other argument, and the misspelling
``argment'' is corrected in those two sentences; the last property equates the
product of the two results with \EXP{\left|m \cdot n\right|}.
The overview of operators adds that both results are nonnegative and that, on a
fixed-size integer type, a result that the type cannot hold throws an
\TYP{IntegerOverflow}, with two examples on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided it on
22~September 2026.
Each paragraph of the entry begins by saying that the result is always nonnegative,
and the source of its declarations gives both results the type \EXP{\mathbb{N}}
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/basic-integers.tex}, lines
518--519); the sentences that followed contradicted that when the other argument is
negative, and the revival reads them as the absolute value, as both of its
implementations compute.
The overview of operators says that for integer results overflow throws an
\TYP{IntegerOverflow}; the revival applies that to \OPR{GCD} and \OPR{LCM}, as both
of its implementations do on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}.
\item[Effect] \EXP{0 \OPR{GCD} -4} is \EXP{4}, \EXP{1 \OPR{LCM} -5} is \EXP{5}, and
on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} a greatest common divisor or a least
common multiple that the type does not hold throws an \TYP{IntegerOverflow}, on both
implementations of the revival.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/basic-integers.tex},
lines 523--529:
\begin{quote}
The operator \OPR{GCD} computes the greatest common divisor of its two arguments.
The result is always nonnegative.  If either argment is \EXP{0}, the result
equals the other argument.

The operator \OPR{LCM} computes the least common multiple of its two arguments.
The result is always nonnegative.  If either argment is \EXP{1}, the result
equals the other argument.  If either argument is \EXP{0}, the result is \EXP{0}.
\end{quote}
and line 544, the last property:
\begin{quote}
\begin{Fortress}
{\tt~~}\(  \KWD{property} \forall (m, n)\; (m \OPR{GCD} n) \cdot (m \OPR{LCM} n) = m \cdot n\)
\end{Fortress}
\end{quote}
The overview of operators said nothing of either operator's sign or overflow.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The example of coercion resolution}
\seclabel{revival-coercion-example}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{resolving-coercion}.
\item[Change] In the example over \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} and
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}128}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} excludes the two others and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}
excludes \EXP{\mathbb{Z}128}; the sentence after it names the exclusion and the
rejection from which \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64 \morespecific \mathbb{Z}128} follows, and
says that the same facts make the example's two overloadings valid.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A. The example contradicted
the definition before it: \VAR{T} is no less specific than \VAR{U} only if \VAR{T} is
a subtype of \VAR{U} or excludes, coerces to and rejects \VAR{U}, and without the
clauses \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} neither excludes \EXP{\mathbb{Z}128} nor rejects it.
The clauses added are those the definition and the Meet Rule of
\secref{more-specific-rule} need, spelled as \library\ spell the exclusions of
their integer types, each type excluding the wider ones
(\nolinkurl{Library/FortressLibrary.fsi}, lines 510--511 and 552--553).
The team's later Types chapter says the same of an \KWD{excludes} clause: an
instantiation of the declared trait ``excludes every type in this set''
(\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/types.tick}, lines 344--351,
in the revival's repository).
\item[Effect] The example is a valid program, and both implementations of the revival
run it as printed, the call resolving to \EXP{f(\mathbb{Z}64)}; as the working draft
printed it, both refuse it.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/conversions-coercions.tex},
lines 551--552, the first two lines of the example:
\begin{quote}
\begin{Fortress}
\(\KWD{trait} \mathbb{Z}32 \KWD{end}\)\\
\(\KWD{trait} \mathbb{Z}64\)
\end{Fortress}
\end{quote}
and lines 562--565:
\begin{quote}
Then the call \EXP{f(\mathbb{Z}32)} resolves to the
declaration \EXP{f(\mathbb{Z}64)} because $\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}
\morespecific \EXP{\mathbb{Z}128}$, which follows from the fact that
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} coerces to \EXP{\mathbb{Z}128}.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The algebra of the rational trait}
\seclabel{revival-rational-algebra}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{basic-rationals}.
\item[Change] The listing of \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} extends
\EXP{\TYP{StandardPartialOrder}\llbracket\mathbb{Q}\rrbracket},
\EXP{\TYP{StandardMinMax}\llbracket\mathbb{Q}\rrbracket},
\EXP{\TYP{AdditiveGroup}\llbracket\mathbb{Q}\rrbracket} and
\EXP{\TYP{MultiplicativeRing}\llbracket\mathbb{Q}\rrbracket} besides \TYP{Number},
and one sentence after it says that their laws hold for the finite rationals and need
not hold where \EXP{0/0} or an infinity takes part.
\item[Rationale] The number chapters describe the number types that \library\
declare (\secref{revival-numbers}).
\Library\ declare these four traits on \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}
(\nolinkurl{Library/FortressLibrary.fsi}, lines 387--388), and their sums and
products over \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} rely on the group and the ring (the bounds of its
\OPR{SUM} and \OPR{PROD}, lines 1898--1909); the revision of the number chapters had left the listing
extending \TYP{Number} alone,
because \EXP{0/0} breaks the laws of a group and a ring
(\secref{revival-rationals}).
\Library\ declare the same four traits on \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, whose NaN breaks them
likewise (lines 291--292), and \secref{opr-properties} already says that the
algebraic properties of the rationals are obeyed exactly and those of floating-point
numbers only approximately.
\item[Effect] None on programs, since \library\ already declare the traits; the
listing agrees with the library's declaration.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/numbers.tex},
lines 177--183, the header of the listing, also quoted in \secref{revival-rationals}:
\begin{quote}
\begin{Fortress}
\(\KWD{trait}\:\mathbb{Q}\)\\
{\tt~~~~}\(    \KWD{extends} \{\, \null\)\(\mathbb{R}, \mathbb{Q}^*,\)\\
{\tt~~~~~~~~~~~~~~}\(              \TYP{Field}\llbracket\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\neq},+,-,\cdot,/\rrbracket,\)\\
{\tt~~~~~~~~~~~~~~}\(              \TYP{Field}\llbracket\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\neq},+,-,\times,/\rrbracket,\)\\
{\tt~~~~~~~~~~~~~~}\(              \TYP{Field}\llbracket\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\neq},+,-,\KWD{juxtaposition},/\rrbracket,\)\\
{\tt~~~~~~~~~~~~~~}\(              \TYP{TotalOrderOperators}\llbracket\mathbb{Q},<,\leq,\geq,>,\OPR{CMP}\rrbracket,\)\\
{\tt~~~~~~~~~~~~~~}\(              \TYP{PartialOrderAndLattice}\llbracket\mathbb{Q},\leq,\OPR{MIN},\OPR{MAX}\rrbracket\,\}\)
\end{Fortress}
\end{quote}
Before this revision the listing's header was
\EXP{\KWD{trait}\:\mathbb{Q}\;\KWD{extends}\:\TYP{Number}}.
\item[Route C] By the revival's reading, not measured, the same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The inference of a call's static arguments}
\seclabel{revival-inference}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \chapref{type-inference}; \secref{ranges};
\secref{basic-integers}; \secref{revival-ranges}; \secref{revival-notrevised}.
\item[Change] \chapref{type-inference}, which held a heading and two draft
notes, states how the static arguments of a call that does not write them are
inferred, for type parameters and for \KWD{nat} and \KWD{int} parameters.
The declaration is chosen first, as \secref{resolving-coercion} chooses it, a
declaration with static parameters being applicable to a call if some instance
of it within its bounds is, and compared on the parameter types it declares.
Its static arguments are then inferred: a type, \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int}
parameter that a parameter type mentions inside another type is fixed by the
argument, and a type parameter that the return type mentions inside another
type by the expected type; a type parameter that
is the whole type of parameters takes the narrowest type that their arguments
are substitutable for, which for number arguments is the narrowest type into
which every one of them converts, and otherwise, since 29~September 2026, its
bound (below); every
argument is admitted by substitutability, the coercions the instance needs are
inserted statically, and the converted call dispatches by subtyping.
A call written by tight juxtaposition, as a method invocation or as an operator
application, has an expected type in the contexts the chapter lists, a variable
or field declaration and an assignment to a variable among them, and is
inferred again without it when its result then converts.
A numeral whose conversions tie is read as a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, or as a
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} or a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} by its value; any other tie among
declarations applicable only with coercion is a static error.
On 8~November 2026 the rule for numerals widened (\secref{revival-numerals}):
a numeral takes that default at every place that fixes no type, a type
parameter that several numerals fix takes the narrowest default that holds them
all, and a numeral with a radix point is read as an \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}.
The chapter lists what it does not yet describe, the static arguments of a
\KWD{bool}, \KWD{dim}, \KWD{unit} or operator parameter among them, keeps the
second of the two draft notes, and has three boxes: on the Working Draft, the
bound it assumes and the passages it leaves to the revision of the overloading
chapters; on the interpreter; and on a type parameter that only the return
type mentions and on the \KWD{bool} parameters that neither implementation
infers.
The ranges section says that a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} parameter used as a
component converts to \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} as an integer numeral does, with a
box.
The box in \secref{basic-integers} says that a generic call over two different
integer types infers the narrowest type that both coerce into by the rule of
\chapref{type-inference}.
\secref{revival-notrevised} says that the rule is written and names the passages
the chapter leaves unrevised, and it and the effect of \secref{revival-ranges}
say that the question of a \KWD{nat} parameter in a range is settled.
On 29~September 2026 one sentence of the rule changed.
Of a type parameter that is the whole type of parameters, where no narrowest
candidate exists and the rule for numerals does not settle the tie, the
chapter said that it ``takes the union of the types of those arguments
(\secref{internal-types}), if its bounds permit that, and none of those
arguments is converted''; it now takes the intersection of its upper bounds,
its declared bound, \TYP{Any} if it has none, and what the expected type
requires of the declared return type, none of those arguments converted, with
a box.
With the revision of the overloading chapters (\secref{revival-overloading}),
the first box points to this entry's list of the cases in which the compiled
type checker departs from the chapter and no longer names the passages that revision revised, and the box
on the interpreter says to which type the interpreter binds such a type
parameter.
A later revision adds an item to the rule: a type parameter that neither an
argument nor the expected type fixes, such as one that only the return type
mentions, as its whole type, likewise takes the intersection of its upper
bounds, and inference never instantiates a static parameter at
\TYP{BottomType}, with an example and a box.
The list of what the chapter does not yet describe named instead ``a static
parameter that neither an argument nor the expected type fixes, such as a type
parameter that only the return type mentions, as its whole type, or the element
type of a reduction that only its generator clauses determine'', and left open
``whether inference may give such a parameter \TYP{BottomType}''; it now names
a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} parameter that nothing fixes, and the element type of
a reduction that only its generator clauses determine, to which the rule gives
the bound of the big operator's static parameter.
The box after the list said that the compiled type checker binds such a type
parameter to \TYP{BottomType}, except that it refuses a call with no expected
type when the parameter is declared \EXP{\KWD{extends} \TYP{Any}}, and that
``whether inference may do so is the team's question, kept in the draft note
below''; it now says that the checker follows the rule, what the compiled path's
bound \TYP{Object} makes of it, what reaches a reduction's big operator and what
the interpreter does, and that the rule answers the third item of the team's
draft note, which the chapter keeps.
A later revision of the interpreter changes both boxes on it.
Since the compiled path gives a type parameter declared without an
\KWD{extends} clause the bound \TYP{Any} too (\secref{revival-bound}), the first
box no longer says that the compiled path takes \TYP{Object} for such a
parameter of a program's own declarations (row~412 of the revival's gap ledger),
and the box after the list says that such a parameter may then be fixed by the
expected type alone at a type that \TYP{Object} excludes, such as \TYP{()},
where it said that such a call is a static error there.
The box in \secref{static-arg-inference} said that, where no argument's type is
the narrowest, the interpreter ``binds a type parameter that is the whole type
of parameters to the narrowest named common supertype of the arguments'
run-time types that the bounds permit'', the bound only where the bound is that
supertype; it now says that the interpreter gives such a type parameter its
bound, \TYP{Any} if it has none, and does the same where the arguments that fix
a type parameter inside other types have several minimal common supertypes,
except a type parameter whose bound mentions a static parameter and one
declared with several bounds that the interpreter cannot meet, which it binds as
before to the narrowest named common supertype of the arguments' run-time
types, a call that fixes one of the second kind failing where there is none;
that when it chooses among declarations it passes over a declaration with
static parameters whose arguments have several minimal common supertypes in
favour of another applicable without coercion; and, until 3~October 2026, that
it instantiates at \TYP{BottomType} a type parameter that an argument bounds
only from above.
The box after the list said that ``The interpreter erases a type parameter that
nothing at a call fixes, of a function, to \TYP{BottomType}''; it now says that
the interpreter instantiates such a type parameter at its bound, and one whose
bound mentions other static parameters at that bound with their instances,
except a type parameter that only the bound of a type parameter that the
arguments fix mentions, which it instantiates at the arguments' type, and
names the type parameters it still erases: one declared with several bounds
that it cannot meet, one whose bound mentions the type parameter itself, a big
operator's that a reduction or a comprehension does not write, and one that
only the result of a function expression without a declared return type
fixes.
A revision of the interpreter of 3~October 2026 changes the box in
\secref{static-arg-inference} once more.
It said that the interpreter ``instantiates at \TYP{BottomType} a type
parameter that an argument bounds only from above, as a function argument
bounds the type parameter of its parameter type''; it now says that the
interpreter instantiates such a type parameter at the intersection of the upper
bounds that its declared bound and the arguments place on it, with two
examples, and still at \TYP{BottomType} one whose bound mentions a static
parameter or that is declared with several bounds that it cannot meet, and
that a call whose arguments place on such a type parameter two upper bounds
that it cannot meet fails.
A revision of the interpreter of 9~October 2026 changes the box after the list
once more.
It said that the interpreter ``still erases to \TYP{BottomType} a type
parameter whose bound mentions the type parameter itself, as the big operators
$\sum$ and $\prod$ declare theirs, and the static parameters of a big operator
that a reduction or a comprehension invokes without its static arguments''; it
now says that the interpreter leaves open a type parameter whose bound mentions
the type parameter itself and that nothing at a call fixes, admitting every
value where it checks a value against that type, what that gives a reduction
whose element type nothing fixes, and that it still erases the other static
parameters of such a big operator.
A revision of the interpreter of 14~October 2026 changes both boxes on it once
more.
The box in \secref{static-arg-inference} said that the interpreter ``does not
yet fix a type parameter by the expected type''; it now says that it does so
only at a reduction or a comprehension that writes no static argument and whose
value is that of a local variable declared with a type or of a top-level or
local function declared with a return type.
The box after the list said that the interpreter ``still erases to
\TYP{BottomType} the other static parameters of a big operator that a
reduction or a comprehension invokes without its static arguments, a
comprehension's collection being then built at the type of its elements'', and
that an empty reduction whose element type nothing fixes takes the identity of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and the library's set reductions stop at their first
element; it now says that the interpreter instantiates such a big operator by
the declared type of the local variable, or the declared return type of the
top-level or local function, whose value the reduction or the comprehension is,
otherwise by the join of the run-time types of its
elements, and where there is none at its bound, or open where the bound
mentions the parameter, and names what that leaves: an empty reduction or
comprehension that no declared type directs, a comprehension that no declared
type reaches, and a big operator whose declared result is not one of the
library's three.
A revision of the interpreter of 17~October 2026 changes both boxes on it once
more.
The box in \secref{static-arg-inference} said that the interpreter compares
declarations with static parameters on the parameter types they declare,
``except generic dotted methods''; it now says that the interpreter infers the
static arguments of a generic dotted method as it infers a generic function's,
alone under its name or among the overloads of its receiver, and compares such
a method on the parameter types it declares only where exactly one of two
declarations' declared parameter types lies inside the other's, says that it
compares a functional method of a generic trait or object on the instance that
a call makes, names the call of an inherited generic functional method that
finds no overload, names the two forms of generic
dotted method on which it still stops, and says that it instantiates one that a
trait declares without reading its bounds where they are declared.
The box after the list said that the interpreter ``leaves a generic method's
static parameters uninstantiated''; it now says that it instantiates one that
only the expected type would fix at its bound, as it does one of a generic
function, except a bounded one of a method that a trait declares, and that it
stops at load on an object or trait whose method has a parameter type with a
\KWD{bool} static argument.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A.
The revival decided on 26~September 2026 that a generic call over two different
integer types infers the narrowest type that both coerce into, by a rule
written into this chapter; on 27~September 2026 that the compiled type checker
infers static arguments with coercion and keeps the expected type at a call
written as \EXP{f(x)}, with a retry without it so that a coercion of the result
still applies, and that the chapter is written with it; and on 28~September
2026 that a coercion makes a call possible and never changes which declaration
runs when one is applicable without it, the declaration chosen being then
instantiated by that rule, that a numeral whose conversions tie is read as a
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and that a \KWD{nat} parameter used as a value converts to
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} as a numeral does.
\chapref{multiple-dispatch}, \secref{idn-ref} and \secref{dotted-method-calls}
said that static arguments are inferred as \chapref{type-inference} describes,
and it described nothing; once the compiled type checker infers them by a rule
the text does not state, the text and the implementation disagree, so the
chapter states the rule the checker was built to, and the effect below lists
where the checker departs from it.
The bound in place of the union is the rule by which the dispatcher of the type
group's paper of 2019 instantiates a type parameter that the arguments do not
fix, the intersection of its upper bounds, never \TYP{BottomType}; the team
had assumed the union of the lower bounds, of which the authors write ``we were
unable to complete the proofs of correctness'' (Park, Hong, Steele and Ryu,
``Polymorphic Symmetric Multiple Dispatch with Variance'', POPL 2019,
Section~5.4).
The paper gives no algorithm for static inference and asks of it only that it
never instantiate at \TYP{BottomType}; its one example of a static call is
typed at the union of its arguments' types.
The revival decided on 29~September 2026 to take the paper's rule for static
inference as well, which the paper allows and does not require.
The same rule gives a type parameter that neither an argument nor the expected
type fixes the intersection of its upper bounds: the paper's solving step
``never instantiates method type parameters with Bottom'' (Section~5.2), which
answers the question the Working Draft's own note asks, whether type inference
may infer \TYP{BottomType} for a static parameter.
The reasoning for that item, and for the order in which the compiled type
checker tries a call, is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-instance-bound/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
The revival's decision of 29~September 2026 provided that the interpreter
follow the same rule where it instantiates a declaration, from the run-time
types of the arguments; it has no static return type to add to the bound,
except, since 14~October 2026, the declared type of a local variable or of a
top-level or local function whose value is a reduction or a comprehension that
writes no static argument (below).
It departs from the rule for the type parameters that the two boxes name,
three kinds by decision and the others as defects.
Until 9~October 2026, by decision, a type parameter whose bound mentions the
type parameter itself kept \TYP{BottomType}: it has no bound to take before its
own instance is known, the paper's calculus admits no type parameter of a
method in an upper bound (p.~11:11), and the revival had not decided that case.
The revival decided on 8~October 2026 that the interpreter leaves such a type
parameter open where nothing at a call fixes it: it does not instantiate it,
and it admits every value where it checks a value against that type.
An open type parameter is no instance: the rule that inference never
instantiates a static parameter at \TYP{BottomType} keeps a call from an
instance that admits no value, and an open parameter refuses none.
The reasoning is in \nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/p1-judgement.md} in the
revival's repository.
Until 14~October 2026, by decision, a big operator's static parameter whose
bound does not mention it, that a reduction or a comprehension does not write,
kept \TYP{BottomType}: it is the element type of the reduction, which this
chapter does not yet describe, and from \TYP{BottomType} the interpreter's
library builds a comprehension's collection at the type of its elements, where
from the bound it would build it at the bound.
The revival decided on 14~October 2026 that the interpreter instantiates such a
big operator as the desugaring by the type \VAR{N} of the expression would
(\secref{revival-reductions}), with run-time types in place of \VAR{N}: by the
declared type of the local variable, or the declared return type of the
top-level or local function, whose value the reduction or the comprehension is,
which is the static return type that the rule above lets
constrain the instance, and otherwise by the join of the run-time types of the
elements that its generator clauses give, which fix the parameter as \VAR{N}
would and so are not the value's type of a parameter that nothing fixes; where
there is neither, the parameter takes its bound, or is left open where the
bound mentions it.
The reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/perf-probes/walk-plain-bound/REPORT.md} and
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-walk-elements/REPORT.md} in the
revival's repository.
By decision, a type parameter that only the bound of a type parameter that the
arguments fix mentions takes the arguments' type: the interpreter has no
expected type to fix it, and the library's operator that appends two covariant
collections builds its result at such a type parameter, which at its bound
would be \TYP{Any}.
By decision, a lone type parameter whose bound mentions a static parameter,
itself or another, keeps the narrowest named common supertype of the arguments
rather than its bound: the interpreter cannot evaluate such a bound before the
instances are known, and keeping the candidates it had leaves unchanged the
case of a type parameter whose bound mentions itself and that the arguments
fix, which the revival has not yet decided.
As defects, each gated by an expected failure of the revival's tests, the
interpreter erases to \TYP{BottomType} a type parameter declared with several
bounds that it cannot meet, having no intersection type, and a type parameter
that only the result of a function expression without a declared return type
fixes, since it gives that result the type \TYP{BottomType}.
Until 3~October 2026 it also erased a type parameter that an argument bounds
only from above, taking the lower end of the interval that the arguments give
the type parameter; it now takes the upper end, the intersection of the upper
bounds that the rule gives, except, as defects gated by expected failures, for
a type parameter whose bound mentions a static parameter, which it cannot
evaluate before the instances are known, and one declared with several bounds
that it cannot meet.
The reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-walk-instance/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
The order, a declaration chosen and then instantiated, is that of
\secref{resolving-coercion}, read on the parameter types that declarations
declare, as the type group's paper of 2011 reads a declaration with static
parameters: it ``is applicable to a type if and only if at least one of its
instances is'' (\nolinkurl{Papers/Types/setup.tick}, lines 396--397, in the
revival's repository).
The numeral's default is what the interpreter did before this change, giving
a numeral the narrowest of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} and
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}} that holds its value, \library\ declaring their numeral type
below \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}; the compiled path, whose library declares its
numeral type beside \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and excluding it, took one of the
declarations that a numeral left tied, and not always the same one (row~391 of
the revival's gap ledger).
It is also the Java programming language's type for an integer literal.
Both implementations make a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} parameter used as a value a
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} (\secref{revival-numerals}), where the compiled type checker
gave it the type of a numeral before 8~November 2026, so an \KWD{int} parameter
is read as a \KWD{nat} parameter is; on the Java
Virtual Machine the length and the indices of an array are 32-bit integers.
The team's later Types chapter (Luchangco, 2012) gives the coercion relation,
the coercion of a generic type and the valid instantiations of a quantified
type (\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/types.tick}, lines
938--947, 393--400 and 771--789, in the revival's repository) and never uses
the word ``infer''; the type group's paper leaves the run-time inference of the
static arguments of a dispatched declaration ``beyond the scope of this paper''
(\nolinkurl{Papers/Types/rules.tick}, lines 118--129).
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-inference/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] A generic call over two different integer types, or over a number
type and a numeral, need not write its static argument, and the interim rule
of the effect of \secref{revival-numbers} ends.
A declared type fixes a type parameter that the return type of a call written
as \EXP{f(x)} mentions inside another type, and a call whose result the
declared type converts is accepted as before.
Where the conversions of a numeral tie, the declaration or instance at its
default is taken, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} for a numeral that fits it and
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} for one with a radix point (\secref{revival-numerals});
where another argument's conversions tie, the call is a static error.
A \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} parameter may be a component of a range, as in the
specification's own examples.
The overloading chapters compare declarations on the parameter types they
declare and infer static arguments after the selection, and \secref{type-param}
gives a type parameter without an \KWD{extends} clause the bound \TYP{Any}
(\secref{revival-overloading}, \secref{revival-bound}).
A type parameter that nothing at a call fixes is instantiated at its bound
under the expected type and never at \TYP{BottomType}: a call whose compiled
run failed loading an instance over \TYP{BottomType}, or failed verification
where the call's result was carried as one, now runs; a call with no expected
type whose type parameter is declared \EXP{\KWD{extends} \TYP{Any}} is
accepted; and a call where the bound and the expected type have no common
subtype, such as a type parameter bounded by \TYP{Object} where \TYP{()} is
expected, is a static error unless the result of the instance at the bound
converts to the expected type.
The compiled type checker departed from the chapter in five cases, each gated
by an expected failure of its tests, until it was revised with the item above:
the expected type could let a declaration applicable only with coercion be
chosen over a declaration with static parameters that is applicable without it
(row~508 of the revival's gap ledger); a call that the rule instantiates at the
bound, with another argument converted, was refused (row~515); a type parameter
declared without an \KWD{extends} clause, which has no narrowest candidate, was
instantiated at the union of the arguments' types where the chapter gives
\TYP{Any} (row~516); an argument that is an instance at a union, which only the
checker's own union made, was refused where it fixes a type parameter
(row~518); and an argument whose static type is an intersection, which the
typing of a call between two traits with \KWD{comprises} clauses gives
(\secref{revival-meet}), fixed a type parameter at one of its conjuncts
(row~541).
It now follows the chapter in each, trying a call by subtyping without the
expected type first and keeping that attempt when its result converts to the
expected type, and the five tests are plain tests of the rule.
The compiled path's run time does not yet load an instance at an intersection
type (row~559), which the rule gives by two routes: an argument whose static
type is an intersection, as in the last of them, and a type parameter that
nothing at a call fixes whose declared bound and expected type are two traits
that meet at their intersection.
A program that the compiled path ran before this revision, at one conjunct of
such an intersection or at \TYP{BottomType}, now dies loading that instance.
A written bound, such as \TYP{Number}, is taken by both implementations where
the chapter gives it.
Under the interpreter, a type parameter that nothing at a call fixes runs at its
bound where it ran at \TYP{BottomType}, and a type parameter with no narrowest
candidate at its bound where it ran at the arguments' narrowest named common
supertype, except the type parameters that the boxes on the interpreter name;
a call whose arguments have several minimal common supertypes runs where it
stopped the interpreter, unless they fix a type parameter declared with several
bounds that the interpreter cannot meet or one whose bound mentions the type
parameter itself.
Since 3~October 2026, under the interpreter, a type parameter that an argument
bounds only from above runs at the intersection of its upper bounds where it ran at
\TYP{BottomType}, except the type parameters that the box in
\secref{static-arg-inference} names; a call whose arguments place on such a
type parameter two upper bounds that the interpreter cannot meet, as functions
of two traits neither of which extends the other do, still fails with an error
of unification, as it failed before (row~616 of the revival's gap ledger).
Since 9~October 2026, under the interpreter, a type parameter whose bound
mentions the type parameter itself and that nothing at a call fixes is left
open where it was instantiated at \TYP{BottomType}, so that a reduction whose
element type nothing fixes runs on the types of its elements where it stopped
at its first element (\secref{revival-reductions}); until 14~October 2026 an
empty such reduction took the identity of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} whatever the
type of the expression (row~645), and the library's set reductions
\EXP{\bigcup} and \EXP{\bigcap}, whose reductions take sets of the open
type, stopped (row~662).
Since 14~October 2026, under the interpreter, the big operator of a reduction
or a comprehension that writes no static argument is instantiated by the
declared type of its value, by the join of its elements' run-time types or at
its bound, where its static parameters with a bound that does not mention them
were instantiated at \TYP{BottomType}: an empty sum that is the value of a
function declared \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} gives the \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} zero, the
set reductions run, and a comprehension bound to a local variable declared
with its type is admitted; an empty reduction that no declared type directs
still takes the identity of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and an empty comprehension is
built at its big operator's bound (row~716); a comprehension that no
declared type reaches is built at the run-time types of its elements (rows~20
and~717). Since 16~October 2026 a big operator whose declared result is a
trait other than the library's three is instantiated through the one of them
that the trait extends, as one declared with it is (row~718).
Since 17~October 2026, under the interpreter, a dotted call that writes no
static argument to a generic method infers the method's own static arguments
from the run-time types of the arguments, as a call of a generic function does,
where it stopped with an error of unification, or found no matching overload
where the receiver overloads the method's name (row~21): a generic method that a
trait declares and an object inherits beside a plain overload of its own is
chosen where the plain one does not apply.
A generic functional method that a trait declares and an object inherits beside
a plain functional method of its own was not chosen where only it applies: the
call found no overload (row~765), until 18~October 2026.
It still stops at load on an object or trait that declares a generic dotted
method beside a plain one of the same name (row~760) and on an object or
trait whose method has a parameter type with a \KWD{bool} static argument
(row~761); it stops on an object that extends a generic trait whose dotted
method has a static parameter of its own bounded by the trait's, at load or at a
generic object's first instance (row~762); and a dotted call that writes
static arguments to a method its receiver overloads still stops (row~744).
Until 18~October 2026 it instantiated a generic dotted method that a trait
declares without reading the bounds where they are declared: a type parameter
that is the whole type of parameters took the narrowest named common supertype
of the arguments' run-time types, none of them converted, where the same method
declared in an object takes the narrowest type into which they convert, and a
bounded type parameter that nothing fixes took \TYP{BottomType} (row~766).
Since then it reads the bounds of a generic method that a trait declares, and
the types into which the arguments convert, where the trait declares the
method, as for a method that an object declares, and so it gives such a method
the instance that it gives the same method declared in an object, and chooses
the inherited generic functional method above where only it applies.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/inference.tex},
lines 12--27, which held after the chapter's heading two draft notes:
\begin{quote}
This chapter will include the Fortress static type inference mechanism.
\end{quote}
and the note that the chapter keeps:
\begin{quote}
\begin{itemize}
\item There seems to be a circular dependency between inference and
juxtaposition disambiguation -- you have to know if the subexpressions have
arrow types to disambiguate, but you can't do that without using inference,
which requires knowing how the variable of unknown type is used.
\item Type Inference (Dan's email titled ``Comments on a first reading of the
spec'' on 06/05/07)
\item Do we want to forbid such cases where type inference infers
\TYP{BottomType}s for static parameters?
\end{itemize}
\end{quote}
The other passages this change revises are the revival's own text of 27 and
28~September 2026, not the Working Draft's.
The ranges section said, in
\nolinkurl{Specification/basic/expressions/ranges.tex}, lines 46--48, before
this revision:
\begin{quote}
An integer numeral converts to \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} (\secref{literals}), and
it is a static error if a component of an explicit range has an integer type
other than \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}.
\end{quote}
The box in \secref{basic-integers} ended, in
\nolinkurl{Specification/basic-lib/basic-integers.tex}, lines 90--94:
\begin{quote}
A generic call over two different integer types is to infer the
narrowest type that both coerce into, by a rule to be written into
\chapref{type-inference} together with its implementation; until then such a
call writes its static argument, as in
\EXP{f\llbracket\mathbb{Z}64\rrbracket(a, b)}.
\end{quote}
The effect of \secref{revival-ranges} ended:
\begin{quote}
Whether a \KWD{nat} parameter may be a component of a range is not settled
(\secref{revival-notrevised}).
\end{quote}
and \secref{revival-notrevised} said:
\begin{quote}
The rule by which a generic call over two different integer types
infers the narrowest type that both coerce into is to be written into
\chapref{type-inference} together with its implementation
(\secref{revival-numbers}).
\end{quote}
and
\begin{quote}
Whether a \KWD{nat} parameter, which may appear wherever a variable of type
\EXP{\mathbb{N}32} can (\secref{natparams}), may be a component of a range
is not settled (\secref{revival-ranges}); the static ranges of
\secref{ranges} and the examples whose ranges a \KWD{nat} parameter bounds are
not revised.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The contexts that give a call an expected type}
\seclabel{revival-expected-contexts}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{static-arg-inference}; \secref{numeral-default}.
\item[Change] The list of the contexts in which a call has an expected type,
in \secref{static-arg-inference}, gives the last expression of a block the
block's expected type whether or not local declarations precede it, and gains
two contexts: a clause of an \KWD{if} expression without an \KWD{else} clause,
whose expected type is \TYP{()}, and a clause of a \KWD{typecase} expression
that has an expected type, its \KWD{else} clause among them, whose expected
type is that of the \KWD{typecase} expression.
The sentence after the list says that the expected type is used also for a call
written by loose juxtaposition, as \EXP{f\;x}.
A second revision adds a third context to the list: the body of a \KWD{label}
expression that has an expected type, and the \KWD{with} clause expression of
each \KWD{exit} expression whose target is that \KWD{label} expression, whose
expected type is that of the \KWD{label} expression.
A third revision adds a fourth: an argument of a call that the section does not
accept with its arguments typed without an expected type, where the argument is
itself a call with inferred static arguments, whose expected type is
the parameter type at its position of each declaration of the enclosing call in
turn, instantiated, where that declaration has static parameters, by the
arguments not so typed and the expected type of the enclosing call; and a
paragraph after the list says that the enclosing call then invokes, of the
declarations that are applicable to it with its arguments so typed and whose
return types fit its expected type, the one more specific than every other on
the parameter types they declare, and is otherwise typed as before, so that a
declaration that the section accepts for the call as its arguments are typed
without an expected type is never passed over.
The item of the list of what the chapter does not yet describe, in
\secref{numeral-default}, names the body of a \KWD{for} loop, and no longer
names the contexts that the list of contexts now gives, the body of a
\KWD{label} expression and an argument of another call among them.
Each changed passage has a box.
How a call is instantiated under its expected type is unchanged.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 8~October 2026 that these four contexts give a call the
type that the text already requires of them, a clause of a \KWD{typecase}
expression by the rule for the type of the \KWD{typecase} expression.
The text gives each context its type.
``If there is no \KWD{else} clause in an \KWD{if} expression, then every clause
must have type \EXP{()}'' (\secref{if-expr}).
``If the last element of the expression block is an expression, the value and
type of this expression are the value and type of the expression block as a
whole'' (\secref{block-expr}), whatever local declarations precede it.
The static arguments of an identifier reference ``are statically inferred from
the context of the function call'' (\secref{idn-ref}), however the call is
written.
``The type of a \KWD{typecase} expression is the union of types of all
right-hand sides of the typecase clauses'' (\secref{typecase}), which is a
subtype of the expected type when the type of each clause is.
Since the revival's decision of 29~September 2026 a type parameter that nothing
at a call fixes takes the intersection of its upper bounds, never
\TYP{BottomType} (\secref{revival-inference}), so a call of \VAR{fail} or
\VAR{builtinPrimitive} of \library, whose type parameter only the result
mentions, took the bound \TYP{Any} in these contexts, and the compiled type
checker refused thirteen such calls of \library\ that the interpreter runs
(row~560 of the revival's gap ledger).
The alternative not taken was that the library write the static argument at
each such call, as \EXP{\VAR{fail}\llbracket()\rrbracket(s)}: the revival
decided that the library's own practice is the standard, and the library does
not route round a gap of the type checker.
A reversal would take the four contexts out of the list of contexts and back
into the item, and the compiled type checker would refuse those calls again.
The second revision follows the revival's decision of 9~October 2026 that the
body of a \KWD{label} expression and the \KWD{with} clause values of its exits
take the expected type of the whole \KWD{label} expression, as the clauses of a
\KWD{typecase} expression take that of the \KWD{typecase} expression.
``The type of a \KWD{label} expression is the union of the type of the last
expression of its expression block and the types of the \KWD{with} clause
values of any \KWD{exit} expressions within the \KWD{label} expression whose
target is the \KWD{label} expression's name'' (\secref{label-expr}), which is a
subtype of the expected type when each of those types is.
The thirteenth of the calls of \library\ above ends a clause of a
\KWD{typecase} expression that ends the body of a \KWD{label} expression, and
took the bound \TYP{Any} there (row~642 of the revival's gap ledger).
A reversal of the second revision would take the body of a \KWD{label}
expression and its exits' \KWD{with} clause values out of the list of contexts
and back into the item, and the compiled type checker would refuse that call
again.
The third revision follows the revival's decision of 9~October 2026 that a
call written as an argument of another call is typed again with the parameter
type of each declaration of the enclosing call where the enclosing call is not
accepted otherwise, so that an expected type fixes static arguments by one rule
in every context.
The static arguments of an identifier reference ``are statically inferred from
the context of the function call'' (\secref{idn-ref}), and the context of a
call written as an argument is the parameter to which it is passed; since a
declaration is chosen on the parameter types it declares
(\secref{static-arg-inference}), each declaration of the enclosing call gives
the argument its own expected type.
The compiled type checker typed every argument without an expected type before
it chose a declaration, so that a type parameter of the argument that only its
return type mentions took its bound, and one that only a numeral fixes took the
numeral's own type: for the declarations
\EXP{\VAR{mk}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket()\COLON\TYP{Box}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket},
\EXP{\VAR{wrap}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket(x\COLON{}T)\COLON\TYP{Box}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket}
and \EXP{\VAR{takes}(b\COLON\TYP{Box}\llbracket\mathbb{Z}64\rrbracket)}, it
refused \EXP{\VAR{takes}(\VAR{mk}())} and \EXP{\VAR{takes}(\VAR{wrap}(3))}
(row~455 of the revival's gap ledger).
The argument is typed again only where the enclosing call is not accepted
otherwise, as a coercion is applied only to make a call possible
(\secref{static-arg-inference}), so that a declaration that the section accepts
for a call as its arguments are typed without an expected type is never passed
over.
The alternative not taken was to type again only an argument whose own
declaration has a type parameter that its parameter types do not mention, which
accepts \EXP{\VAR{takes}(\VAR{mk}())} but not \EXP{\VAR{takes}(\VAR{wrap}(3))},
whose type parameter the numeral fixes.
A reversal of the third revision would take the argument of a call out of the
list of contexts and back into the item, and take out the paragraph after the
list, and the compiled type checker would refuse those calls again.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-checker-expected-type/REPORT.md}
and, for the second revision, in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-checker-contexts/REPORT.md},
and, for the third, in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-argument-context/REPORT.md} and
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/argument-context-probe.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The compiled type checker gives these contexts their expected
types: it checks a clause of an \KWD{if} expression without an \KWD{else}
clause against \TYP{()}, and it gives the expected type of the enclosing
expression to the last expression of a block after local declarations, to a
clause of a \KWD{typecase} expression, to the body of a \KWD{label} expression
and the \KWD{with} clause expressions of its exits, and to the application that
a loose juxtaposition makes.
The thirteen calls of \library\ that it refused now check.
It gives an argument of another call the parameter type of each declaration of
the enclosing call in turn, as the list of contexts and the paragraph after it
say, where the argument is a call with static arguments and no attempt at the
enclosing call with its arguments typed without an expected type is accepted;
an argument that writes its static arguments it types again too, to the same
type, and such an argument fixes the static arguments of a generic declaration
of the enclosing call as any other argument does.
Until 14~October 2026 it gave none there (row~455 of the revival's gap ledger);
it now accepts \EXP{\VAR{takes}(\VAR{mk}())} and
\EXP{\VAR{takes}(\VAR{wrap}(3))} above, and such an argument of a generic
declaration, of a method, of an operator or of a constructor, at any depth of
nesting.
Where two declarations, neither more specific than the other, accept the
enclosing call with the argument so typed, it reports the call as not
applicable to the argument typed without an expected type, as the paragraph
after the list has it, and not as ambiguous.
A reduction whose big operator's static argument is not written, which the
reduction's desugaring makes a call written as an argument of another call
(\secref{reduction-expr}), so takes the reduction's expected type where the
big operator's instance at its bound gives the reduction a type that does not
fit it; it is refused as before where the big operator has no instance at its
bound, as one whose bound mentions its own static parameter has none
(row~425).
It gives the expected type also to an operator repeated between three or more
operands, as \EXP{p \oplus q \oplus r}: to the multifix application where a
multifix declaration accepts the operands, and otherwise to the outer of the
binary applications \EXP{(p \oplus q) \oplus r} that it stands for, each an
operator application, which the sentence after the list gives the expected
type; whether the multifix application applies is decided without the expected
type, as for a loose juxtaposition.
Until 9~October 2026 it gave none there (row~644).
It gives the expected type also to the \KWD{juxtaposition} operator
application that a tight juxtaposition of items none of which is a function
stands for (\secref{juxtameaning}), as \EXP{a(b)} where \VAR{a} is not a
function: to the multifix application where a multifix declaration accepts the
items, and otherwise to the outermost of the left-associated binary
applications that it stands for, as to \EXP{(a(b))(c)} for \EXP{a(b)(c)}, each
an operator application, which the sentence after the list gives the expected
type; whether the multifix application applies is decided without the expected
type, as for a loose juxtaposition.
Until 9~October 2026 it gave none there (row~660); the reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-array-bound/REPORT.md} in the
revival's repository.
The interpreter has no static types and is unchanged; it fixes no type
parameter by an expected type (\secref{static-arg-inference}), so that
\EXP{\VAR{takes}(\VAR{mk}())} and \EXP{\VAR{takes}(\VAR{wrap}(3))} fail there
when they run, \VAR{mk} instantiated at the bound of its type parameter and
\VAR{wrap} at the type of the numeral's default, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and a call that another declaration of the
enclosing call accepts with the argument so instantiated runs that declaration
(row~705 of the revival's gap ledger).
\item[Original text] The two passages are the revival's own text of
29~September 2026, not the Working Draft's, whose chapter held a heading and two
draft notes (\secref{revival-inference}); the paragraph after the list of
contexts is new in the third revision.
The list of contexts said, in \nolinkurl{Specification/basic/inference.tex},
lines 129--139, before this revision:
\begin{quote}
A call has an expected type in these contexts: as the right-hand side of a
variable or field declaration that declares its type, that type; as the
right-hand side of an assignment to a variable whose type is declared, that
type; as the body of a functional that declares its return type, that type;
as the last expression of a block that has an expected type, where no local
declaration precedes it in the block, the block's expected type; and as a
branch of an \KWD{if} expression with an \KWD{else} clause that has an
expected type, the expected type of the \KWD{if} expression.
The expected type is used whether the call is written by tight juxtaposition,
as \EXP{f(x)} (\secref{juxtameaning}), or as a method invocation or an operator
application.
\end{quote}
The item of the list of what the chapter does not yet describe said, in lines
254--258:
\begin{quote}
an expected type in any context other than those listed above, such as
an argument of another call, an expression that a local declaration precedes in
a block, an \KWD{if} expression without an \KWD{else} clause, a \KWD{typecase}
expression, the body of a \KWD{for} loop, or a call written by loose
juxtaposition, as \EXP{f\;x};
\end{quote}
Before the second revision, the list of contexts ended, in lines 138--142:
\begin{quote}
\ldots; and as a clause of a \KWD{typecase} expression that has an expected
type, its \KWD{else} clause among them, the expected type of the
\KWD{typecase} expression, of which the union of the types of its clauses, the
type of the \KWD{typecase} expression (\secref{typecase}), is then a subtype
where the type of each clause is.
\end{quote}
and the item said, in lines 266--268:
\begin{quote}
an expected type in any context other than those listed above, such as
an argument of another call, the body of a \KWD{for} loop or the body of a
\KWD{label} expression;
\end{quote}
Before the third revision, the list of contexts ended, in lines 143--148:
\begin{quote}
\ldots; and as the body of a \KWD{label} expression that has an expected
type, or as the \KWD{with} clause expression of an \KWD{exit} expression whose
target is that \KWD{label} expression, the expected type of the \KWD{label}
expression, of which the union of the type of its body and the types of those
\KWD{with} clause values, the type of the \KWD{label} expression
(\secref{label-expr}), is then a subtype where each of those types is.
\end{quote}
and the item said, in lines 277--278:
\begin{quote}
an expected type in any context other than those listed above, such as
an argument of another call or the body of a \KWD{for} loop;
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The single-precision floating-point type}
\seclabel{revival-rr32}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{basic-rationals}; \secref{revival-numbers}.
\item[Change] The passage that names the number types names \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}
(\STR{RR32}) beside \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, a sibling of the others under
\TYP{Number}, and says that \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} coerces from \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}.
The effect of \secref{revival-numbers} no longer says that the library's
\EXP{\mathbb{R}32} stays below \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}.
\item[Rationale] \Library\ declared \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} a value object extending
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, so that a value of \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} was also a value of
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, which \secref{types-libraries} excludes: ``These types are
mutually exclusive; no value has more than one of them.''
The team's later Types chapter (Luchangco, 2012) keeps that sentence
(\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/types.tick}, lines
977--978, in the revival's repository).
Route~A makes the number types siblings under \TYP{Number}, each with its own
algebra, and the revival decided on 26~September 2026 that a conversion between
number types is a coercion where it is exact; every \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} value is
exact in \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}.
\Library\ now declare \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} extending \TYP{Number} and the four
algebraic traits at its own type, as they declare \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, and
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64}'s coercion from it
(\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/LibraryBuiltin/FortressBuiltin.fsi}, lines 47--48;
\nolinkurl{Library/FortressLibrary.fsi}, lines 291--296).
The team's own compiler library has declared the two types siblings since its
change of 31~August 2009, whose message says ``RR32 is NOT a subtype of RR64''
