and ``this would be a good time to get coercion working''; it declares the same
coercion today (\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/LibraryBuiltin/CompilerBuiltin.fsi},
lines 433--435 and 475).
The example of \secref{widening} computes an \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} with an
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} by the declaration on \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} after a coercion from
\EXP{\mathbb{R}32}, which is what the library now does.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-rr32-sibling/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] A value of \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} is not a value of \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}.
Two \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} values combine by the operators declared on
\EXP{\mathbb{R}32}, into an \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}; an \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} combined
with an \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} or a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} is
converted to \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, and the result is an \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}; an
\EXP{\mathbb{R}32} combined with a numeral by an operator that
\EXP{\mathbb{R}32} declares over two of its values, as $+$ or \OPR{MAX}, takes the
numeral converted into \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}, which declares a coercion from both
numeral types, and the result is an \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}; the interpreter departs
where a floating-point numeral stands to the left of the \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}, and
runs the operator of \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} (row~958 of the revival's gap ledger); an
\EXP{\mathbb{R}32} is converted where an \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} is expected.
A conversion from \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} into \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}, which can lose
precision, stays explicit.
\item[Original text] The Working Draft of February 2011 names \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}
in no passage of the number chapters; it says that ``32-bit precision floats are
written \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}''
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/preliminaries/overview.tex}, line 579),
that the numeric types are mutually exclusive
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/types-vals-vars.tex}, line 502), and
gives the example of \secref{widening}
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/conversions-coercions.tex}, lines
844--896).
The passages this change revises are the revival's own text of
27~September 2026, not the Working Draft's.
The passage said, in \nolinkurl{Specification/basic-lib/numbers.tex}, lines 27--31,
before this revision:
\begin{quote}
The number types of this chapter are
the integer types \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32},
\EXP{\mathbb{N}64}, and \EXP{\mathbb{N}32} (\secref{basic-integers}),
the rational type \EXP{\mathbb{Q}},
and the floating-point type \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} (\STR{RR64}).
\end{quote}
and, in line 43:
\begin{quote}
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} (\STR{RR64}) coerces from \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and from integer numerals (\secref{literals}).
\end{quote}
The effect of \secref{revival-numbers} said:
\begin{quote}
The library's \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} stays below \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, as before, a
value object that adds no instantiation of its own.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C: the numeric types are mutually exclusive
whichever rule on instantiations the compiled type checker keeps.
\end{description}

\subsection{The conversion into a float, declared by each number type}
\seclabel{revival-asfloat}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{basic-rationals}; \secref{revival-numbers}.
\item[Change] The passage on the conversions between number types says that
the explicit conversion into \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, \EXP{\VAR{asFloat}(x)}, is the
method that each number type declares, and that \TYP{Number} itself declares
no conversion, where it said that \library\ declare the method on
\TYP{Number}.
The change of \secref{revival-numbers} says the same.
\item[Rationale] The revival decided on 6~November 2026 that \VAR{asFloat}, and
the \EXP{=} that the library built on it, leave the trait \TYP{Number}, and that
each of the library's number types keeps its own \VAR{asFloat}.
The team's later compiler library declares \TYP{Number} with nothing in it
(\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/LibraryBuiltin/CompilerBuiltin.fss}, lines
510--511, in the revival's repository), and declares its conversion into
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} on each of its number types, not on a shared trait; the
Working Draft names no such method.
With \TYP{Number} open to a program's own number types, an abstract
\VAR{asFloat} would oblige a program's complex number to give a float, and an
\EXP{=} on \TYP{Number} built on it would call two different complex numbers
equal.
\Library\ now declare \VAR{asFloat} on \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{R}32},
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}32},
\EXP{\mathbb{N}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} and the two numeral types
\TYP{NaturalNumeral} and \TYP{RadixPointNumeral} (\secref{revival-numerals};
\nolinkurl{Library/FortressLibrary.fsi};
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/LibraryBuiltin/FortressBuiltin.fsi}), and the vector
norm at each of the eight number types as its element type.
A reversal would declare \VAR{asFloat} on \TYP{Number} again, abstract, with an
\EXP{=} built on it, so that every type below \TYP{Number} must give a float, or
declare it on a new trait that the number types extend.
\item[Effect] \EXP{\VAR{asFloat}(x)} is unchanged at every number type of the
library.
Two numbers of different types compare by \EXP{=} where a coercion carries one
into the other: for each of the library's fourteen coercions between number
types, and each of its eleven from the two numeral types
(\secref{revival-numerals}), the type converted to declares \EXP{=} at the type
converted from, and that type declares \EXP{=} at the first, each converting
the narrower operand as the coercion does.
Two numbers of different types that no coercion joins, as a \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}
and an \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, compare by the library's fallback \EXP{=} over
\TYP{Any}, which answers \EXP{a \sequiv b}; a program that means their values
writes the conversion, \EXP{\VAR{asFloat}(x) = y}.
Two values of different types are never strictly equivalent
(\secref{equivalence}), so that fallback answers \VAR{false} for them, on the
interpreter as in the compiled code's helper.
A number type of a program's own compares by the \EXP{=} it declares, or else by
the fallback.
\item[Original text] The Working Draft of February 2011 has no such sentence:
the revival added it in its own text of 27~September 2026, not the Working
Draft's.
The passage said, in \nolinkurl{Specification/basic-lib/numbers.tex}, lines
46--49, before this revision:
\begin{quote}
Every other conversion between number types is written explicitly;
in particular, a conversion into \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} from
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}32}, or \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}
is explicit, written \EXP{\VAR{asFloat}(x)} with the method that \library\ declare on \TYP{Number}.
\end{quote}
and the change of \secref{revival-numbers} said that the conversion into
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} is written ``with the method the library declares on
\TYP{Number}''.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{Overloading with static parameters}
\seclabel{revival-overloading}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{overloading-terms};
\secref{functional-applicability}; \secref{dotted-method-applicability};
\secref{resolving-overloading}; \secref{principles-overloading};
\secref{overloading-static-params}; \secref{resolving-coercion}; and, in a
draft build, the proposed features of the appendix ``Internal Document''.
\item[Change] The sentence of \secref{overloading-terms} that declarations of
one name may not differ in their static parameters, and its echo in
\secref{principles-overloading}, are replaced: declarations of one name may have
different static parameters, or some of them static parameters and others none,
and a declaration with static parameters is read as one declaration whose
parameter type ranges over its instances.
Such a declaration is applicable to a call when some instance of it within its
bounds is, and it is compared with others on the parameter types they declare,
taken over all their instances; the static arguments that a call does not write
are inferred for the declaration it selects, after the selection
(\chapref{type-inference}).
The three sentences of \chapref{multiple-dispatch} that inferred static
parameters before applicability was checked and before declarations were
compared, and the chapter's assumption that the static variables of a call have
been instantiated or inferred, are rewritten to match.
A new section, \secref{overloading-static-params}, states for declarations with
static parameters the three rules of the type group's paper of 2011, the No
Duplicates Rule, the Meet Rule and the Return Type Rule, the last checked over
every instance of the less specific declaration; how two parameter types with
static parameters exclude each other, by existential reduction with
instantiation exclusion; and a pair that the Return Type Rule refuses, in the
shape of that paper's own example, beside the pair that repairs it.
A box in that section states the restriction that the compiled implementation
adds, that of two declarations with as many static parameters of their own, one
of which is more specific than the other, the two agree on their static
parameters position by position, with an override that reorders them as its
example.
A later revision states the section's No Duplicates Rule on the relation without
``and not the other way round'', that $d_1$ and $d_2$ are not each applicable to
every call to which the other is applicable, with a box, and the box on Jan's
example in the internal document gives that rule as its reason.
A further revision has that box say that the factory of \library\ gives a
\TYP{Vector} where its type argument is one of the eight number types and a
plain array where it is any other type, \TYP{Number} among them: the factory
called the vector factory at its own type argument, which has no bound, an
instance that the bound of a \TYP{Vector}'s element type refuses
(\secref{type-param}), and it now builds the vector at each number type, so
that a \TYP{Vector} of \TYP{Number}, which is not well-formed, is no longer its
result.
Another revision has the section's existential reduction read instantiation
exclusion where the two parameter types, or their elements at one position, are
or extend instances of one parameterized trait, where it read it only where they
mention one, with a box, and the internal document's box on the questions on
exclusion says the same.
\secref{resolving-coercion} says in three places that a declaration with static
parameters is applicable when some instance of it is, that it is compared on the
parameter types it declares, and that the declaration selected is then
instantiated, the coercions of its instance inserted statically.
In the internal document, the proposed features on relaxing the restrictions on
the static parameters of overloaded functionals and on overloading with static
parameters are marked done, Jan's example answered by \library's factory and the
questions on exclusion by the existential reduction.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided
on 26~September 2026 that the sentence goes and that this working draft states
the model of the type group's paper of 2011 (Allen, Hilburn, Kilpatrick,
Luchangco, Ryu, Chase, and Steele, ``Type Checking Modular Multiple Dispatch
with Parametric Polymorphism and Multiple Inheritance'', OOPSLA 2011;
\nolinkurl{Papers/Types/rules.tick}, lines 132--180, and
\nolinkurl{Papers/Types/setup.tick}, lines 393--397, in the revival's
repository), which the compiled type checker applies, with two changes to the
checker: the Return Type Rule checked over every instance, and the positional
restriction.
On 28~September 2026 it decided that a coercion makes a call possible and never
changes which declaration runs when one is applicable without it, declarations
being chosen on the parameter types they declare and the declaration chosen
being then instantiated.
Neither implementation enforced the sentence: the interpreter has overloaded
declarations with different static parameters since May 2007, the compiled type
checker applies the rules of that paper to them, and \library\ overload across
different static parameters throughout.
The sentence is text of 2009.
The Fortress~1.0 release of 2008 compared generic declarations instead by
using ``their bounds as their supertypes'', in a section whose type relations
``may be replaced when the static type checker and the type inference engine
are implemented'' (\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/fortress.1.0.pdf},
Section~15.6.1), and the team's unfinished restart of this specification in
2012 dropped the sentence
(\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/overloading.tick},
lines 100--114, in the revival's repository).
The restart's Types chapter (Luchangco, 2012) gives function types that hold
generic and plain declarations together, as sets of arrow types and universal
arrow types, and drops from a quantified type a static parameter that appears
in none of its types
(\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/types.tick}, lines
553--558, 764--767 and 794--801).
The positional restriction is the compiled implementation's and not the
language's: its code generator hands the static arguments that a call writes,
by position, to whichever declaration dispatch reaches (row~398 of the
revival's gap ledger), while the type group's paper of 2019 instantiates a
dispatched declaration by substitution and renames static parameters freely
(Park, Hong, Steele and Ryu, ``Polymorphic Symmetric Multiple Dispatch with
Variance'', POPL 2019, Sections~4 and~5.2).
That paper's soundness proof excludes an upper bound that names a type
parameter, its own included, and closed types, so that \library's families over a type parameter
bounded by an instance of a trait at itself, such as \STR{SUM}, \STR{BIG~MAX}
and those over \TYP{Integral}, rest on the rules of 2011 as the compiled type
checker implements them, and not on the proof of 2019.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-overloading/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
The No Duplicates Rule was first stated on the strict sense of more specific
that the section defines, under which no two declarations are each more
specific than the other, so that it refused no pair, and two declarations each
applicable to every call to which the other is fell to the Meet Rule (row~696
of the revival's gap ledger).
The paper of 2011 defines its relation of specificity without strictness, one
declaration being more specific than another when every type to which the first
is applicable is one to which the second is, and its No Duplicates Rule
``forbids distinct declarations from being equally specific''
(\nolinkurl{Papers/Types/rules.tick}, lines 97--108, 158--162 and 184--185);
the paper of 2019 states it as ``Distinct method declarations must have
different applicable sets'' (Section~2.2), and its rules [No-Dup-Not-Less] and
[No-Dup-Not-Gtr] refuse exactly a pair whose domain types are each a subtype of
the other (Section~4.1 and Figure~5).
The restatement follows the revival's decision of 23~September 2026 that the
type group's later word, that paper among it, outweighs the early text; its
reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-popl-examples/REPORT.md} in the
revival's repository.
The revision of the existential reduction follows the paper of 2011, whose
exclusion of two instances of type constructors reads every pair of their
ancestors (\nolinkurl{Papers/Types/fig-exclusion.tick}, lines 136--147, in the
revival's repository), and the revival's decision of 17~October 2026 that two
declarations with static parameters exclude each other as the existential
reduction reads their whole parameter types, in both implementations; its
reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-existential-reduction/REPORT.md} in
the revival's repository.
The compiled type checker's test of exclusion reads the static parameters'
bounds by the revival's decision of 23~October 2026: the existential reduction of
the paper of 2011 solves the condition that the meet is not empty together with
the bounds of its static parameters (\nolinkurl{Papers/Types/exc-reduction.tick},
lines 40--46 and 68, in the revival's repository), which the compiled type
checker carried but from which its solver took no equality; its reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-self-type-exclusion/REPORT.md} in the
revival's repository.
\item[Effect] Declarations of one name may differ in their static parameters,
as \library's do.
The compiled type checker refuses the pair of the Return Type Rule's example,
which it accepted before and whose compiled class then failed verification, and
an override whose return type reorders its static parameters, which it accepted
before and which then failed at run time (row~398).
Neither implementation runs every set these rules allow.
The compiled type checker refuses more than the Return Type Rule where the more
specific declaration's parameter type is an object type, as in
\EXP{\VAR{ident}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket(x\COLON{}T)\COLON{}T} beside
\EXP{\VAR{ident}(x\COLON \TYP{Circle})\COLON \TYP{Circle}} where \TYP{Circle} is an
object (row~542); the compiled code generator fails on two declarations with
static parameters whose bounds differ, the pair the Return Type Rule allows in
\secref{overloading-static-params} among them, and on the example of the type
group's paper of 2019 that overloads a method over an array list and a list
(row~537); and the interpreter reads two functions with static parameters, no
pair of whose parameter types excludes each other as it compares them parameter
by parameter, on the two declared domains, each over its own instances, by these
rules, but finds a third declaration that holds the overlap of two neither of
which lies inside the other only where their parameter types are, position by
position, instances of one generic type or static parameters, so that it
refuses that example (row~539).
Of two generic functional methods one of whose declared domains lies inside
the other's, it refuses at load the pair that a trait or object without
static parameters declares, as failing the Meet Rule, where it loads the same
two functions (row~779), and it loads the pair that a trait with static
parameters declares, where a call to which both apply then fails, finding no
definition of the method (row~780).
Jan's example is still not a valid overloading, its two declarations having the
same parameter type.
The compiled type checker applies the No Duplicates Rule as it is now stated:
it refuses two declarations whose parameter types, read over their instances,
are each a subtype of the other, ``There are multiple declarations of \VAR{dup}
with the same parameter type'', two functional methods among them wherever their
\KWD{self} parameters stand.
Among them is a declaration whose parameter types are its type parameters,
written with no bound and so bounded by \TYP{Any}, beside one without static
parameters whose parameter types are \TYP{Any}, as
\EXP{\VAR{g}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket(a\COLON{}T)} beside
\EXP{\VAR{g}(a\COLON \TYP{Any})}, as is the same pair with the bound written,
\TYP{Any} or one below it; while the compiled path bounded such a type
parameter by \TYP{Object} (\secref{revival-bound}), it accepted that pair and
ran the first (row~759 of the revival's gap ledger).
It reads the abstract functional methods that a trait or object provides as it
reads the abstract dotted ones, so that an abstract functional method beside a
concrete one with the same parameter type is among the pairs it refuses, which
only its code generator refused before, as a malformed overload set.
It checks the overloading of every operator by these rules, a symbolic one such
as $+$ or a subscript as one whose name is a word of capitals, so that it refuses
two declarations of such an operator with the same parameter type, which only its
code generator refused before, as a malformed overload set; the interpreter's
check of the Meet Rule at load reads no symbolic operator (row~611 of the
revival's gap ledger).
The interpreter refuses at load two declarations with static parameters whose
parameter types, each read over its own instances, are each a subtype of the
other, as \VAR{dup}'s are, and so a declaration whose parameter types are its
static parameters beside one whose parameter types are their bounds, a plain one
or one whose parameter types mention none of its static parameters.
The compiled type checker and the interpreter both find that two declarations,
one or both with static parameters, whose parameter types exclude only together,
through a static argument that another parameter's type fixes, exclude each
other, and the compiled type checker finds so too of two whose parameter types
exclude through the one instance of a supertype they share, which the
interpreter loaded already.
The compiled type checker's test of whether two such parameter types exclude
each other also reads each static parameter that stands at the top of their
meet, the meet itself, an element of a tuple or a member of an intersection or
union, as within its declared bounds.
So it accepts, beside the $+$ of \TYP{AdditiveGroup}, the $+$ of \library\
between a number of type \VAR{T} and an
\EXP{\TYP{Array}\llbracket{}T, I\rrbracket}, with \VAR{T} bounded by
\TYP{Number} and \EXP{\TYP{AdditiveGroup}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket}, since no
array is a number; and it accepts
\EXP{\VAR{f}\llbracket{}T \KWD{extends} \{K, R\llbracket{}T\rrbracket\}\rrbracket(x\COLON{}T)}
beside \EXP{\VAR{f}(x\COLON{}Z)}, where \VAR{K} comprises \VAR{A}, \VAR{B} and
\VAR{C}, \VAR{Z} excludes \VAR{A} and \VAR{B}, and \VAR{C} extends \VAR{Z}
but not \VAR{R}, which the interpreter refuses at load as failing the Meet Rule
(row~713 of the revival's gap ledger).
The interpreter loads and runs the first of these shapes in a program's own
component too, generic operators between an array of a number type and a
number, on either side, beside the operator of a trait with a self-type
parameter: where the operator declares no static parameters of its own, it
reads the two declared domains as it reads two generic functions, and in its
test of exclusion reads the trait's \KWD{self} as the trait met with its
self-type parameter, for a trait whose own parameter is bounded by the trait
at that parameter.
Since it does not check that a type extending such a trait at a type argument
is of that type, it loads an object that breaks that rule, and runs one of the
two declarations that fit a call on it (row~893 of the revival's gap ledger).
The interpreter does not so read a declaration with a \KWD{where} clause of its
own, and refuses such a pair, which the compiled type checker accepts and its
code generator does not compile (row~787 of the revival's gap ledger).
The interpreter also reads an object type over a static parameter as excluding
a type that the object type is at some instance of the static parameters, and
so loads pairs that the Meet Rule refuses, as
\EXP{\VAR{g}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket(x\COLON \TYP{Box}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket,
y\COLON \mathbb{Z}32)} beside \EXP{\VAR{g}(x\COLON
\TYP{Box}\llbracket\mathbb{Z}32\rrbracket, y\COLON \TYP{Any})}, where \TYP{Box}
is an object type: the two overlap at a
\EXP{\TYP{Box}\llbracket\mathbb{Z}32\rrbracket} and a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and
neither is more specific than the other; the interpreter runs the first for a
call to which both apply, and the compiled type checker refuses the pair
(row~788 of the revival's gap ledger).
They also differ on a pair whose static parameters' bounds exclude each other, as
\EXP{\VAR{tint}\llbracket{}T \KWD{extends} \TYP{Red}\rrbracket(x\COLON
\TYP{Box}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket)} beside
\EXP{\VAR{tint}\llbracket{}T \KWD{extends} \TYP{Blue}\rrbracket(x\COLON
\TYP{Box}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket)} where \TYP{Red}, \TYP{Blue} and
\TYP{Box} are object types: the interpreter loads it, reading two static
parameters whose bounds exclude each other as never one type, and the compiled
type checker refuses it, its reduction making of the two static parameters one
bounded by both, which it does not find empty: though its test of exclusion reads
the bounds, \EXP{\TYP{Box}\llbracket\TYP{BottomType}\rrbracket} is an
instance of both domains (row~786 of the revival's gap ledger).
The calculus of \appref{overloading-calculus} keeps its rule that two
declarations with the same number of parameters have static parameters with the
same bounds; it admits fewer overloadings than these chapters, so that its valid
programs are still valid, and its rules and its soundness claim are unchanged
(\secref{revival-calculi}).
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/overloading.tex},
lines 100--108:
\begin{quote}
Although there may be multiple declarations with the same functional name,
it is an error for their static parameters to differ (up to
$\alpha$-equivalence), or for one declaration to have static parameters and
another to not have them.
Hence, static parameters do not enter into the determination of which
declarations are applicable, so we ignore them for most of this chapter.
\end{quote}
lines 137--138:
\begin{quote}
We assume throughout this chapter that all static variables in functional calls
have been instantiated or inferred.
\end{quote}
lines 173--175, and in the same words for the types $\Ps_0$ and $\Ps$ of a
dotted method declaration, lines 205--207:
\begin{quote}
If the parameter type $\Ps$ includes static parameters, they are inferred as
described in \chapref{type-inference} before checking the applicability of the
declaration to the call.
\end{quote}
lines 292--295:
\begin{quote}
If the declarations include static parameters, they are inferred as described
in \chapref{type-inference} before comparing their parameter types to determine
which declaration is more specific.
\end{quote}
and \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/advanced/overloading.tex}, lines
95--103:
\begin{quote}
Overloaded declarations must have static parameters that are identical (up to
$\alpha$-equivalence).  Also, valid overloadings for declarations that contain
keyword or varargs parameters are determined by analyzing the expansion of these
declarations as described in \secref{applicability-with-special-params}.
Therefore, static parameters, keyword parameters, and varargs parameters are
ignored in the remainder of this chapter.
\end{quote}
The sentences added to \secref{resolving-coercion}, after the Working Draft's
lines 455--459, 522--524 and 567--570 of
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/conversions-coercions.tex}, and the new
section have no counterpart there.
The new section, the revival's text of 30~September 2026, stated:
\begin{quote}
The \emph{No Duplicates Rule}: $d_1$ and $d_2$ are not each more specific than
the other.
\end{quote}
and, of the existential reduction:
\begin{quote}
where the two parameter types mention one parameterized trait, instantiation
exclusion (\secref{trait-types}) lets them meet only where the static arguments
of those mentions are the same
\end{quote}
and the box on Jan's example in the internal document gave as its reason that
the two declarations have the same parameter type ``and each is more specific
than the other''; until the further revision, the box ended: ``which chooses its
result by a \KWD{typecase} on its type argument, a \TYP{Vector} where the
argument is a \TYP{Number}''.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C, except that the existential reduction
uses the rule of \secref{revival-routec} in place of instantiation exclusion;
the No Duplicates Rule does not depend on the rule on instantiations.
\end{description}

\subsection{The implicit bound of a type parameter}
\seclabel{revival-bound}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{type-param}; \secref{principles-overloading}.
\item[Change] A type parameter declared without an \KWD{extends} clause has the
implicit clause ``\EXP{\KWD{extends} \TYP{Any}}'' in place of
``\EXP{\KWD{extends} \TYP{Object}}'', with a box.
The restriction of \chapref{overloaded-declarations} that a functional whose
single parameter's type is a naked type parameter of bound \TYP{Any} cannot be
overloaded is stated for a bound written \TYP{Any}, and a sentence says that a
type parameter declared without an \KWD{extends} clause does not fall under it,
with \library's value factory of one-dimensional arrays as its example, and a
box.
The last sentence of the box in \secref{type-param} said that the compiled path
still gives such a type parameter of a program's own declarations the bound
\TYP{Object} (row~412 of the revival's gap ledger), a divergence that lasts
until the compiled path is switched over to the interpreter's library; since the
compiled path gives it the bound \TYP{Any} too, the box says so, and points to
this entry for the instances that still fail there.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival took
\TYP{Any} as the implicit bound on 27~September 2026 and decided it on
29~September 2026.
\TYP{Any} holds every value, tuples, functions and \TYP{()} among them; the
team's later Types chapter makes \TYP{Object} a supertype of every trait type,
arrow type and function type that excludes \TYP{()} and every tuple type
(Luchangco, 2012,
\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/types.tick}, lines
232--236, in the revival's repository).
\Library\ instantiate type parameters declared without a bound at tuple types,
an array indexed by pairs among them, which the bound \TYP{Object} would refuse;
the interpreter, and the compiled type checker when it checks \library, read
such a parameter as bounded by \TYP{Any}.
The type group's paper of 2019 gives the same default: ``By default, each type
parameter has an upper bound Any and a lower bound Bottom'' (Park, Hong, Steele
and Ryu, POPL 2019, Section~3.2).
Under the implicit bound \TYP{Any}, the restriction on a naked type parameter of
bound \TYP{Any} would forbid overloading every functional whose single
parameter's type is a type parameter declared without a bound, \library's
factory \EXP{{array}_{1}\llbracket{}T, \KWD{nat}\:s_{0}\rrbracket(v\COLON{}T)}
beside \EXP{{array}_{1}\llbracket{}T, \KWD{nat}\:s_{0}\rrbracket()} among them;
the compiled type checker applies the restriction only to a bound written
\TYP{Any}, and draws no such refusal on \library.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-overloading/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] A type parameter declared without a bound may be instantiated
with a tuple type or with \TYP{()}, on the compiled path as in the interpreter.
On the compiled path some such instances compile and then fail at run time: an
instance at \TYP{()} where a parameter has the type parameter's type (row~339
of the revival's gap ledger), and a generic dotted method whose one parameter
has a tuple type (row~832).
Until the compiled path is switched over to the interpreter's library, the
compiler's own library declares the concatenation of a \TYP{String} with an
\TYP{Object}, and \VAR{print} and \VAR{println} of an \TYP{Object}, not of
\TYP{Any}, so there a string concatenated with a value of such a type parameter,
or such a value printed, is a static error.
The rationale of \secref{revival-tabulate}, written before this change, speaks
of the implicit bound \TYP{Object}.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/trait-parameters.tex},
lines 49--50:
\begin{quote}
If a type parameter does not have an \KWD{extends} clause, it has an
implicit ``\EXP{\KWD{extends} \TYP{Object}}'' clause.
\end{quote}
and \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/advanced/overloading.tex}, lines
114--116:
\begin{quote}
In addition, a functional which takes a single parameter of type a naked type
parameter of bound \TYP{Any} cannot be overloaded.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The instance at which a dispatched declaration runs}
\seclabel{revival-dispatch}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{resolving-overloading}.
\item[Change] The paragraph on dispatch gains sentences: a declaration with
static parameters is applicable to a call at run time when some instance of it
within its bounds is, so the declaration to which a call dispatches may be one
that the static call did not select; a declaration that the static call
selected runs at the instance inferred for the call, and any other at the
instance in which each type parameter is the intersection of the upper bounds
placed on it, its declared bound and those that the dynamic types of the
arguments and the static type of the call place on it, fixed by the value where
the type parameter is a static argument of a parameterized trait in a parameter
type, and never \TYP{BottomType} nor the union of the types of the arguments.
A box says that both implementations take the dynamic type of the argument where
the value does not fix the type parameter.
Two examples follow the definition of ``more specific'': a generic declaration
beside a plain one that is more specific, and a generic declaration that a call
reaches only by dispatch, at the instance the value fixes, with a box.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A.
Which declaration runs follows from the revival's decision of 28~September 2026
that declarations are compared on the parameter types they declare, a
declaration with static parameters being applicable when some instance of it is;
the revival confirmed on 29~September 2026 that a generic declaration that a
call reaches only by dispatch runs.
The instance is the rule of the type group's paper of 2019, which the revival
took on 29~September 2026: its dispatcher solves the bounds that the dynamic
types of the arguments and the static type of the call place on each type
parameter and ``computes a substitution \ldots\ by taking the intersection of
the upper bounds'', never instantiating a type parameter with \TYP{BottomType};
the team had assumed ``that once the tightest possible upper and lower bounds
for a method type parameter have been found, then the union (or pseudo-union) of
the lower bounds should be chosen'', and for it ``we were unable to complete the
proofs of correctness'' (Park, Hong, Steele and Ryu, POPL 2019, Sections~5.2
and~5.4).
Naden's notes of 2012 on instantiation at run time restricted by the return type
(\nolinkurl{Papers/RuntimeInstantiation/}, in the revival's repository) are the
type group's draft of that rule; the type group's paper of 2011 left the
run-time inference of the static arguments of a dispatched declaration ``beyond
the scope of this paper'' (\nolinkurl{Papers/Types/rules.tick}, lines 118--129).
The paper's dispatcher solves every call so; this text keeps the instance
inferred for the declaration that the static call selected, which differs from
the paper's only for a type parameter that neither the static type of the call
nor an invariant position in a parameter type constrains.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-overloading/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] No value that a valid program computes changes.
The interpreter, which has no static types, and the compiled path, whose
dispatcher is not given the static type of the call, take the dynamic type of
the argument where the value does not fix the type parameter; the compiled
path's departure is gated as an expected failure (row~538 of the revival's gap
ledger).
The compiled path does not yet run a generic declaration that a call reaches
only by dispatch beside a plain one (row~496).
The instance of a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} parameter that nothing fixes at run
time is not described (\secref{revival-notrevised}).
\item[Original text] The paragraph is unchanged:
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/overloading.tex}, lines 263--276:
\begin{quote}
To evaluate a given functional call, it is necessary to determine which
functional declaration to dispatch to.
To do so, we consider the declarations that are applicable to that call at run
time.
If there is exactly one such declaration, then the call dispatches to that
declaration.
If there is no such declaration, then the call is \emph{undefined}, which is a
static error.
(However, see \secref{applicability-with-coercion} for how coercion may add to
the set of applicable declarations.)
If multiple declarations are applicable to the call at run time, then we choose
an arbitrary declaration among the declarations such that no other applicable
declaration is more specific than them.
\end{quote}
The Working Draft forbade both pairs of the examples, by its lines 100--105,
quoted in \secref{revival-overloading}.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The interpreter's choice of a coercion}
\seclabel{revival-coercion-walk}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{resolving-coercion}.
\item[Change] One box after the example of the paragraph that says that
coercion is resolved statically; the paragraph and its example are unchanged.
The box says that the compiled path follows the text; that the interpreter,
which has no static types, chooses a coercion on the value and so runs
\EXP{f(B)} for the example's \EXP{f(c)}, and answers the same way wherever an
argument's value is of a type that another declaration already fits; that both
cases are gated as expected failures; and that the interpreter is to follow the
text once it takes the coercions that the compiled type checker inserts.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided
on 29~September 2026 that the interpreter's choice of a coercion on the value is
its limit, since it has no static types from which to resolve a coercion
statically, and that the interpreter is made to follow the text once the
compiled type checker checks the programs it runs and it takes the coercions the
checker inserts.
The type group's paper of 2019 also carries the static types of calls to run
time for its dispatcher, which the interpreter does not have.
\item[Effect] None on the language.
The interpreter's answer, 4 where the text gives 3, and its answer in the
narrower case are gated by
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/XXXCoercionStaticRungC.fss} and
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/XXXCoercionStaticNarrowRungC.fss} (row~19 of
the revival's gap ledger).
\item[Original text] The passage is unchanged:
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/conversions-coercions.tex}, lines
567--604, which begin:
\begin{quote}
Notice that coercion is resolved statically.  Once a coercion is statically
chosen for a call, this coercion is conceptually inserted into the call.
This means that the statically chosen coercion is applied at run time.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{Traits with comprises clauses read at the level of values}
\seclabel{revival-covering}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{internal-types};
\secref{abstractFunctionDeclarations}; \secref{methods}; a draft note in the
subsection ``Export Statements'' of \secref{components}; the effect of
\secref{revival-comprises}.
\item[Change] \secref{internal-types} defines covering: a type covers another
when every value of the second is a value of the first; a type covers each of
its subtypes, and a trait with a \KWD{comprises} clause is covered by the union
of its listed types; covering is not the subtype relation.
Its example concludes that $V$ covers $S \inter T$, where it concluded that
$V = S \inter T$, and a sentence says that a trait may stand between $S$ or $T$
and their listed types, so that $S \inter T$ is not in general equal to a named
type.
\secref{abstractFunctionDeclarations} asks the argument types of the concrete
declarations to cover the argument type of the abstract declaration, where it
asked their union to equal it, and, in its example, for a definition for every
type listed in the \KWD{comprises} clause of \TYP{Molecule} or definitions that
together cover those types, where it asked for one for every immediate subtype.
Each of these four passages has a box.
The draft note says ``all its listed types'' where it said ``all its immediate
subtraits''; a draft note prints only in a draft build, so it has no box.
The effect of \secref{revival-comprises} no longer says that these passages are
not revised.
A correction of 9~October 2026 changes the last sentence of the box after the
example in \secref{abstractFunctionDeclarations} and the sentence of this
entry's effect that repeats it.
They said that the compiled type checker checks that an object's concrete
methods cover the abstract methods it inherits, ``reading no \KWD{comprises}
clause''; they now say that it reads such clauses to do so, so that concrete
methods over the types that a trait lists together cover an abstract method
over that trait.
The normative text is unchanged.
An amendment of 14~October 2026 revises the sentence of \secref{methods} on
abstract methods: an object inheriting an abstract method defines it by a method
definition whose parameter type, not counting the type of the self parameter, is
that of the abstract method declaration, or by definitions whose parameter types
are subtypes of it and together cover it, or it is a static error, where the
sentence said only that the object must define a body expression for the method.
The sentence has a box.
The requirement of \secref{object-decls} that an object define all abstract
methods inherited from its supertraits is unchanged; the box says that it is
read in this sense.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided
on 29~September 2026 that the passages that read a \KWD{comprises} clause at the
level of types are restated at the level of values, which is how
\secref{trait-decls} reads the clause since its revision of 28~September 2026
(\secref{revival-comprises}).
The team's later Types chapter names covering beside subtyping and exclusion as
a relation between types, and says that an instantiation of a trait with a
\KWD{comprises} clause ``is covered by the union'' of the listed types
(\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/types.tick}, lines 36--38
and 384--390, in the revival's repository).
Welterweight Fortress, the type group's calculus of 2012, reads the clause at
the level of values and counts an abstract declaration as covered when it is
implemented for all the types comprised
(\nolinkurl{Papers/Welterweight/grammar.tick}, lines 21--22;
\nolinkurl{Papers/Welterweight/fig-covered.tick}).
Under that reading a trait with static parameters may stand unlisted between a
trait with a \KWD{comprises} clause and its listed types; an instantiation of it
is then a subtype of the closed trait and of no listed type, though every value
of it is a value of a listed type, so the passages' conclusions hold of values
and not of types.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-overloading/decision-record.md}
and \nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/comprises-type-level-judgement.md} in the
revival's repository.
The sentence that the correction of 9~October 2026 changes was false when it was
written: since 6~June 2012 the team's compiled type checker, testing whether an
abstract method's parameter type is a subtype of the union of the parameter
types of the concrete methods below it, tests whether the union of the types
that a trait with a \KWD{comprises} clause lists is a subtype of that union
(\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/src/com/sun/fortress/scala_src/typechecker/AbstractMethodChecker.scala}
and \nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/src/com/sun/fortress/scala_src/types/TypeAnalyzer.scala},
in the revival's repository).
It accepts an object whose methods over the two types that a trait lists cover
an abstract method over that trait, and refuses the same object when the trait
has no \KWD{comprises} clause.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-walk-override/REPORT.md} in the
revival's repository.
The amendment of 14~October 2026 follows the revival's decision of 13~October
2026 that an object defines an inherited abstract method when its definitions
of that name at narrower parameter types together cover the abstract method's
parameter types, \KWD{comprises} clauses read as the compiled type checker
reads them; a definition at the abstract method's own parameter type is the
plain case.
It is the type group's rule of 2012.
The compiled type checker checks, at each object declaration, whether each
inherited abstract method ``is covered by one or more concrete definitions'',
its parameter type a subtype of the union of theirs (Steele, May 2012, in
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/src/com/sun/fortress/scala_src/typechecker/AbstractMethodChecker.scala},
in the revival's repository), and reads \KWD{comprises} clauses to do so since
6~June 2012.
Welterweight Fortress uses the same rules to check methods as functions, the
type of the trait or object within which the method is declared not considered
(\nolinkurl{Papers/Welterweight/static.tick}, lines 27--47).
The sentence of \secref{methods} is older than that reading; the chapter's own
sentence on overloaded functional methods points to
\secref{abstractFunctionDeclarations}, which asks for coverage, as the Meet Rule
does.
The team's library and tests define abstract methods by covering definitions:
the trait \TYP{RunRanges} of the component \TYP{Pairs}
(\nolinkurl{Library/Pairs.fss}) and the trait \TYP{dna} of the test
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/FileConversion.fss}, which the sentence read to
the letter would refuse.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/walk-load-readings-check.md}, section~2, in the
revival's repository.
\item[Effect] No example of this specification becomes invalid.
Covering is not subtyping: where an instantiation of a trait with static
parameters stands between $S$ and $T$ and their listed types, its value is a
value of $V$ but its static type is not a subtype of $V$, and the compiled type
checker refuses to assign it to a variable of type $V$; the interpreter, which
has no static types, runs such an assignment.
The compiled type checker checks, at each object declaration, that the
object's concrete methods cover the abstract methods it inherits, and reads
\KWD{comprises} clauses to do so: concrete methods over the types that a trait
lists together cover an abstract method over that trait; top-level abstract
function declarations run on neither implementation.
Since the amendment of 14~October 2026 the interpreter checks the same at load,
at each object without static parameters, reading \KWD{comprises} clauses as its
check of the Meet Rule reads them: it accepts an object whose definitions at
narrower parameter types together cover an inherited abstract method, as before,
and refuses one whose definitions do not cover it, where it accepted any one
definition at narrower parameter types before.
It does not check objects with static parameters and object expressions in
generic functions (row~665 of the revival's gap ledger), and it
refuses an object where its search through the \KWD{comprises} clauses
does not settle: a cycle, its bound of 10,000 steps or of 4,096 combinations of
parts, or a type it cannot read.
It refuses an object whose one definition of an inherited abstract method has a
static parameter of its own and covers the method, as
\EXP{\VAR{g}\llbracket{}T \KWD{extends} \TYP{Side}\rrbracket(\KWD{self}, x\COLON{}T)}
covers \EXP{\VAR{g}(\KWD{self}, x\COLON \TYP{Side})}, which the compiled type
checker accepts (row~728 of the revival's gap ledger).
The compiled type checker splits a tuple of parameter types through
\KWD{comprises} clauses as the interpreter does, and refuses where its search
meets a cycle or passes the same bounds: it accepts an object whose concrete
methods cover an inherited abstract method of two parameters only through
combinations of the types that a trait lists, as the interpreter does.
It splits no tuple where the abstract method or a concrete method below it has
static parameters of its own, and so refuses an object whose concrete methods,
one of them with a static parameter of its own, cover such a method only
through those combinations (row~725 of the revival's gap ledger).
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/types-vals-vars.tex},
lines 568--570:
\begin{quote}
because of the \KWD{comprises} clauses of $S$ and $T$ and the \KWD{excludes}
clause of $U$, any subtype of both $S$ and $T$ must be a subtype of $V$.  Thus,
$V = S \inter T$.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/functions.tex}, lines 377--378:
\begin{quote}
Furthermore, the union of the argument types of the concrete declarations for
\VAR{f} must be equal to \VAR{T}.
\end{quote}
and lines 413--415:
\begin{quote}
For the latter, the programmer must provide a definition for every immediate
subtype of \TYP{Molecule}, or it is a static error.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/components/source-code.tex}, line
429, the words of the draft note's second item that changed:
\begin{quote}
provided that all its immediate subtraits are considered to have concrete
method declaration with that signature.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/traits.tex}, lines 510--512:
\begin{quote}
An abstract method declaration declares an \emph{abstract method}; any object
inheriting an abstract method must define a body expression for the method.
\end{quote}
The effect of \secref{revival-comprises}, the revival's text of 28~September
2026, ended:
\begin{quote}
The passages that read a \KWD{comprises} clause at the level of types, the
intersection of two traits with such clauses (\secref{internal-types},
\secref{more-specific-rule}) and the definitions an abstract function
declaration requires (\secref{abstractFunctionDeclarations}), are not revised;
whether they are to be restated at the level of values is not settled.
\end{quote}
The box after the example in \secref{abstractFunctionDeclarations}, the
revival's text of 30~September 2026, ended:
\begin{quote}
The compiled type checker checks, at each object declaration, that the
object's concrete methods cover the abstract methods it inherits, reading no
\KWD{comprises} clause; top-level abstract function declarations run on neither
implementation of the revival.
\end{quote}
and the effect of this entry said the same in its words.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C: the reading of a \KWD{comprises} clause
at the level of values does not depend on the rule on instantiations.
\end{description}

\subsection{The Meet Rule for traits with comprises clauses}
\seclabel{revival-meet}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{principles-overloading};
\secref{more-specific-rule}; \secref{overloading-static-params};
\secref{overloading-coercion-resolution}; and, in a draft build, the proposed
features of the appendix ``Internal Document''.
\item[Change] Where the Meet Rule for functions, or for functional methods,
asks for a declaration on the intersection of two parameter types, declarations
whose parameter types are each a subtype of both and together cover the
intersection also satisfy it, every pair of those declarations satisfying the
rules in turn; the case does not apply to the condition on the types from which
the two parameter types define coercions.
A box at the rule answers the draft note that asks what the intersection of two
declarations is; the Meet Rule of \secref{overloading-static-params} gains the
same case, with a box.
The Meet Rule's example concludes that $V$ covers $S \inter T$, and that $f(V)$
is applicable to and more specific for any call at run time to which $f(S)$ and
$f(T)$ are applicable.
\secref{principles-overloading} says that a static call whose argument's static
type stands between a trait with a \KWD{comprises} clause and its listed types
may have several declarations applicable to it that no other applicable
declaration is more specific than, and is not a static error.
The second of the two facts of \secref{overloading-coercion-resolution} keeps a
single most specific declaration for the dynamic call and, but for such a call,
for the static call; the declaration that the dynamic call runs is the same as
or more specific than every declaration applicable to the static call, its
return type a subtype of theirs; the type of a static call whose most specific
declarations, and every declaration more specific than them that the call may
run, have no static parameters is the intersection of their return types; and a sentence gives the scope of the proof.
In the internal document, the proposed feature on the intersection of two types
with \KWD{comprises} clauses is marked done.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided
on 29~September 2026 that the Meet Rule gains the case of declarations that
together cover the intersection, and that a call whose argument's static type
stands between two traits with \KWD{comprises} clauses is typed by the
intersection of its candidates' return types, with the proof of
\appref{overloaded-functional} revised to the uniqueness of the most specific
declaration at run time (\secref{revival-proof}).
The team's list of proposed features records the same plan for the example:
``We agreed to revise the Meet rule to address this with the coverage check''.
Under the reading of \secref{trait-decls}, the intersection of two traits with
\KWD{comprises} clauses is not in general equal to a named type, and without the
case the Meet Rule would refuse the example given to illustrate it.
The case is an addition to the Meet Rule of the type group's paper of 2011,
which asks for a declaration on the meet, as the type group's calculus
Welterweight Fortress does (\nolinkurl{Papers/Welterweight/fig-meetrule.tick},
in the revival's repository).
The typing of such a call follows from the Return Type Rule: the declaration
that runs is below every most specific declaration of the static call, and its
return type below each of theirs.
For a declaration with static parameters one instance would have to meet every
one of those return types at once, which the proof does not show, so the typing
is stated for declarations without static parameters only.
The covering is a judgement of the overloading rules alone: it makes no type a
subtype of another.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-overloading/decision-record.md}
and \nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/comprises-type-level-proof-addendum.md} in
the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The Meet Rule's example is valid; both implementations refused it
before this change (row~492 of the revival's gap ledger).
A static call whose argument's static type stands between two traits with
\KWD{comprises} clauses, as an instantiation of a trait with static parameters
that extends both unlisted does, is not a static error: it runs the declaration
that covers its value, and, where those declarations and every declaration more
specific than them that the call may run have no static parameters, its type is
the intersection of the return types of its most specific declarations.
The compiled type checker departs from this in four ways: it types such a call
by the intersection only in a family none of whose declarations has static
parameters, and otherwise by one of its most specific declarations (row~543 of
the revival's gap ledger); its code generator stops at a method call on a value
of such a type (row~540); it refuses the case for declarations with static
parameters (row~546); and its verdict on a trait between the two that provides
both declarations of a functional method can depend on the components it
compiled before (row~547).
The Meet Rule for dotted methods keeps its form, asking for a declaration on the
intersection (\secref{revival-notrevised}).
\item[Original text] The Working Draft's rules are unchanged, the cases being
added after them.
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/advanced/overloading.tex}, lines 303--307,
the example's conclusion:
\begin{quote}
Because of the \KWD{comprises} clauses of $S$ and $T$ and the \KWD{excludes}
clause of $U$, any subtype of both $S$ and $T$ must be a subtype of $V$.  Thus,
$V = S \inter T$, and the declaration $f(V)$ ``disambiguates'' $f(S)$ and
$f(T)$, i.e., it is applicable to and more specific for any call to which both
$f(S)$ and $f(T)$ are applicable.
\end{quote}
and lines 460--468, the second fact and the sentence after the two:
\begin{quote}
If any declaration is applicable to a static call then there exists a single
most specific declaration that is applicable to the static call and a single
most specific declaration that is applicable to the corresponding dynamic call.

Moreover, we can prove that the most specific declaration that is applicable to
a dynamic call is more specific than the most specific declaration that is
applicable to the corresponding static call.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The proof of overloading resolution}
\seclabel{revival-proof}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{proof-overloading-resolution}.
\item[Change] The proof states its scope and its assumptions: declarations
whose parameter and return types have no static parameters, calls to which
declarations are applicable without coercion, the overloading rules, bodies
that exist and are well typed, a sound and transitive subtype relation, and
every value of a trait with a \KWD{comprises} clause a value of one of its
listed types, which the compiled type checker does not yet enforce across
components.
It states the Meet Rule as it uses it: declarations below both parameter types
that together hold every value of both, of which a declaration on the
intersection is the case of one.
Lemma~\ref{lem:le} proves only that the dynamic call has at most one most
specific declaration, taking a covering declaration where it took the
declaration on the intersection;
Theorem~\ref{thm:overloading-subtyping} proves only that the dynamic call has
exactly one when the static call has any;
Theorem~\ref{thm:dynamic-subtype-static} proves that the declaration that runs
is the same as or more specific than every declaration applicable to the static
call; and a new Theorem~\ref{thm:result-type} proves that its return type is a
subtype of the return type of every most specific declaration of the static
call.
The lemmas of existence are unchanged.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided
on 29~September 2026, with the case of the Meet Rule of \secref{revival-meet},
that the proof keeps its lemmas of existence and gives up the uniqueness of the
most specific declaration for the static call for its uniqueness for the
dynamic call, with its scope and its assumption on \KWD{comprises} clauses
marked.
Covering gives a declaration that holds the value of the argument, not one
whose parameter type is a supertype of the static type of the argument, so the
static call may have more than one most specific declaration.
The type group's paper of 2019 keeps a unique most specific declaration for
every call as a theorem (Park, Hong, Steele and Ryu, POPL 2019, Theorem~4.1);
a call whose argument's static type stands between two traits with
\KWD{comprises} clauses is outside that paper, which has no closed types, and
the revival gives up the unique most specific declaration for the static call
there on the assumptions this proof states.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/comprises-type-level-proof-addendum.md} and
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-overloading/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The appendix proves that no call is undefined or ambiguous at run
time within its scope and on its assumptions; it does not prove the soundness of
declarations with static parameters, of inference or of coercion.
Where a type declared in a later component escapes the check of a
\KWD{comprises} clause (row~487 of the revival's gap ledger), the uniqueness of
the most specific declaration for the dynamic call is not guaranteed.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/appendices/overloading-function.tex},
lines 37--48:
\begin{quote}
Below we prove: $|\Sigma| \neq 0$ implies $|\sigma| = 1$, and $|\Sigma| \neq 0$
implies $|\delta| = 1$.
Informally, if any declaration is applicable to a static call then there exists
a single most specific declaration that is applicable to the static call and a
single most specific declaration that is applicable to the corresponding
dynamic call.
\end{quote}
lines 84--135, the lemma and the two theorems this change revises:
\begin{quote}
\emph{Lemma.} $|\sigma| \le 1$.  Also, $|\delta| \le 1$.

\emph{Proof.} We prove this for $\delta$, but the case for $\sigma$ is
identical.
For the purpose of contradiction suppose there are two declarations
$\f(\Ps)$ and $\f(\Qs)$ in $\delta$.  Since both $\f(\Ps)$ and
$\f(\Qs)$ are applicable without coercion to the call $\f(\Xs)$ we
have $\Xs \Ovrsubtype \Ps \inter \Qs$.  Therefore it is not the case
that $\Ps \incompatible \Qs$.  By the overloading rules, $\Ps
\neq \Qs$ and either $\Ps \excludes \Qs$ or there is a declaration
$\f(\Ps \inter \Qs)$ visible at $Z$.  Since it cannot be the case that
$\Ps \excludes \Qs$ there must exist a declaration $\f(\Ps \inter
\Qs)$ visible at $Z$.  Since $\Ps \inter \Qs \Ovrsubtype \Ps$ and $\Ps
\inter \Qs \Ovrsubtype \Qs$ we know $\f(\Ps \inter \Qs)$ is applicable
without coercion to the call $\f(\Xs)$.  Since $\Ps \neq \Qs$ either
$\Ps \inter \Qs \strictsubtype \Ps$ or $\Ps \inter \Qs \strictsubtype
\Qs$.  Either case contradicts our assumption.

\emph{Theorem.} If $|\Sigma| \neq 0$ then $|\sigma| = 1$.  Also, if
$|\Sigma| \neq 0$ then $|\delta| = 1$.

\emph{Proof.} Follows from the three lemmas before it.

\emph{Theorem.} If $\sigma = \set{S}$ and $\delta = \set{D}$ then
$D \Ovrsubtype S$.

\emph{Proof.} If the declaration with parameter type $S$ and the declaration
with parameter type $D$ satisfy the Subtype Rule then the theorem is
proved.  Otherwise, by the definition of $\sigma$ we have $\sigma
\subseteq \Sigma$.  Therefore $S \in \Sigma$.  By the first lemma,
$S \in \Delta$.  Notice that $S,D \in \Delta$ implies
$X \Ovrsubtype S$ and $X \Ovrsubtype D$.  Therefore, $S \not \excludes
D$.  By the More Specific Rule for Functions, there must exist a
declaration with parameter type $S \inter D$.  Because $X
\Ovrsubtype (S \inter D)$, $(S \inter D) \in \Delta$.
Notice $(S \inter D) \Ovrsubtype S$ and $(S \inter D) \Ovrsubtype D$.
By the definition of $\delta$, we have $\lnot \exists D'\in\Delta: D'
\strictsubtype D$.  In particular, $(S \inter D) \not \strictsubtype
D$.  Therefore $(S \inter D) = D$.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The type of an integer numeral}
\seclabel{revival-numeral-type}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{literals}; \secref{coercion}.
\item[Change] \secref{literals} says that \library\ give every numeral without a
radix point the type \TYP{IntLiteral}, which extends \TYP{Number} and none of the
number types, and that each of the five integer types, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} and
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} declares a coercion from it.
The box of \secref{coercion} says the same of the coercion into
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, and that the interpreter does not yet give a numeral that
type.
The change of \secref{revival-numerals}, of 8~November 2026, replaced this
text: an integer numeral has the type \TYP{NaturalNumeral}, beside
\TYP{RadixPointNumeral} for a numeral with a radix point, and is converted into
its default where nothing fixes a type; this entry keeps the change of
29~September 2026, and its effect below states what of it holds since then.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided
on 29~September 2026 that a numeral has a type of its own, \TYP{IntLiteral},
from which each number type converts, the compiled path's own library's working
version taken in place of the numeral types of \secref{literals}, which no
library declares, and that \library\ take it before the interpreter does, since
the interpreter can give a numeral that type without changing the declarations
its calls reach only once it runs with the static types of the compiled type
checker.
\Library\ declared their numeral type an object extending \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32},
its arithmetic withheld by the comment ``Do not enable these until coercion is
implemented; doing so will cause all our arithmetic to occur on IntLiterals.''
(\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/LibraryBuiltin/FortressBuiltin.fss}, lines
495--496 before this change, in the revival's repository).
Coercion is implemented on both paths now, and \library\ declare the type as
the compiled path's own library does
(\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/LibraryBuiltin/CompilerBuiltin.fsi}, lines
390--431): beside the number types under \TYP{Number}, with the arithmetic that
comment withheld, each integer type declaring a coercion from it.
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}} and \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, which that library does not declare from
a numeral, coerce from it too, since \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} coerces from every integer
type (\secref{basic-rationals}) and \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} from integer numerals
(\secref{revival-int-float}).
\item[Effect] The type \TYP{IntLiteral} of this change is named
\TYP{NaturalNumeral} in \library\ since the change of \secref{revival-numerals};
the compiled path's own library keeps the name \TYP{IntLiteral}.
The compiled type checker converts an integer numeral to the
integer type, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} or \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} that its context expects,
\EXP{\mathbb{N}32} and \EXP{\mathbb{N}64} among them, which refused a numeral
before.
A numeral beside a value of an integer type is converted to that type where no
declaration of the operator is applicable to the two without coercion, and that
type's own declaration of the operator is then the most specific
(\secref{resolving-coercion}); \library\ state on each integer type the
comparisons \EXP{<}, \EXP{\leq}, \EXP{>} and \EXP{\geq}, \OPR{CMP},
\OPR{MAXNUM}, \OPR{MINNUM} and a power with a numeral exponent, which the types
inherited before from a declaration that a converted numeral leaves tied with
those of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} and \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}.
Equality is stated in pairs instead: each integer type declares an \EXP{=}
whose parameter is of type \TYP{NaturalNumeral}, and \TYP{NaturalNumeral} one whose
parameter is of each integer type, and each converts the numeral by its
coercion into the integer type and compares by that type's own \EXP{=}
(\secref{basic-rationals} gives the pairs of the other number types).
Inequality is not stated in pairs: the top-level declaration of \EXP{\neq},
whose parameters are of type \TYP{Any}, is applicable to an integer value and a
numeral without coercion, and since the change of \secref{revival-numerals} it
receives the numeral at its default, which it compares with the integer value by
\EXP{=}; so \EXP{u \neq 0} is \VAR{true} for a zero \VAR{u} of \EXP{\mathbb{N}32}
or \EXP{\mathbb{N}64}, neither of which declares \EXP{=} with
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, as the interpreter answered before, a departure from
\secref{operator-NE} that the effect of \secref{revival-numerals} names.
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}} states in its api, for the same reason, the additive and
multiplicative operators its body declares.
\TYP{IntLiteral} declared its own \VAR{zero}, \VAR{one}, \VAR{even},
\VAR{odd}, \VAR{floor}, \VAR{ceiling}, \VAR{truncate}, \OPR{DIVIDES},
\OPR{TIMES} and a power with a numeral exponent; \TYP{NaturalNumeral} declares
none of them, since an expression of numerals alone is folded and a numeral
reaches those of its default type (\secref{revival-numerals}).
A numeral given where any integer type is accepted, as the count of a shift,
is converted to the narrowest of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} and
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}} that holds its value, as \secref{numeral-default} reads a
numeral whose conversions tie.
The interpreter converts an integer numeral where its place fixes a type, and
gives it its default elsewhere (\secref{revival-numerals}).
Its calls of the comparisons, \OPR{CMP}, \OPR{MAXNUM} and \OPR{MINNUM} on an
integer type reach that type's own declarations, which answer as the
declarations inherited before did, except that \OPR{MAXNUM} and \OPR{MINNUM}
with a converted argument, which stopped at a tie with those of
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}} or answered at \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, now answer at the integer
type both arguments convert to.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/literals.tex},
lines 146--148, which stand unchanged and are now followed by the new
sentence:
\begin{quote}
\Library\ define coercions
from numerals to integers (for simple numerals)
and rational numbers (for compound numerals).
\end{quote}
The box of \secref{coercion} is the revival's own text of 26~September 2026,
not the Working Draft's; it said, in
\nolinkurl{Specification/basic/conversions-coercions.tex}, lines 84--85,
before this revision:
\begin{quote}
The interpreter gives an integer numeral outside the range of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}
a wider integer type, and so does not yet convert it.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The type of a varargs parameter}
\seclabel{revival-varargs}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{function-decls}.
\item[Change] A box after the sentence of \secref{function-decls} that gives a
varargs parameter the type \EXP{\TYP{HeapSequence}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket}
says that no library declares that type, nor the immutable sequence that the
team's draft note at the start of \secref{function-app} agreed on in its place;
that both implementations give the parameter the array type
\EXP{\TYP{ImmutableArray}\llbracket{}T, \mathbb{Z}32\rrbracket} of \library;
and that the compiled path's own library declares no such type.
The normative text is unchanged.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 23~September 2026 that the implementers' later word
outweighs text that the designers left unfinished.
The text and its draft notes name \TYP{HeapSequence} and
\TYP{ReadOnlyHeapSequence} (``We agreed to implement varargs tuples using
\TYP{ReadOnlyHeapSequence} which is immutable and invariant.
We'll add \TYP{MakeReadOnlyHeapSequence} into the core library.''), and no
library declares either.
The authors of the compiled type checker named a type for the parameter,
\EXP{\TYP{ImmutableArray}\llbracket{}T, \mathbb{Z}32\rrbracket}, with the
comment ``Replace ImmutableArray with ImmutableHeapSequence when
ImmutableHeapSequence is put into the libraries''
(\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/src/com/sun/fortress/compiler/Types.java}, lines
47--49, in the revival's repository), and the interpreter binds the parameter
to an immutable one-dimensional array of the arguments that the factory
\VAR{\_\_immutableFactory1} of \library\ builds
(\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/src/com/sun/fortress/interpreter/evaluator/values/NonPrimitive.java},
lines 224--251), a value of that type.
The type is immutable, as the draft note asks.
Before this change the compiled type checker typed the parameter in the body
as \VAR{T}, the type of one argument, and read a parameter list with plain
bindings before a varargs binding as if the varargs binding were one more plain
binding, so that a call that passed no argument, or more than one, for the
varargs binding after plain bindings was refused (row~604 of the revival's gap
ledger).
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-checker-defects/REPORT.md} in the
revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The compiled type checker applies a declaration with a varargs
parameter to a call as \secref{function-app} and
\secref{applicability-with-special-params} say, whatever the number of
arguments for the varargs binding, none among them, each a subtype of the type
of the binding or convertible to it; and it types the parameter in the body at
\EXP{\TYP{ImmutableArray}\llbracket{}T, \mathbb{Z}32\rrbracket} of \library,
where it typed it as \VAR{T}.
Under the compiled path's own library, which declares no \TYP{ImmutableArray},
it refuses every use of a varargs parameter, in a body, a contract or a
function expression, naming the type.
The code generator does not yet compile a function with a varargs parameter,
so no program with one runs on the compiled path.
The interpreter is unchanged.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/functions.tex},
lines 178--180, which stand unchanged and are now followed, after the draft
note that refers to the library section for \TYP{HeapSequence}, by the box:
\begin{quote}
The type of a varargs parameter
is \EXP{\TYP{HeapSequence}\llbracket{}T\rrbracket} where \VAR{T} is
the type mentioned in (or inferred for) that binding.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The type of a parameter that a declaration leaves out}
\seclabel{revival-paramtype}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{type-inference-components}.
\item[Change] One box after the paragraph of \secref{type-inference-components}
that says that type inference is performed over all program constructs that
still include elided types; the paragraph is unchanged.
The box says that the compiled type checker does not yet infer the type of a
parameter that a function declaration leaves out, at top level, in a trait or
object, or local to an expression block, and refuses the component with the
message ``Missing parameter type for'' followed by the parameter's name; and
that the inference the paragraph asks for is not yet described.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 8~October 2026 that a local function whose parameter
type is left out is refused with a message, as a top-level function is,
instead of stopping the compiled type checker.
The inference the paragraph asks for is not described:
\chapref{type-inference} names the inference over a whole program of the types
that declarations elide among what it does not yet describe.
Before this change the compiled type checker refused such a parameter of a
top-level function or a method with that message, which no passage stated
(row~405 of the revival's gap ledger), and stopped on such a parameter of a
local function, with ``Type is not inferred'', ``TryChecker returned an untyped
expr'' or ``Result of typechecking still contains intermediate nodes'' (rows~620
to 622).
A stop of the checker is no answer that the text gives; the refusal names the
parameter, and departs from the text as the refusal at top level already did.
Inferring the types, from the uses of the parameter or from an arrow type that
the context expects, is the repair, which waits until the text describes the
inference.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-checker-overloading/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The compiled path refuses a component that leaves out the type
of a parameter of a function declaration other than the self parameter of a
method.
Among them is a keyword parameter, whose type \secref{function-decls} infers
from its default expression; the compiled path does not yet read a keyword
parameter as one, and the interpreter stops on a call that leaves out its
argument (row~652 of the revival's gap ledger).
The parameter of a function expression is not affected: where the context
expects an arrow type, the compiled type checker takes the parameter's type
from it.
The interpreter, which has no static types, runs a top-level or local function
whose parameter type is left out, and stops on a method that leaves it out and
implements an abstract declaration (row~405).
\item[Original text] The passage is unchanged:
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/components/type-inference.tex},
lines 44--46:
\begin{quote}
Once $C$ is expanded, type inference
is performed over all program constructs that still include elided types.
Empty bodies are ignored.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The rounding of an infinite or indefinite rational}
\seclabel{revival-rational-rounding}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{basic-rationals}.
\item[Change] The sentence after the method entries of \VAR{floor}, \VAR{ceiling},
\VAR{round} and \VAR{truncate} and of the enclosing operators
\EXP{\lfloor\ldots\rfloor} and \EXP{\lceil\ldots\rceil} said that all of these
methods simply return the argument if it is \EXP{+\infty}, \EXP{-\infty}, or
\EXP{0/0}; it now says that they throw a \TYP{DivisionByZero} there, none of
these values having an integer value, as a division by zero whose result is an
integer does (\chapref{operators}).
The entries' result type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} is unchanged.
The sentence's opening words, ``For types \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} and
\EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}},'', left it before (\secref{revival-rationals}).
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 9~October 2026 that the declared integer result and the
peers win over the sentence.
The method entries declare the result \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, and \library\ declare
it too; a method that returned its argument at \EXP{+\infty}, \EXP{-\infty} or
\EXP{0/0} returned a rational number where it declares an integer, and the
compiled type checker refused the two bodies of \library\ that did so (row~635
of the revival's gap ledger).
A rational number with the denominator zero is the quotient of a division by
zero, and a division by zero whose result is an integer throws a
\TYP{DivisionByZero} (\chapref{operators}); the Working Draft of February 2011 threw the same
exception when a value \EXP{1/0} or \EXP{-1/0} was assigned to a variable of the
rational type without the infinities (\secref{revival-literals}).
Among the peers, Python's exact rational type refuses a zero denominator with a
\texttt{ZeroDivisionError}, and its floor, ceiling and truncation of a
floating-point infinity or NaN raise an error instead of returning a value.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-library-slips/REPORT.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] \Library\ throw \TYP{DivisionByZero} from \VAR{ceiling} and
\VAR{truncate} at these three values, and so from \VAR{floor}, \VAR{round} and
the two enclosing operators, which reach them; the interpreter runs them so.
Before this change it returned the argument from all of them but \VAR{round},
which stopped the program there.
The compiled path's own library, used until the one library replaces it,
declares no rational type.
The floating-point types' rounding methods are not affected; which of them
keep the floating-point type, and what they return at an infinity or a NaN,
is row~330 of the revival's gap ledger.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/numbers.tex},
lines 479--481:
\begin{quote}
For types \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^*} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}^{\#}},
all of these methods simply return the argument if it is
\EXP{+\infty}, \EXP{-\infty}, or \EXP{0/0}.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
A return to the original sentence would need the six methods and operators to
declare a rational result, against their entries' \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, or would
leave them returning a value outside the type they declare.
