\end{description}

\subsection{Which objects are value objects}
\seclabel{revival-object-kinds}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] The opening section of \chapref{objects}, its list of
the kinds of objects.
\item[Change] Its sentence on the value objects named booleans, numeric
literals, IEEE floating-point numbers and integers; it now names integers of
every precision and rational numbers too.
Its sentence on the reference objects named arbitrary-precision numbers and
aggregates such as arrays, lists and sets; it now names strings and the
aggregates.
The passage gains one box.
The rule of strict equivalence (\secref{equivalence}) is unchanged: values of
different types are never strictly equivalent, value objects of one type are
when their fields are, a binary word by its bit pattern, and reference objects
only when they are one object.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows two
decisions of the revival.
By the first, of 6~November 2026, the values of the arbitrary-precision integer
and rational types are value objects: \library\ declare the arbitrary-precision
integer as a value object, and the sentence's own list called integers value
objects and arbitrary-precision numbers reference objects, so the text
disagreed with itself and with the library; the library's declaration is the
deliberate word.
By the second, of 6 and 7~November 2026, strictly equivalent means the rule of
\secref{equivalence} on both implementations, with no special case for any type,
and strings are reference objects: \library\ declare every kind of string as an
object without the \KWD{value} modifier, so two strings are strictly equivalent
only when they are one object, and their text equality is their own \EXP{=}.
The Working Draft of February 2011 named no kind for strings, and the
team's implementations compared two flat strings by their text and two ropes
by identity.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/any-carrier-2026-11-06.md}, section~2.4, and
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/string-equivalence-2026-11-06.md} in the
revival's repository.
\item[Effect] Both implementations answer \EXP{\sequiv} by the rule, and both
read the \KWD{value} modifier.
\Library\ declare the rational type's object and the two numeral types,
\TYP{NaturalNumeral} and \TYP{RadixPointNumeral}, as value objects.
A numeral is converted where it stands on both implementations
(\secref{revival-numerals}), so a floating-point numeral bound to a variable of
type \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} is strictly equivalent to a computed \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}
value of the same bits, and \EXP{x \sequiv z} is \VAR{true} for \EXP{x = 3} and
\EXP{z\COLON\mathbb{Z}32 = 3} on both.
Two string literals of one text are one object (\secref{revival-string-literals}),
so \EXP{\hbox{\rm``\STR{ab}''} \sequiv \hbox{\rm``\STR{ab}''}} is \VAR{true}, and
a string built as a program runs is not strictly equivalent to an equal literal
unless the operation that builds it answers an existing string.
The departures:
the compiled path's own library, used until the one library replaces it,
answers \EXP{\sequiv} on values of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64},
\EXP{\mathbb{R}32} and \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, on strings and on booleans by
\EXP{=} before it
reaches the rule, so there two equal strings built apart are strictly
equivalent and \EXP{0.0} is strictly equivalent to \EXP{-0.0} (row~908 of the
revival's gap ledger);
and \library\ declare \EXP{=} on pairs and triples only, so two tuples of four
or more elements are equal only when they are strictly equivalent, and two such
tuples of equal strings built apart are not equal (row~923 of the revival's
gap ledger).
The answers are gated by \nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/StrictEquivalenceRuleWalk.fss},
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/ValueObjectCC64Walk.fss} and, for the compiled
helper, by \nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/compiler_tests/StrictEquivalenceRuleCompiled.fss}.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/objects.tex},
lines 39--43 and 52--54:
\begin{quote}
Most objects with simple standard types,
such as booleans,
numeric literals,
IEEE floating-point numbers,
and integers are value objects.
\end{quote}
\begin{quote}
Reference objects include
arbitrary-precision numbers
and aggregates such as arrays, lists and sets.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
A return to the original sentences would need \library\ to declare the
arbitrary-precision integer and the rational object without the \KWD{value}
modifier, so that two of their values built apart are never strictly
equivalent, and would leave the kind of strings unnamed.
\end{description}

\subsection{When two sizes are the same}
\seclabel{revival-sizes}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \chapref{types}.
\item[Change] Two sentences and a box after the sentence of \chapref{types}
on identical types, which is unchanged.
They say that two \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} static arguments are identical if
and only if they are written alike once each operation whose operands are
numerals is replaced by its value, so that \EXP{2\;3} is \EXP{6} and
\EXP{s_{0}\;s_{1}} is not \EXP{s_{1}\;s_{0}}; that two such arguments are known
to differ when they are two different numerals; and that a static parameter,
or an expression in which one occurs, is not known to differ from a numeral.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 9~October 2026 on arithmetic in a size.
The text allows a sum, a difference, a product or a power as a static
argument (\appref{concrete-syntax}) and says that its value is determined
once the static parameters in it are instantiated (\secref{static-expr}),
but it never said when two such arguments are identical: the sentence on
identical types says only that their static arguments are, beside the team's
note that it ``isn't complete in any case''.
\library\ write a product in the size of the storage of a matrix
(\EXP{\VAR{mem}\COLON\TYP{PrimitiveArray}\llbracket{}T, (s_{0}\;s_{1})\rrbracket})
and sums in result types; written forms compared once numerals are folded
accept what they write, each product written once, with no solver of
arithmetic, which nothing in them needs.
The team's own questions and answers on overloaded operators, in the appendix
``Internal Document'', refuse \EXP{f(x\COLON T\llbracket{}n+1\rrbracket)}
beside \EXP{f(x\COLON T\llbracket{}0\rrbracket)}, ``now that \EXP{n+1} is
necessarily nonzero'', and allow \EXP{T\llbracket{}1\rrbracket} beside
\EXP{T\llbracket{}0\rrbracket}: a numeral differs from another numeral, and an
expression with a static parameter in it is not known to differ from a numeral.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-size-expressions/REPORT.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The compiled type checker accepts a sum, a difference, a product
or a power in a size, computes each operation whose operands are numerals,
checks the result against the parameter's kind as it checks a numeral, and
compares sizes as the new sentences say; a power of a base other than 0, 1
and $-1$ at an exponent above 4096 it does not compute, and refuses as outside
every kind; a clause
\EXP{N\llbracket{}0\rrbracket} of a \KWD{typecase} on
\EXP{N\llbracket{}b_{0}\rrbracket} is reachable.
It still counts two different static parameters as different sizes, which the
text does not say (row~683 of the revival's gap ledger).
It infers a static argument written with a static parameter in it: a call of
\EXP{\VAR{get}\llbracket\KWD{int}\;i\rrbracket(b\COLON\TYP{IBox}\llbracket{}i\rrbracket)}
with an argument of type \EXP{\TYP{IBox}\llbracket{}i-1\rrbracket} instantiates
\VAR{get} at \EXP{i-1}.
Where a parameter type writes a static parameter of the callee inside an
operation, neither it nor the interpreter infers that parameter: a call of
\EXP{\VAR{getp}\llbracket\KWD{int}\;i\rrbracket(b\COLON\TYP{IBox}\llbracket{}i+1\rrbracket)}
with an argument of type \EXP{\TYP{IBox}\llbracket{}3\rrbracket} is refused, and
under the interpreter fails, unless it writes
\EXP{\VAR{getp}\llbracket{}2\rrbracket}, though the instance at 2 has the
argument's type (row~767).
The compiled path computes a size when it names a class, an operation on
numerals when the program is compiled and the rest when the class is first
loaded, so that \EXP{\TYP{Box}\llbracket{}2+1\rrbracket} and
\EXP{\TYP{Box}\llbracket{}3\rrbracket} are one class; it does not check a size
computed at that time against its parameter's kind, and makes a size beyond
\EXP{\mathbb{N}32} that the interpreter refuses (row~675 of the revival's
gap ledger).
The interpreter computes a sum, a difference, a product and a power exactly
when it instantiates a type, refuses a power at a negative exponent, and
refuses a size outside its kind.
\item[Original text] The passage is unchanged:
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/types-vals-vars.tex}, lines 51--52:
\begin{quote}
Two types are identical if and only if they are the same kind and
their names and static arguments (if any) are identical.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
A return to the text before this change would leave the identity of two size
expressions unsaid, as the compiled type checker read it before 9~October
2026: a size with arithmetic in it refused, and a static parameter counted as
different from every numeral.
\end{description}

\subsection{The type of \KWD{self} in a trait with a self-type parameter}
\seclabel{revival-self-type}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{idn-ref}; \secref{trait-decls}; \secref{methods}.
\item[Change] In \secref{idn-ref}, a sentence after the one on the self-type
idiom: in a declaration of a trait \VAR{T} with no \KWD{comprises} clause, or one that lists only ``\EXP{\ldots}'', and a
self-type parameter \VAR{X}, \KWD{self} has the type \EXP{T \cap X} in the bodies of
the trait's methods, and the \KWD{self} parameter keeps the type \VAR{T} in the
signature of each method declaration.
In \secref{trait-decls}, a paragraph after the one on \KWD{comprises} clauses
defines a self-type parameter: a static parameter of a trait with no
\KWD{comprises} clause, or one that lists only ``\EXP{\ldots}'', that is bounded by the trait at its own static
parameters, or by an instantiation of a trait with a self-type parameter that the
trait also lists in its \KWD{extends} clause, with that static parameter as the
static argument for the self-type parameter.
The paragraph requires every trait or object declaration and every object
expression that extends an instantiation of such a trait to be a subtype of
the static argument of that instantiation for the self-type parameter, a trait
standing as the type of \KWD{self} in its bodies, the intersection of its type with the types
its \KWD{comprises} clause lists or with its own self-type parameter, and gives an example marked ``Not allowed''.
In \secref{methods}, a box after the sentence that a functional method's
\KWD{self} parameter implicitly has the type of the enclosing trait or object
says that the sentence still holds of the declaration's signature.
Each of the three passages has a box.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 13~October 2026 on the bodies of the self-typed traits,
and its second form of a self-type parameter, through a trait that the trait
extends, follows a default taken under that decision on 14~October 2026.
\library\ declare their orders and their algebra as traits whose static
parameter is bounded by the trait itself, as in
\EXP{\KWD{trait}\;\TYP{Integral}\llbracket{}I \KWD{extends} \TYP{Integral}\llbracket{}I\rrbracket\rrbracket},
and give them default bodies that return \KWD{self} where that parameter is
declared, or pass it where the parameter is expected: the team's minimal
complete definitions, such as \OPR{MIN} on \TYP{StandardTotalOrder} and
\VAR{floor} on \TYP{Integral}.
The interpreter runs them; the compiled type checker refused 29 of them,
since it typed \KWD{self} as the trait.
A bound constrains the static argument, not \KWD{self}: an object may extend
\EXP{\TYP{Integral}\llbracket\mathbb{Z}32\rrbracket} without being a
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and a body that treats \KWD{self} as the parameter is sound
only if every declaration that extends the trait is a subtype of its static
argument for that parameter.
Scala states that rule for its declared self types (``The self type of a class
or object must conform to the self types of all classes which are inherited by
the template'', \emph{Scala Language Specification 2.13}, section 5.1); the revival reads the self type from the bound, which \library\
already write, as the team's compiled type checker reads it from a
\KWD{comprises} clause (\secref{idn-ref}), a reading that the team's gated test
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/compiler_tests/XXX10k.test} pins.
Naden's account of the Return Type Rule (2012) asked that the self-type idiom
become ``a separate construct which cannot be used as a first class type, but
instead only in bounds''
(\nolinkurl{Papers/Types/journal/justificationOfRTR.tex}, lines 626--630).
The signatures keep the type of the trait because the type group's account of
overloading of 2019 (Park, Hong, Steele and Ryu, POPL 2019) assumes, for the
completeness of its matching of types, that no declared type holds a type
parameter inside an intersection (pp.~11:21--22), and its Return Type Rule
compares declared domains (p.~11:13): with the intersection in the signatures,
the domains of the two declarations of \OPR{CMP} on \TYP{StandardPartialOrder}
and \TYP{StandardTotalOrder} became equivalent, and the compiled type checker
reported 18 errors of the Return Type Rule that the declarations do not make.
The test by which the compiled type checker's overloading check decides whether
the parameter types of two declarations exclude each other reads the \KWD{self}
parameter of a functional method of such a trait as \EXP{T \cap X} all the same,
following the revival's decision of 23~October 2026: every declaration that
extends an instantiation of \VAR{T} is a subtype of its static argument for
\VAR{X}, so every value that the method takes as \KWD{self} is an \VAR{X}, and
the two reasons above bear on which declaration is more specific and on the
Return Type Rule, not on whether a value lies in both domains.
With the overloads of symbolic operators checked, the plain trait type in that
test, beside an existential reduction that read no bounds
(\secref{revival-overloading}), made the compiled type checker refuse 8 pairs of
\library\ between the $+$ and $-$ of \TYP{AdditiveGroup}, which \TYP{Vector}
and \TYP{Matrix} extend, and the operators between an array and a number, though
no call fits both: a vector's own $+$ takes two vectors, the other an array and
a number, and arrays exclude numbers.
The second form of a self-type parameter keeps valid as written the mixins of
\library\ that the team wrote in 2009, \TYP{ContainmentBySubset} and
\TYP{ContainmentByComparison}, whose static parameter is bounded by the trait
\TYP{Containment}, which they extend at it.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/self-typed-bodies-judgement.md} and
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-self-type/decision-record.md}, and for
the test of exclusion in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-self-type-exclusion/REPORT.md}, in the
revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The compiled type checker types \KWD{self} as \EXP{T \cap X} in
the bodies of the dotted and functional methods of a trait with a self
parameter \VAR{X}, keeps \VAR{T} in the signatures that overloading, the Return
Type Rule and the choice of a declaration at a call read, except that the test
by which its overloading check decides whether two declarations' parameter types
exclude each other reads the \KWD{self} parameter of a functional method as
\EXP{T \cap X} where the self type comes from the bound, and refuses a trait
or object declaration or an object expression that the new paragraph of
\secref{trait-decls} does not allow, with the message
``\VAR{D} extends \EXP{C\llbracket{}A\rrbracket}, whose self parameter is \VAR{A},
but \VAR{D} is not a subtype of \VAR{A}.''.
The interpreter has no static types: it runs the bodies as before, and loads a
declaration that the paragraph refuses (row~893 of the revival's gap ledger).
The code generator compiles a default body that returns \KWD{self} as the self
parameter with no conversion to the type of the instance, so a program that
runs such a body, which the compiled type checker now accepts, with the parameter at an object type
fails the JVM's verification when the body's class is loaded (row~695 of the revival's gap
ledger).
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/var-ref.tex},
lines 53--54 and 64--69, which stand unchanged and are now followed by the new
sentence:
\begin{quote}
The type of \KWD{self} is the type of the trait or object being declared by
the innermost enclosing trait or object declaration or object expression.
\end{quote}
and
\begin{quote}
If the innermost enclosing such construct is a trait declaration (for a trait
called, say, `\EXP{T}'), and that trait declaration has a \KWD{comprises}
clause, and `\EXP{U_1}', `\EXP{U_2}', ..., `\EXP{U_n}' are the traits mentioned
in the \KWD{comprises} clause, then the type of \KWD{self} is instead
``\EXP{T \cap (U_{\mathrm{1}} \cup U_{\mathrm{2}} \cup \ldots \cup U_{\mathrm{n}})}''.
\end{quote}
The paragraph of \secref{trait-decls} is new.
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/traits.tex}, line 486, the end of the
sentence of \secref{methods} that the new box follows, stands unchanged:
\begin{quote}
and implicitly has the type of the enclosing trait or object.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] Under route~C a self-typed trait is read by a \KWD{comprises}
clause that lists its static parameter, as the compiled path's own library
declares its orders (\nolinkurl{Library/CompilerAlgebra.fsi}, lines 16 and 24,
in the revival's repository); under such a clause the self-type idiom of
\secref{idn-ref} types \KWD{self}, and this rule is not reached.
A return to that form would need the clause on the self-typed traits of
\library, which would also close each of them to its static parameter; a way
for the interpreter past its stack overflow on a clause that lists a static
parameter (row~407 of the revival's gap ledger); and leave in
\secref{trait-decls} for a static parameter as a listed type, which the type
group's calculus of 2012 does not admit.
\end{description}

\subsection{The index type of a subscript}
\seclabel{revival-index}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{type-relations}; \secref{trait-types};
\secref{tuple-types}; \secref{trait-decls}; \secref{overview-objects-traits};
\chapref{lib:object}; and, in a draft build, the item on
array indexing among the proposed features of the appendix ``Internal
Document''.
\item[Change] \library\ declare the trait \TYP{Index},
\EXP{\KWD{trait}\:\TYP{Index} \KWD{extends}\:\TYP{Any}}, which
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} extends and \TYP{Range} excludes, and bound every index
parameter of their generic indexed types by it,
\EXP{I \KWD{extends}\:\TYP{Index}}: \TYP{Indexed}, its delegated and mutable
forms, the array traits, the full ranges, and the mapped, reversed and
index-value views over an indexed generator.
\TYP{BoundedRange}, whose index type stays unbounded since the open range
\EXP{(:)} is a range over \TYP{Any}, keeps its narrowed \VAR{atMost}, whose
result is a full range, under the method condition
\EXP{\KWD{where} \{ I \KWD{extends}\:\TYP{Index} \}}.
In \secref{tuple-types}, a sentence says that a tuple type whose element types
are all subtypes of \TYP{Index} is a subtype of \TYP{Index}, and no subtype of
\TYP{Object}; the sentence on the exclusion of non-tuple types names
\TYP{Index} beside \TYP{Any}, and says that a tuple type excludes \TYP{Index}
if and only if one of its element types excludes \TYP{Index}.
In \secref{trait-types}, the sentence that every trait type excludes every
tuple type excepts \TYP{Index}.
In \secref{type-relations}, the immediate subtypes of \TYP{Any} include
\TYP{Index}.
In \secref{trait-decls}, the sentence on the implicit extension of
\TYP{Object} names \TYP{Index} beside \TYP{Any} and \TYP{Object}, a sentence
says that a trait that extends \TYP{Index} and no other trait but \TYP{Any}
implicitly extends \TYP{Object} too, and the hierarchy is rooted at
\TYP{Object} and \TYP{Index}.
In \chapref{lib:object}, the immediate subtypes of the trait \TYP{Any} include
\TYP{Index}.
In the same chapter, the sentence that every trait other than \TYP{Any} and
\TYP{Object} extends \TYP{Object}, which is the revival's
(\secref{revival-object}), names \TYP{Index} beside them.
In \secref{overview-objects-traits}, the hierarchy of traits is rooted at the
traits \TYP{Object} and \TYP{Index}.
The item on array indexing among the proposed features gains a box, and so
does each changed passage.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 23~October 2026 on the index type of \library's
subscripts, and its place of \TYP{Index} outside \TYP{Object} and its
\VAR{atMost} follow the revival's answers of the same day.
The bound finishes the team's wish.
Their item on array indexing calls \TYP{Indexed}'s two subscripts, one over an
index and one over a range of indices, ``not really a valid overloading'', and
goes on: ``We want to say that the type variable \VAR{I} extends a type, say,
\TYP{Index} which either excludes \EXP{\TYP{Range}\llbracket{}I\rrbracket} or is
a supertype of \EXP{\TYP{Range}\llbracket{}I\rrbracket}''
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/appendices/future.tex}, lines 200--203).
With \VAR{I} bounded by an \TYP{Index} that \TYP{Range} excludes, the two
subscripts never apply to one value, and the pair is a valid overloading at
every instance (\secref{overloading-static-params}); unbounded, at the instance
\TYP{Any} the range subscript is the more specific, and its result is no
element, which the Return Type Rule refuses.
The compiled type checker, made to check the overloading of symbolic operators
over \library, reported 124 such errors in the generic indexed types, and none
at the bound with the rule below.
The tuple rule is the revival's departure from the designers' text on tuple
types.
The team named the obstacle themselves: ``However, we cannot say that because
when we invoke that subscripting method, we write \EXP{a[1, 2, 3]} where the
arguments are passed as a tuple, \EXP{(1, 2, 3)}. If we want to use
\TYP{Index}, we need a magic to convert that tuple argument expression to
something of type \TYP{Index}.'' (lines 204--207).
They asked for a conversion, and a conversion would change every index value
that a subscript passes; the rule makes the tuple type a subtype of
\TYP{Index} instead, a typing rule that changes no value.
The Working Draft's text on tuple types forbids it (quoted below), and so does
the team's later Types chapter, twice, its macros for the void and bottom types
written out: ``Every trait type excludes \TYP{()}, \TYP{BottomType} and every
tuple type.'' and ``A tuple type excludes \TYP{BottomType}, \TYP{()}, and every
trait type, arrow type or function type.'' (Luchangco, 2012,
\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/types.tick}, lines 341
and 424, in the revival's repository).
\TYP{Index} stands outside \TYP{Object}, as \TYP{Any} and \TYP{Object} do, so
that a tuple of indices is no \TYP{Object} (\secref{revival-bound}); the
specification's library declares its trait of all tuples so too,
\EXP{\KWD{trait}\:\TYP{Tuple} \KWD{extends}\:\TYP{Any}
\KWD{excludes} \{\,\TYP{Object}\,\}} (\secref{core-tuple}).
Two ways stay open, each with its own cost: a constraint
\EXP{\KWD{where} \{ I \KWD{excludes}\:\TYP{Range}\llbracket{}I\rrbracket \}},
which the team left as an open question (the item ``Type variables in
\KWD{excludes} clauses'' of the same appendix) and which is not among the
Working Draft's forms of constraint; and subscripts by a range only at the
index types that \library\ fix, as the arrays of rank one to three, the strings,
\TYP{Just} and \TYP{Nothing} already have them, at the price of slicing in the
generic views.
A reversal would remove the rule, the bound and \TYP{Index}, and leave the 124
errors standing, or take one of the two ways.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-index-bound/decision-record.md} in
the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The interpreter and the compiled type checker, when it checks
\library, read the rule and the exclusion by the qualified name of
\library's trait, so a program's own trait named \TYP{Index} is not read as
it, and both keep \TYP{Index} outside \TYP{Object}; a program with an index
type of its own declares it an \TYP{Index}.
A program's own generic declaration that instantiates an indexed type of
\library\ at its own type parameter bounds that parameter by \TYP{Index}; the
compiled type checker refuses it unbounded, ``The static argument \VAR{I} does
not satisfy the corresponding bound \TYP{Index}'', and the interpreter runs
it.
The compiled type checker refuses a tuple expression of indices passed to a
function whose one parameter's type is \TYP{Index}, as \EXP{f(i, j)} for
\EXP{f(x\COLON \TYP{Index})}, though it takes a variable of the tuple type
(row~805 of the revival's gap ledger); the interpreter runs it.
A numeral at a parameter of type \TYP{Index}, a type that declares no
coercion from the numeral's type, takes its default where the default is an
\TYP{Index}: the compiled type checker, checking against \library, and the
interpreter pass \EXP{f(3)} for \EXP{f(x\COLON \TYP{Index})} a
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} (\secref{revival-numeral-type}).
The interpreter loads a function over \TYP{Index} beside a function over a
tuple type whose element types are neither subtypes of \TYP{Index} nor exclude
it, as \EXP{h(x\COLON \TYP{Index})} beside
\EXP{h(x\COLON (\TYP{Foo}, \TYP{Foo}))} for a trait \TYP{Foo}, which the Meet
Rule refuses (\secref{more-specific-rule}) and the compiled type checker
refuses (row~806 of the revival's gap ledger).
The interpreter refuses a program's own type named \TYP{Index} as ambiguous
with \library's, as it refuses one named \TYP{Indexed}.
The compiled path still compiles programs against its own library, which
declares no \TYP{Index}, until it is switched over to the interpreter's.
The code generator no longer refuses a method with a \KWD{where} clause of
its own, so after the switch-over neither \TYP{BoundedRange}'s \VAR{atMost}
nor the two \VAR{distribute} methods of \library's sum reduction stops at that
refusal; no compiled program reaches \library\ before then.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/types-vals-vars.tex},
lines 134--136, 215--216 and 272--273, which the change revises:
\begin{quote}
We say that a value is \emph{an instance of} its runtime type and of every
supertype of its runtime type; immediate subtypes of \TYP{Any} comprises of
tuple types, arrow types, \TYP{()}, and \TYP{Object}.
\end{quote}
\begin{quote}
Every trait type also excludes every arrow type and every tuple type, and the
special types \TYP{()} and \TYP{BottomType}.
\end{quote}
\begin{quote}
A tuple type excludes any non-tuple type other than \TYP{Any}.
\end{quote}
lines 263--265, which stand unchanged and are now followed by the rule:
\begin{quote}
Every tuple type is a subtype of \TYP{Any}. No other type encompasses all tuple
types. Tuple types cannot be extended by trait types.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/traits.tex}, lines 181--183 and
193--194, which the change revises:
\begin{quote}
In addition, every trait except \TYP{Any} and \TYP{Object} implicitly extends
the trait \TYP{Object} if it does not do so explicitly.
\end{quote}
\begin{quote}
This relation must form an acyclic hierarchy rooted at trait \TYP{Object}.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic-lib/objects.tex}, lines 19--20, which
the change revises:
\begin{quote}
The immediate subtypes of the trait \TYP{Any} are \TYP{Object}, \TYP{Tuple},
and \TYP{()}.
\end{quote}
and \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/preliminaries/overview.tex}, lines
1061--1062, which the change revises:
\begin{quote}
Traits in Fortress exist in a multiple inheritance hierarchy rooted at trait
\TYP{Object}.
\end{quote}
The sentence of \chapref{lib:object} on the traits that do not extend
\TYP{Object} has no counterpart in the Working Draft's chapter; it restates
the sentence of \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/traits.tex}, lines
181--183, quoted above.
The box in the appendix of proposed features is new.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{An abstract method of an exported trait}
\seclabel{revival-export-abstract}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{export-statements}.
\item[Change] The rule by which a trait or object declaration of a component
satisfies an exported one asks, beside a satisfying declaration for each field
declaration and non-abstract method declaration of the exported declaration, a
method declaration for each abstract method declaration of it, with the same
header apart from the modifier \KWD{abstract}, with a body or without one.
The sentence that allows a concrete declaration for an abstract method stands.
The passage gains a box.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 25~October 2026, which the revival confirmed against this
passage on 29~October 2026, that a method that an \apiN\ declares abstract is
declared in the component that exports the \apiN.
The decision takes the type group's later work over this text, as the revival
does wherever the two conflict.
The compiler's own library declares, in its \apiN, the abstract methods of its
trait \TYP{GeneratorZZ32}, such as \VAR{loop}, and restates each of them in its
component without a body
(\nolinkurl{Library/CompilerLibrary.fsi}, lines 110--120, and
\nolinkurl{Library/CompilerLibrary.fss}, lines 301--312, in the revival's
repository).
The team's compiled type checker compared every member of an exported trait
with the component's: in 2009 member by member, an abstract method matched
by a declaration of the same header apart from the modifier \KWD{abstract}
(Sukyoung Ryu, commit \texttt{edb516dba}), and in its snapshots of 2011 and
2012 by refusing a component whose trait has fewer members than the
exported one (commit \texttt{5a68404fd}, the last).
The code generator makes a call of a method through a trait an interface call
on the component's declaration of the trait, so a compiled call of an abstract
method that the component's trait leaves out, or declares at another header,
stops at run time with \texttt{NoSuchMethodError}, also where every object
defines the method (row~816 of the revival's gap ledger).
\Library\ followed the earlier practice of the interpreter, which finds a
method on the object by name at a call and never needed the declaration, and
left twelve such methods undeclared in their component
\TYP{FortressLibrary}; the component now declares them, without bodies.
The text of \secref{apis}, which asks a matching definition to include
definitions for all declarations other than the methods and fields declared
abstract, is unchanged: it asks no body for an abstract method, and the revised
rule asks none either.
A reversal would restore the original sentences and return the compiled
export check to asking no declaration for an abstract method of an exported
trait, so that a compiled call of a method that a component leaves out stops
at run time again; the twelve declarations of \library\ would then be
unnecessary, though harmless, or the code generator would declare such a
method in the component's interface itself, a change that still leaves a
method declared at another header, a narrower return type and a functional
method failing at run time.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-abstract-declared/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The compiled type checker refuses a component whose trait leaves
out a method that the exported trait declares abstract (``missing members of
the trait/object in the component'') or declares it at another header
(``different method''), and takes a declaration with a body or without one.
The interpreter compares no component with the \apisN\ it exports, so it loads
and runs a component that leaves such a method out, or declares it at another
header, where each object defines it (row~841 of the revival's gap
ledger).
The compiled type checker compares a value parameter of an object
declaration by its modifiers other than \KWD{var}: it takes a component's
object declaration whose value parameter is declared \KWD{var} where the
exported one's is not, as \TYP{OneShot}'s parameter \VAR{canTryIt} in
\TYP{OneShotFlag}.
The rule above, like the matching definition of \secref{apis}, asks the same
header, and an object's header holds its value parameters with their
modifiers (row~844 of the revival's gap ledger).
The compiled type checker compares no name of a value parameter of an object
declaration: a component's object declaration whose value parameter is named
\VAR{z} satisfies an exported one whose value parameter is named \VAR{a}, so
that a compiled client that reads the field \VAR{a} stops at run time with
\texttt{NoSuchMethodError}, and one whose two value parameters have each
other's names satisfies the exported one too (row~839 of the revival's gap
ledger).
The compiled type checker takes only a field declaration as satisfying a field
declaration of an exported trait, where \secref{export-statements} takes a
getter declaration too: a component's trait that declares a getter \VAR{x} for
the exported trait's field \VAR{x} is refused (``different field'', row~842 of
the revival's gap ledger).
The compiled type checker takes only a method declaration with the same
header, its return type included, as satisfying a method declaration of an
exported trait that is not abstract, where \secref{export-statements} takes one
whose return type is a subtype of the exported one's: a component's method
\VAR{m} whose return type is \TYP{B}, where \TYP{B} extends \TYP{A}, does not
satisfy an exported method \VAR{m} whose return type is \TYP{A} (``different
method''); whether the rule or the check is to change is open (row~834 of the
revival's gap ledger).
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/components/source-code.tex},
lines 380--385, which the change revises:
\begin{quote}
and contains,
for each field declaration and non-abstract method declaration
in the exported declaration,
a satisfying declaration (or a set of declarations).
When a trait has an abstract method declared,
a satisfying trait declaration is allowed to provide a concrete declaration.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{A native binding that names a static helper}
\seclabel{revival-native-helpers}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] \secref{FortressBuiltin}: the comment of
\VAR{builtinPrimitive} in the \apiN\ \TYP{FortressBuiltin}, which this
specification typesets from the \apiN's source file.
\item[Change] The comment says that the argument of \VAR{builtinPrimitive}
names either a glue class, a subclass of
\texttt{com.sun.fortress.interpreter.glue.NativeApp}, which only the
interpreter runs, or a helper, a public static method of a Java class of the
package \texttt{com.sun.fortress.nativeHelpers}, which both the interpreter
and the compiled code call; that the interpreter loads a helper beside its glue
classes, taking among the methods of that name the one whose Java parameter and
result types carry the declared types, \KWD{self} first, and carrying each value
at its declared type; that the compiler, before type checking, rewrites such a
body into a call of the helper, which the type checker checks against the
helper's Java signature; and that a string under that package that names no
such method is an error on both.
It also names the package that defines the subclasses of the classes
\texttt{NativeFn0} to \texttt{NativeFn4}
\texttt{com.sun.fortress.interpreter.glue.prim}, where it said
\texttt{com.sun.fortress.interpreter.glue.primitive}, a package that the
implementation does not have.
The comment gains a box.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 10~October 2026 that every native binding of \library\
keeps the library's own form, a body that is exactly a call of
\VAR{builtinPrimitive}, and that its string names a static helper of the
package \texttt{com.sun.fortress.nativeHelpers}, the compiled code's own
helpers, in place of a glue class, which takes the interpreter's values and
which the compiled code cannot call.
The bindings move onto helpers step by step, and the type checker checks each
moved binding against its helper's Java signature from the step that moves it.
The alternative not taken was \KWD{import} \KWD{java} declarations written in
the source files of \library, the compiler's own form for its natives, which
the interpreter does not run.
A reversal would restore the original comment, put the moved bindings back on
their glue classes, and leave the compiled code without a binding of the
natives of \library\ that it can call.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-natives-walk/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] 374 of the 402 bindings of \library\ name a helper:
the operators \EXP{=}, \EXP{<}, the prefix and infix \EXP{-} and
\EXP{\dotminus}, \EXP{+}, \EXP{\dotplus}, \EXP{\cdot}, \EXP{\times},
juxtaposition, \EXP{\dottimes}, \EXP{\div}, \OPR{REM}, \OPR{MOD}, \OPR{GCD},
\OPR{LCM}, \OPR{BITAND}, \OPR{BITOR}, \OPR{BITXOR}, \OPR{BITNOT} and
\OPR{CHOOSE} of the traits \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, with the
functions \VAR{widen}, \VAR{unsigned}, \VAR{big} and \VAR{partitionL} of the first and
\VAR{narrow}, \VAR{unsigned}, \VAR{big} and \VAR{partitionL} of the second, with its
getters \VAR{asZZ32} and \VAR{asNN32}, and
the same operators but \EXP{<} of the trait \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, with its
\VAR{cmp} and its getters \VAR{asZZ64} and \VAR{asNN64}; the shifts \OPR{LSHIFT} and \OPR{RSHIFT} and the power of these
three traits, of the trait \EXP{\mathbb{N}64} and of the interpreter's object
\EXP{\mathbb{N}32}, with the \VAR{shift} of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, each bound at a
count or exponent of type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} or \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} beside a
declaration over \TYP{AnyIntegral} that converts its count or exponent and calls
it; twenty-nine bindings of the trait \EXP{\mathbb{N}64}, its getter
\VAR{asNN32} among them, and of the
interpreter's objects thirty-one of \EXP{\mathbb{N}32}, three of
\TYP{UnsignedLong}, forty-one of \TYP{Char}, sixty-two of \TYP{Float} and
fifty-one of \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}; the getters \VAR{asString}, \VAR{asExprString}
and \VAR{asFloat} of the interpreter's objects \TYP{Int}, \TYP{Long} and
\TYP{BigNum}; the two private functions that round a numeral's value once,
into \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} for the numeral types' \VAR{asFloat} and the
coercions of \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, and into \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} for the
coercions of \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}; the thirteen methods of the object \TYP{FlatString} of \library\
and its line separator; the method \VAR{ilkName} of the trait \TYP{Object} and
\EXP{\sequiv} over \TYP{Any}; the four methods of the trait
\TYP{JavaBufferedWriter} and its three factories, over a file's name, standard
output and standard error; the eight methods of the trait
\TYP{JavaBufferedReader} and its two factories, over a file's name and standard
input; the three methods and the factory of the trait \TYP{JavaArrayStore}
and the two methods and the factory of the trait \TYP{JavaRR64ArrayStore}, the
stores of the arrays of \library; the methods \VAR{wait}, \VAR{ready} and
\VAR{stop} of the object \TYP{Thread} and the private method under its
\VAR{val}; the count and the $i$th of the program's arguments, from which
\VAR{args} is built; and the functions \VAR{abort}, \VAR{printTaskTrace}, \VAR{char}, \VAR{fromRawBits},
\VAR{nanoTime}, \VAR{random} and \VAR{match}, with \VAR{printThreadInfo},
\VAR{printWithThread}, \VAR{printlnWithThread}, \VAR{getEnvironment} and
\VAR{getProperty}, each of these five declared over \TYP{FlatString} beside an
overload over \TYP{String} that flattens its argument, and \VAR{match} beside
one over two \TYP{String}s that flattens both.
Fifteen of the helpers named the interpreter does not load:
\texttt{stringOps.typeName} for the method \VAR{ilkName} of the trait
\TYP{Object}, \texttt{equality.sEquiv} for \EXP{\sequiv} over \TYP{Any}, the
six of \texttt{runtime} for the object \TYP{Thread}, \VAR{abort} and
\VAR{printTaskTrace}, and the seven of \texttt{arrayOps} for the two stores;
it answers each with an operation of its own, and the compiled code calls the
helper.
The other 28 still name a glue class, which the compiled code cannot call,
those of the component \TYP{Reflect}.
Until the compiled code moves onto \library, the type checker checks the moved
bindings, but no compiled program runs them.
Where a binding declares \EXP{\mathbb{N}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}64} or \TYP{Char}
at a position at which its helper takes or answers a Java \texttt{int} or
\texttt{long}, or \TYP{UnsignedLong}, the interpreter's object of
\EXP{\mathbb{N}64}, at an argument, the compiler composes the call with one of six transfer helpers
at that position, as the compiled library writes its own operations on those
types, and the type checker checks the composition.
Under the interpreter a helper's raise of \TYP{IntegerOverflow} or
\TYP{DivisionByZero} is the library's own exception, so \EXP{\div}, \OPR{REM} and \OPR{MOD} by zero on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32},
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}32} and
\EXP{\mathbb{N}64} throw a \TYP{DivisionByZero}, as \chapref{operators} says
of an integer result.
The interpreter refuses at load a helper bound with a parameter declared
\TYP{String}, since such a value need not be a flat string; a glue class bound
over \TYP{String} is passed the text \TYP{String} in place of its
characters where the value is not a flat string, as a \TYP{String} made by
concatenating two long strings (row~846 of the revival's gap ledger).
No binding of \library\ takes a parameter declared \TYP{String}.
For a method, the interpreter is told the type that declares it: it refuses at
load a helper bound in a method of a type that no Java type carries, as a
program's own object, and takes only an overload whose first Java parameter
carries that type, which it reads by the type's name for the types of
\library\ whose names no program can give its own types, and for the object
\TYP{FlatString} of \library\ by the component that declares it.
It refuses such a binding in a dotted method of a generic trait or object only
where an instance's method is made, so a program that makes no instance loads
(row~864 of the revival's gap ledger); and it reads the declaring type, not the
value's, so a program's object that extends a carried trait of \library\
against its \KWD{comprises} clause, which the interpreter loads (row~551 of the
revival's gap ledger), still reaches the helper with a value that no Java type
carries.
The compiler leaves a declaration with a varargs parameter as a call of
\VAR{builtinPrimitive}, so the type checker does not check such a binding
against its helper (row~849 of the revival's gap ledger); no binding of
\library\ that names a helper has a varargs parameter.
Nor does it rewrite such a body in a local function or in a method of an object
expression, which stays a call of \VAR{builtinPrimitive} and does not compile
(row~865 of the revival's gap ledger); \library\ has no binding in either.
\item[Original text] The comment is typeset from the file
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/LibraryBuiltin/FortressBuiltin.fsi} of the revival's
repository, lines 14--29, unchanged from the team's last commit until this
revision; \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/} holds no copy of it, since
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/library/default-libraries.tex}, line 39,
inputs a file \nolinkurl{library/apis/FortressBuiltin.tex} that the
specification's build typesets from that \apiN\ and that neither copy keeps.
The comment, as written in the file:
\begin{quote}
The \texttt{builtinPrimitive} function is actually recognized as a special
piece of built-in magic by the Fortress interpreter.  The \texttt{javaClass}
argument names a Java Class which is a subclass of
\texttt{com.sun.fortress.interpreter.glue.NativeApp}, which provides code
for the closure which is used in place of the call to
\texttt{builtinPrimitive}.  Meanwhile all the necessary type information,
argument names, etc.\ must be declared here in Fortress-land.  For
examples, see the end of this file.

In practice, if you are extending the interpreter you will probably
want to extend \texttt{com.sun.fortress.interpreter.glue.NativeFn0,1,2,3,4}
or one of their subclasses defined in
\texttt{com.sun.fortress.interpreter.glue.primitive}.  These types are
generally easier to work with, and the boilerplate packing and
unpacking of values is done for you.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{A comparison at a NaN or at the rational indefinite}
\seclabel{revival-nan-comparison}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] The subsection ``Comparisons Operators'' of the
overview of operators in \chapref{operators}.
\item[Change] Its two sentences on the comparison of rational and of
floating-point values by the operators $<$, \EXP{\leq}, \EXP{\geq} and $>$
said that a comparison of rational values throws
\TYP{RationalComparisonError} if either argument is \EXP{1/0} or \EXP{0/0},
and that a comparison of floating-point values throws
\TYP{FloatingComparisonError} if either argument is a \TYP{NaN}.
They now say that a comparison of rational values by these four operators
returns \VAR{false} if either argument is \EXP{0/0}, for compatibility with
floating-point arithmetic, and that \EXP{-1/0} is less than and \EXP{1/0}
greater than every finite rational value (\secref{basic-rationals}); and that a
comparison of floating-point values by these four operators returns
\VAR{false} if either argument is a \TYP{NaN}, and \OPR{CMP} returns
\TYP{Unordered}.
The two sentences gain one box.
\TYP{RationalComparisonError} and \TYP{FloatingComparisonError} stay declared in
\library, and nothing throws them.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 5~November 2026 that a floating-point value compared with
a \TYP{NaN} answers \VAR{false} for these four operators and \TYP{Unordered} for
\OPR{CMP}, on both implementations, and that the overview's two sentences are
revised to the reading of the chapter on rational numbers.
The team built these answers for both floating-point types on both
implementations and threw \TYP{FloatingComparisonError} nowhere.
The chapter on rational numbers gives \EXP{0/0} \VAR{false} ``for
compatibility with floating-point arithmetic'' and orders the infinities
(\secref{basic-rationals}); the trait \TYP{Comparison} of the library chapters
gives \OPR{CMP} the fourth value \TYP{Unordered} for values of a partial order;
and the overview's own subsection on \OPR{MAX} and \OPR{MIN} answers a
\TYP{NaN} with a value, not an exception.
Java, Swift, Python's floating-point numbers, Julia and Haskell all answer
\VAR{false}.
A test for a \TYP{NaN} in software, which a throw would need, costs 15 to 20
percent of a comparison on unboxed values of Java's \texttt{double}
(\nolinkurl{explorations/perf-probes/nan-check-cost/REPORT.md} in the
revival's repository); whether it would stop the vectorisation of a loop is not
measured.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-spec-nan-comparison/decision-record.md}
in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] No program changes: both implementations already answered so.
The interpreter departs from the new text in one case: it answers \EXP{0/0}
compared with itself with \VAR{true} for \EXP{=}, \EXP{\leq} and \EXP{\geq}, and
with \TYP{EqualTo} for \OPR{CMP}, where the chapter on rational numbers gives
\VAR{false} and \TYP{Unordered} (row~440 of the revival's gap ledger).
The compiled path's own library, used until the one library replaces it,
declares no rational type and no \OPR{CMP} on floating-point values.
The answers are gated by
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/ComparisonAtNaNWalk.fss} and, for the
compiled helpers of the floating-point comparisons, by
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/compiler_tests/NativeHelperFamilyFloatCompiled.fss}
and \nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/compiler_tests/NativeHelperFamilyRR32Compiled.fss}.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/operators/opr-overview.tex},
lines 274--278:
\begin{quote}
Comparison of rational values throws \TYP{RationalComparisonError}
if either argument is the rational infinity \EXP{1/0} or the
rational indefinite \EXP{0/0}.
Comparison of floating-point values throws \TYP{FloatingComparisonError}
if either argument is a \TYP{NaN}.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
A reversal to the original sentences would need the four operators to throw
\TYP{FloatingComparisonError} at a \TYP{NaN} on both implementations, and the
rational operators \TYP{RationalComparisonError} at \EXP{1/0} and \EXP{0/0}:
a test for a \TYP{NaN} in every compiled floating-point comparison, at the cost
above, and in the interpreter's.
\end{description}

\subsection{A character literal that writes a character with its code}
\seclabel{revival-char-redundant-literal}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected section] The list of the forms of the sequence of enclosed
characters of a character literal, in \secref{preprocessing-character-literals}.
\item[Change] The list named a single character, a sequence of four or more
hexadecimal digits identifying the code point of a Unicode character, the
names \txt{TAB}, \txt{NEWLINE} and \txt{RETURN}, a sequence that ASCII
conversion converts to one character, a \unicode\ name, and an escape sequence.
It now also names a single character followed by a space and a sequence of four
or more hexadecimal digits identifying the code point of that same character,
as \txt{'$\alpha$ 03B1'}.
The passage gains one box.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; it follows the
revival's decision of 6~November 2026 that the text by which a \TYP{Char}
writes itself as an expression, its getter \VAR{asExprString}, reads back as
the same character at every code point, on both implementations: it writes
\txt{'{\char'134}"'} and \txt{'{\char'134}{\char'134}'} for the quotation mark
and the reverse solidus, the code point alone for the control characters,
U+201C, U+201D, U+2028, U+2029 and the surrogates, and for every code point
past U+FFFF, and otherwise the character itself followed by a space and its code
point, as \txt{'\'e 00e9'} for U+00E9.
The form with the character and its code was built by the team in 2011, in one
commit with the parser's rule that reads it, but the list did not name it.
A printable character written as itself stays legible, where the code alone
hides it; Python and Swift also write a printable character outside ASCII as
itself.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/char-asexprstring-2026-11-06.md} in the
revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The parser already read the form.
The text of \VAR{asExprString} changes in the interpreter at every code point
but the printable ASCII characters and the five escapes \txt{{\char'134}b},
\txt{{\char'134}t}, \txt{{\char'134}n}, \txt{{\char'134}f} and
\txt{{\char'134}r}, and in the compiled path's helper at 2,054 code points: the
quotation mark, the reverse solidus, U+201C, U+201D, U+2028, U+2029 and the
2,048 surrogates.
No code of \library, no test of the suites and no line of the revival's model
program calls the \VAR{asExprString} of a \TYP{Char}.
The parser's rule for a code point in hexadecimal digits now tries six digits,
then five, then four, so that a code of six digits, and in lower case a code of
five digits whose first four are decimal digits, as \txt{'1000a'}, read as their
character (row~898 of the revival's gap ledger); every literal that the parser
read before reads as the same character.
A surrogate written alone cannot be stored in a source file in UTF-8, so the
text of a surrogate reads back from a string given to the parser, not from a
file.
The parser departs from the text in one case: it
reads a single 16-bit unit as the character of a character literal, so it
refuses a character past U+FFFF written as itself, alone or followed by its code
(row~924); \VAR{asExprString} writes the code alone there.
The texts are gated by
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/NativeHelperFamilyCharWalk.fss} and
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/compiler_tests/NativeHelperFamilyCharCompiled.fss},
and the forms that they write are read back by
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/CharExprStringReadBack.fss}.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/lexical-structure.tex},
lines 861--866:
\begin{quote}
The sequence of enclosed characters may be
a single character
(e.g., `\txt{a}', \txt{'\$'}, `\txt{$\alpha$}', \txt{`$\oplus$'}),
a sequence of four or more hexadecimal digits
identifying the code point of a Unicode character
(e.g., `\txt{001C}', \txt{`FBAB'}, `\txt{1D11E}'), \ldots
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
A reversal to the original list would need the parser to refuse the form, and
\VAR{asExprString} to write another form at the printable characters outside
ASCII, such as the code point alone, which reads back at every code point but
hides the character.
\end{description}

\subsection{The conversions between the integer types}
\seclabel{revival-int-conversions}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{basic-integers}; \secref{revival-shifts}.
\item[Change] The passage on the explicit conversions between the integer types
states the getters \VAR{asZZ32}, \VAR{asZZ64}, \VAR{asNN32}, \VAR{asNN64} and
\VAR{asZZ} of each of the five integer types, which keep the value and throw
\TYP{IntegerOverflow} where the type converted to does not hold it, and the
getters \VAR{bitsAsNN32}, \VAR{bitsAsNN64}, \VAR{bitsAsZZ32} and \VAR{bitsAsZZ64}
of the four fixed-size types, which keep the bits.
It states \VAR{narrow}, \VAR{widen}, \VAR{signed}, \VAR{unsigned} and \VAR{big} as
the methods that convert without throwing \TYP{IntegerOverflow}, each declared on
every integer type to which it applies and returning a value of its result type
unchanged, and it drops \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} from the sentence on \VAR{narrow}:
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}} has neither \VAR{narrow} nor \VAR{widen}, and converts to a
fixed-size type only by a getter.
The passage gains one box, and the box of \secref{revival-shifts} beside it names
the paragraph that now states \VAR{narrow}.
The change of \secref{revival-shifts} no longer gives \VAR{narrow} on
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}}.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided on
6~November 2026 that each integer type has getters that convert and throw
\TYP{IntegerOverflow} where the value does not fit, that the fixed-size types
have getters for the bit pattern, that \VAR{narrow}, \VAR{widen}, \VAR{signed},
\VAR{unsigned} and \VAR{big} stay as the conversions that keep bits, declared on
every type to which they apply, and that the \VAR{narrow} and \VAR{widen} of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}} go.
The team's later compiler library declares the getter \VAR{asZZ32} of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, which throws \TYP{IntegerOverflow} for a value that does not
fit, and the four getters \VAR{bitsAsNN32}, \VAR{bitsAsNN64}, \VAR{bitsAsZZ32}
and \VAR{bitsAsZZ64} (\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/LibraryBuiltin/CompilerBuiltin.fsi},
in the revival's repository); this working draft says in general that an
integer result too large for its type throws \TYP{IntegerOverflow}; and a plain
name that checks beside a name that says it keeps bits is the pattern of Swift
and Julia.
Before the revision \library\ declared each of the five methods on some of the
integer types only, so that a call on another type went through a coercion to
another type's method: \VAR{widen} of the \EXP{\mathbb{N}64} \EXP{2^{64}-1} gave
the \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} \EXP{-1}, and \VAR{unsigned} of an \EXP{\mathbb{N}32} gave
an \EXP{\mathbb{N}64}.
The \VAR{narrow} and \VAR{widen} of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} kept the low 32 and 64 bits,
so that \VAR{widen}, under the name of a widening, narrowed, and turned
\EXP{2^{63}} negative.
A reversal would declare \VAR{narrow} and \VAR{widen} on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} again,
keeping the low bits, beside the getters or in their place, and would remove the
methods that return their argument unchanged.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/integer-conversions-2026-11-06.md} in the
revival's repository.
\item[Effect] The interpreter computes each getter and each method as the text
states, on all five types, and refuses \VAR{narrow} or \VAR{widen} of a value of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}} at the call, which finds no declaration.
The compiled path, which until it is built on \library\ compiles against a
smaller library of its own, declares of the getters only \VAR{asZZ32} of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, \VAR{asNN32} of \EXP{\mathbb{N}32},
\VAR{asNN64} of \EXP{\mathbb{N}64}, \VAR{asZZ} of the four fixed-size types and
the four getters that keep the bits, none on \EXP{\mathbb{Z}}; and of the
methods only \VAR{narrow} of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} and of \EXP{\mathbb{N}64},
\VAR{widen} of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and of \EXP{\mathbb{N}32}, \VAR{unsigned} of
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} and of \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, and \VAR{signed} of
\EXP{\mathbb{N}32} and of \EXP{\mathbb{N}64}, with no \VAR{big} (row~349 of the
revival's gap ledger).
The getters are gated by \nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/IntegerConversionsWalk.fss},
and the helpers that five of them name by
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/compiler_tests/IntegerConversionHelpersCompiled.fss}.
No line of the revival's model program calls any of these conversions.
\item[Original text] None: no chapter of this working draft names \VAR{asZZ32},
\VAR{asZZ64}, \VAR{asNN32}, \VAR{asNN64}, \VAR{asZZ}, the four getters that keep
the bits, \VAR{narrow}, \VAR{widen}, \VAR{signed}, \VAR{unsigned} or \VAR{big}.
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{The numeral types, their conversion and their default}
\seclabel{revival-numerals}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{literals}; \secref{types-libraries}; \secref{static-expr};
\secref{coercion}; \secref{static-arg-inference}; \secref{numeral-default};
\secref{basic-rationals}; \secref{natparams}; \secref{lib:BinaryEndianWord};
\secref{lib:BinaryLinearEndianSequence};
\secref{lib:BinaryEndianLinearEndianSequence}; \secref{revival-numeral-type};
\secref{revival-inference}; \secref{revival-asfloat};
\secref{revival-object-kinds}.
\item[Change] \secref{literals} says that every numeral has the type
\TYP{Numeral}, through \TYP{NaturalNumeral} for a numeral without a radix point
or \TYP{RadixPointNumeral} for one with a radix point, and that its value holds
the four numbers that were the static arguments of the Working Draft's numeral
types, its digit count $n$, the number $m$ of its digits after the radix point,
its radix $r$ and the value $v$ of its digits, as the getters \VAR{digits},
\VAR{fractionDigits}, \VAR{radix} and \VAR{digitValue}; the two types with an
explicit radix fold into the radix, and \TYP{Literal} into the digit count.
\secref{types-libraries} names \TYP{Numeral}, without static arguments, among
the standard types for literals.
Its paragraph that said that numerals are not directly converted now says that a
numeral is converted where it stands, and its sentences on library designers
and their coercions stand.
New paragraphs say where a place fixes a type: where the type declared there
declares a coercion from the numeral's type or is a numeral type; that there
the numeral is converted from its exact value, exactly into the integer types
and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} and with one rounding into \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} and
\EXP{\mathbb{R}32}, a numeral outside the type's range being a static error;
that elsewhere it is converted into its default, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32},
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} or \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} by its value, or \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} with
a radix point, the default applying to a numeral and never to an expression of
a numeral type; that a type of a program's own receives a numeral only through
its coercion from the numeral's type, which sees the four numbers of a numeral
as written and is applied once for each place, so that the place denotes one
object; that a numeral type is written only as a parameter type; and that
inside a coercion's body two numeral values are equal and strictly equivalent
by their value.
Its box on the numeral's type is rewritten, and two boxes are added, after the
numeral types and after the paragraph on static rational expressions.
\secref{static-expr} says, after its list of the traits of static expressions,
that \library\ declare no constant trait and that an expression of numerals and
operators is folded exactly into a signed constant, which takes one of three
defaults where nothing fixes its type, and which operators it leaves alone; a
box follows, and another follows the paragraph on the concatenation of literals,
which says that the concatenation of two natural numerals is built.
\secref{coercion} says that a numeral's conversion into its default is a rule
beside coercion, that a numeral is applicable without coercion to a parameter of
its default type and to one that fixes no type, and that a type of a program's
own without a coercion from the numeral's type refuses a numeral, with a box;
its box on integers in floating-point expressions names the type
\TYP{NaturalNumeral}, and no longer says that the interpreter leaves a large
numeral unconverted.
\secref{static-arg-inference} says that \EXP{\VAR{id}(3)} is instantiated at the
numeral's default, with a box.
\secref{numeral-default} gives the default at every place that fixes no type,
the narrowest default for a type parameter or the branches that several
numerals fix, and the tie of a numeral with a radix point read as an
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, with a box.
\secref{basic-rationals} names the coercions from the two numeral types, their
pairs of \EXP{=} and their \VAR{asFloat}, with a box.
\secref{natparams} gains a box on a \KWD{nat} parameter used as a value, and
the binary chapter's declarations over \TYP{NaturalNumeral} gain eight boxes;
the text of both stands.
The entries \secref{revival-numeral-type}, \secref{revival-inference},
\secref{revival-asfloat} and \secref{revival-object-kinds} are revised where
they named \TYP{IntLiteral} or said what the interpreter does with a numeral.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A.
The revival decided on 8~November 2026 that a numeral is converted where it
stands, into the type that its place fixes or else into the machine default;
that its own type is the Working Draft's \TYP{NaturalNumeral} or
\TYP{RadixPointNumeral}, below \TYP{Numeral}, the four numbers fields of its
value; and that a type of a program's own receives a numeral through its
coercion from that type, which the compiled code applies once for each place.
The Working Draft gives numerals types of their own so that library designers
decide by coercions how numerals meet other types, and so that an interval type
receives \EXP{3.1415926535897932384} unrounded
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/literals.tex}, lines
132--148).
The team built that coercion under its own names, with the numeral's exact
value, in the compiled path's own library (``ZZ32 now coerces IntLiteral as
described in the spec'', the team's commit \texttt{128f313b5b} of 17~November
2009, in the revival's repository), but never the four numbers.
The change keeps what the team built, one type for each kind of numeral with
its exact value at the coercion, and adds the Working Draft's names and
numbers: a coercion that receives only a floating-point value rounds twice, as a
16-bit floating-point type of a program's own would round
\EXP{1.00390625000000000001} first to the nearest \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} and then to
a 16-bit value other than the one nearest to the numeral.
A field cannot give a static size, so the binary chapter's static word width
from a numeral's digit count cannot be declared, and its \EXP{\|} with a numeral
has no static result type; those declarations stand, each with a box.
The default is the type that the interpreter already gave an integer numeral by
its value, and the Java programming language's type for an integer literal.
The revival declined exact defaults, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}, as
the team's restart of 2012 gives \EXP{\mathbb{N}} and \EXP{\mathbb{Q}}
(\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/Expressions/literals.tick},
line 25), since its model program's constants declared without a type would then
be rationals and every index declared without a type would need a conversion.
In two places the change departs from the revival's rule that the team's later
decision wins over its earlier one: the compiled path's own library, the team's
later word, names its numeral types \TYP{IntLiteral} and \TYP{FloatLiteral}, and
keeps a numeral's own type at every place that fixes no type.
The revival takes the Working Draft's names and the default as its own
finishing of the text; no source of the team calls those names either final or
temporary.
The team's one word on a numeral's value at run time, that ``IntLiterals should
always be transient objects with minimal lifetime''
(\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/src/com/sun/fortress/compiler/runtimeValues/FIntLiteral.java},
lines 21--23), is kept: a program meets a numeral's value only inside a
coercion or an operator whose parameter has a numeral type.
An expression of numerals alone is folded exactly, as the Working Draft computes
a static rational expression exactly and rounds it at most once
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/literals.tex}, lines
162--173); its result is a signed constant written as numerals, not a value of a
constant trait, so that a coercion of a program's own sees only the four numbers
of a numeral as written, and \EXP{x = 7/2} stays a rational.
A \KWD{nat} parameter used as a value has one type, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, as the
revival decided on 28~September 2026 for the ranges (\secref{revival-inference}),
not \EXP{\mathbb{N}32} as \secref{natparams} says.
A numeral at a type of a program's own that declares no coercion from the
numeral's type is refused, since coercion does not chain; the alternative not
taken converts it into its default first and then by the type's own coercion
from that default.
A reversal to the Working Draft's polymorphic numerals would keep a numeral's
own type wherever nothing fixes a type, as the compiled path's own library does,
so that \EXP{x = 3} binds a numeral and \EXP{\TYP{Cell}(3)} is a
\EXP{\TYP{Cell}\llbracket\TYP{NaturalNumeral}\rrbracket}; a reversal to the names
of \secref{revival-numeral-type} would rename the two types \TYP{IntLiteral} and
\TYP{FloatLiteral}, which no program that does not write them would notice.
The full reasoning is in
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/literal-design-judgement-2026-11-08.md} and
\nolinkurl{explorations/compile-ladder/rung-literals-spec/REPORT.md} in the
revival's repository.
\item[Effect] Both implementations convert a numeral where its place fixes a
type and give it its default elsewhere.
So \EXP{x = 3} binds a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and \EXP{x \sequiv z} is \VAR{true}
for \EXP{z\COLON\mathbb{Z}32 = 3} on both, where the compiled path answered
\VAR{false}; \EXP{y\COLON\mathbb{R}64 = 0.5} is strictly equivalent to
\EXP{a + a} for \EXP{a\COLON\mathbb{R}64 = 0.25}, \EXP{q\COLON\mathbb{Q} = 0.5}
binds \EXP{1/2}, and
\EXP{1/2 = 0.5} is \VAR{true}, where the interpreter answered otherwise
(rows~943 and~944 of the revival's gap ledger); a variable of type
\EXP{\mathbb{N}32} or \EXP{\mathbb{N}64} takes a numeral, and one of type
\EXP{\mathbb{R}32} takes a numeral with one rounding, where the interpreter
refused them (row~454); a variable bound from \EXP{3000000000} is a
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} and reaches a declaration over \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}, where the
compiled path stopped at run time (row~946); \EXP{7} passed to a parameter of
type \TYP{Object} is a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} there (row~79); a numeral outside its
type's range is a static error, where the compiled path raised an exception at
run time (row~325); a numeral at a declared floating-point type is rounded once,
where the compiled code rounded it two or three times (row~862); and a
\KWD{nat} parameter used as a value is a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, which a variable of
type \EXP{\mathbb{N}32} refuses, where the compiled type checker took it
(row~486).
\EXP{\VAR{round}(2.50000000000000001)} is 2, its numeral read as an
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64}.
A numeral that took its default prints as a value of its default type, so that
\EXP{\hbox{\rm``''}\;2.50} is \hbox{\rm``\STR{2.5}''}, and the team's test
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/tests/NumberPrintTest.fss}, which asserted that a
numeral prints as written, is changed; a value of a numeral type prints as the
numeral is written, with its radix after an underscore where it is not ten.
The top-level \EXP{\neq} of \library, whose parameters are of type \TYP{Any},
departs from \secref{operator-NE}, by which \EXP{\neq} applies \EXP{\neg} to
the result of \EXP{=} on the same operands: it takes a numeral at its default
and compares that by \EXP{=}, so that \EXP{u \neq 0} is \VAR{true} for a zero
\VAR{u} of \EXP{\mathbb{N}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{N}64} or \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}, none of
which declares \EXP{=} with \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and \EXP{q \neq 0.5} is
\VAR{true} for \EXP{q\COLON\mathbb{Q} = 1/2}, since \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} declares no
\EXP{=} with \EXP{\mathbb{R}64}, though \EXP{u = 0} and \EXP{q = 0.5} are
\VAR{true} by the pairs of \EXP{=} (row~966 of the revival's gap ledger).
The interpreter answered the two inequalities so before this change; the
compiled path's own library declares \EXP{\neq} on each integer type with an
operand of that type, into which the numeral is converted, so that there
\EXP{u \neq 0} is \VAR{false}.
The interpreter, which has no static types, refuses an integer numeral outside
its type's range when the place is evaluated, and converts a numeral that
rounds to an infinity of \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} or \EXP{\mathbb{R}32} into that
infinity with no error (row~967); it converts a numeral at a parameter
of the declaration that a call chooses, at a variable or a field of a declared
type and at a declared return type, but not one passed through a tuple or a
varargs parameter (rows~395 and~511).
The compiled path, which until it is built on \library\ compiles against a
smaller library of its own, gives its numerals that library's types,
\TYP{IntLiteral} and \TYP{FloatLiteral}, which a program there writes as a
coercion's parameter type; the compiled type checker does not apply the rule
that a numeral type is written only as a parameter type to the libraries' own
files.
On the compiled path until then, a coercion of a program's own receives that
library's numeral value without its four numbers, the conversion of a numeral by
an \EXP{=} of a number type is not folded where it is called, and two numeral
values are not compared by their value.
The compiled code applies a program's coercion from a numeral type once for
each place, in the initialization of the class of the component that holds the
place, before the program runs, so that a coercion that throws an exception
stops the program; at a place whose type is a generic type of the program's own
it applies the coercion at each evaluation of the place (row~957).
The implementations depart from the text in these further ways, each with its
row of the revival's gap ledger: the interpreter runs the operator of
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} where a floating-point numeral stands to the left of an
\EXP{\mathbb{R}32} (row~958), and counts only one of the leading zeros of a
numeral with a radix point among its digits (row~960); the compiled path gives
a program's coercion a negative numeral with its sign, not as the prefix
operator \EXP{-} on the coercion's result for the natural numeral (row~961),
stops with an error that no \KWD{catch} sees where a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int}
parameter of $2^{31}$ or more is used as a value (row~962), and keeps a
numeral's own type at the selector of a \KWD{typecase} (row~963); and both
leave an expression of numerals alone as written where the form of its result
needs a prefix \EXP{-} or a \EXP{/} that its component writes nowhere
(row~956).
No line of the revival's model program changes, and by reading none of its
numerals changes its value.
\item[Original text] \nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/literals.tex},
lines 83--86, 104--108, 110--115, 117--125 and 127--129, which the numeral types
change in form; the draft notes of lines 87--102 stand:
\begin{quote}
A numeral containing only digits (let $n$ be the number of digits)
has type \EXP{\TYP{NaturalNumeral}\llbracket{}n,10,v\rrbracket} where $v$ is the value of the numeral
interpreted in radix ten.  If the numeral has no leading zeros, or is
the literal \EXP{0}, then it also has type \EXP{\TYP{Literal}\llbracket{}v\rrbracket}.
[The two draft notes of lines 87--102, kept unchanged in the revised text.]

A numeral containing only digits (let $n$ be the number of
digits) then an underscore, then a radix indicator (let $r$ be the
radix) has type \EXP{\TYP{NaturalNumeralWithExplicitRadix}\llbracket{}n,r,v\rrbracket}
where $v$ is
the value of the $n$-digit numeral interpreted in radix $r$.

A numeral containing only digits (let $n$ be the total number
of digits) and a radix point (let $m$ be the number of digits after
the radix point) has type
\EXP{\TYP{RadixPointNumeral}\llbracket{}n,m,10,v\rrbracket} where $v$ is
the value of the numeral, with the radix point deleted,
interpreted in radix ten.

A numeral containing only digits (let $n$ be the total number
of digits) and a radix point (let $m$ be the number of digits after
the radix point), then an underscore, then a radix indicator (let $r$
be the radix) has the following type:
\EXP{\TYP{RadixPointNumeralWithExplicitRadix}\llbracket{}n,m,r,v\rrbracket}
where $v$ is the value of the $n$-digit numeral, with the radix point
deleted, interpreted in radix $r$.

Every numeral also has type
\EXP{\TYP{Numeral}\llbracket{}n,m,r,v\rrbracket}
for appropriate values of \VAR{n}, \VAR{m}, \VAR{r}, and \VAR{v}.
\end{quote}
Lines 132--141, which the change contradicts at the places that fix no type, and
which the revised text replaces up to the sentence ``Therefore, numerals have
their own types as described above.'', which stands with lines 142--148:
\begin{quote}
Numerals are not directly converted to any of the number types
because, as in common mathematical usage,
we expect them to be polymorphic.
For example, consider the numeral \EXP{3.1415926535897932384};
converting it immediately to a floating-point number
may lose precision.
If that numeral is used in an expression
involving floating-point intervals,
it would be better to convert it directly to an interval.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/constant.tex}, lines
44--56, which the signed constant changes in form, and which stand:
\begin{quote}
The \emph{constant} traits, which describe values denoted by
expressions composed from literals and operators (described in
\secref{constant-values});
the type of a constant expression encodes the value of the expression.
For example, the type of a constant expression \EXP{3+5},
\EXP{\TYP{IntegerConstant}\llbracket\VAR{false}, 3+5\rrbracket},
encodes the value of the expression; the operator \EXP{+} is
used in the static arguments.
[\ldots]
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/conversions-coercions.tex}, lines
78--81, which stand, the default being a rule beside them:
\begin{quote}
Also, coercion occurs
only when the declared type of the corresponding parameter in the
functional declaration is exactly the type \VAR{U} being coerced to
not if it is a supertype of \VAR{U}.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/trait-parameters.tex}, lines 83--86,
which the change contradicts for a \KWD{nat} parameter used as a value, and
which stand with a box:
\begin{quote}
A \KWD{nat} parameter may be used to instantiate other
\KWD{nat} parameters, or to appear in any context that a
variable of type \EXP{\mathbb{N}32} can appear,
except that it cannot be assigned to.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/advanced-lib/binary.tex}, lines 1620--1621
and 1659--1662, typeset in lines 1681--1682 and 1720--1723, which the change
contradicts, and which stand with boxes, as do the declarations over
\TYP{NaturalNumeral} of lines 905--914, 1117--1135, 1750, 1860--1868, 2016,
2078--2082, 2114 and 2271--2281:
\begin{quote}
\EXP{\KWD{coerce}\llbracket\KWD{nat} m,\KWD{nat} \VAR{radix},\KWD{nat} q,\KWD{nat} k,\KWD{nat} v\rrbracket(x\COLON \TYP{NaturalNumeral}\llbracket{}m,\VAR{radix},v\rrbracket)}\\
\quad\EXP{\KWD{where} \{\,\VAR{radix} = 2^{q}, q \cdot m = n \cdot 2^{\VAR{bk}}\,\}}\\
\EXP{\KWD{opr}\,\|\,\llbracket\KWD{nat} m,\KWD{nat} \VAR{radix},\KWD{nat} q,\KWD{nat} k,\KWD{nat} v\rrbracket(\KWD{self}, \VAR{other}\COLON \TYP{NaturalNumeral}\llbracket{}m,\VAR{radix},v\rrbracket)\COLON}\\
\quad\EXP{\TYP{LinearSequence}\llbracket{}\TYP{BinaryWord}\llbracket{}b\rrbracket,n+k\rrbracket \KWD{where} \{\,\VAR{radix} = 2^{q}, q \cdot m = k \cdot 2^{b}\,\}}\\
\EXP{\KWD{opr}\,\|\,\llbracket\KWD{nat} m,\KWD{nat} \VAR{radix},\KWD{nat} q,\KWD{nat} k,\KWD{nat} v\rrbracket(\VAR{other}\COLON \TYP{NaturalNumeral}\llbracket{}m,\VAR{radix},v\rrbracket, \KWD{self})\COLON}\\
\quad\EXP{\TYP{LinearSequence}\llbracket{}\TYP{BinaryWord}\llbracket{}b\rrbracket,n+k\rrbracket \KWD{where} \{\,\VAR{radix} = 2^{q}, q \cdot m = k \cdot 2^{b}\,\}}
\end{quote}
The other passages this change revises are the revival's own text, not the
Working Draft's.
\nolinkurl{Specification/basic/expressions/literals.tex}, lines 149--165, said,
before this revision:
\begin{quote}
In \library, every numeral without a radix point has the type
\TYP{IntLiteral}, which extends \TYP{Number} and none of the number types
(\secref{basic-rationals}); each of the five integer types
(\secref{basic-integers}), \EXP{\mathbb{Q}} and \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} declares a
coercion from it.
\end{quote}
and its box said that ``the compiled type checker gives every numeral without a
radix point that type'', and that the interpreter ``gives such a numeral the
type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, or \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} or \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} when its
value does not fit in the narrower type, and it is to give it the type
\TYP{IntLiteral} once it runs with the static types of the compiled type
checker''.
The box of \secref{coercion} said, in
\nolinkurl{Specification/basic/conversions-coercions.tex}, lines 84--88:
\begin{quote}
\Library\ give an integer numeral the type \TYP{IntLiteral}, from which
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} coerces, and the compiled type checker types a numeral so
(\secref{literals}, \secref{revival-numeral-type}).
The interpreter does not yet: it gives an integer numeral outside the range of
