\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} a wider integer type, and so does not yet convert it.
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification/basic/inference.tex}, lines 185--187, said that
\EXP{b\COLON\mathbb{Z}64 = \VAR{id}(3)} ``instantiates \VAR{id} at the
numeral's own type (\secref{literals}) and coerces the result'', and lines
304--307 said that a numeral whose conversions tie ``is read as a
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, or as a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} or a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} when its
value does not fit in the narrower type''.
\nolinkurl{Specification/basic-lib/numbers.tex}, line 43, said that
``\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} (\STR{RR64}) coerces from \EXP{\mathbb{R}32}, from
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, and from integer numerals''.
The effect of \secref{revival-numeral-type} said:
\begin{quote}
Inequality is not converted: the top-level declaration of \EXP{\neq}, whose
parameters are of type \TYP{Any}, is applicable to an integer value and a
numeral without coercion, and the compiled type checker selects it and converts
neither operand.
\end{quote}
and that ``\TYP{IntLiteral} declares its own \VAR{zero}, \VAR{one}, \VAR{even},
\VAR{odd}, \VAR{floor}, \VAR{ceiling}, \VAR{truncate}, \OPR{DIVIDES},
\OPR{TIMES} and a power with a numeral exponent'', and that ``the interpreter
still gives an integer numeral the type \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64}
or \EXP{\mathbb{Z}} by its value, so a numeral reaches none of the declarations
of \TYP{IntLiteral} there''.
The rationale of \secref{revival-inference} said that ``the compiled type
checker gives a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} parameter used as a value the type of a
numeral'', and its effect that ``where the conversions of a numeral tie, the
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} declaration or instance is taken''.
The rationale of \secref{revival-asfloat} named ``the numeral type
\TYP{IntLiteral}'' among the types that declare \VAR{asFloat}, and its effect
named ``the library's 21 coercions between number types''.
The effect of \secref{revival-object-kinds} said that \library\ declare ``the
two numeral objects of the interpreter'' as value objects, and named two
departures:
\begin{quote}
the interpreter gives a floating-point numeral bound to a variable of type
\EXP{\mathbb{R}64} the type of the numeral object, so it is not strictly
equivalent to a computed \EXP{\mathbb{R}64} value of the same bits;
an integer numeral is a \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32}, \EXP{\mathbb{Z}64} or
\EXP{\mathbb{Z}} value in the interpreter, by its magnitude, and a numeral
object to the compiled type checker, so a numeral
compared with a typed value can answer differently on the two paths;
\end{quote}
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/types-vals-vars.tex}, lines 494--500,
which the numeral types change in form:
\begin{quote}
\Library\ define simple standard types for literals such as
\EXP{\TYP{BooleanLiteral}\llbracket{}b\rrbracket},
\TYP{()} (pronounced ``void''),
\TYP{Character}, \TYP{String}, and
\EXP{\TYP{Numeral}\llbracket{}n,m,r,v\rrbracket}
for appropriate values of \VAR{b}, \VAR{n}, \VAR{m}, \VAR{r}, and \VAR{v}
(See \secref{literals} for a discussion of Fortress literals).
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C: the numeral types are siblings of the
number types under \TYP{Number} whichever rule on instantiations the compiled
type checker keeps.
\end{description}

\subsection{A string literal as one object}
\seclabel{revival-string-literals}

\begin{description}
\item[Affected sections] \secref{literals}; \chapref{objects};
\secref{static-expr}; \secref{revival-object-kinds}.
\item[Change] \secref{literals} says that two string literals of the same text
denote one object, so that every evaluation of a string literal with a given
text yields the same string; that a string that a program builds as it runs is
another object than a literal of the same text, unless the operation that builds
it answers an existing string; and that a concatenation of string literals is a
string literal.
The opening of \chapref{objects} says the same of strict equivalence.
Each passage gains a box, and so does the paragraph of \secref{static-expr} on
the concatenation of literals, which says that it is built.
The effect of \secref{revival-object-kinds} gains a sentence.
\item[Rationale] This change does not follow from route~A; the revival decided
on 8~November 2026 that two string literals of one text are one object, on both
implementations, and that a concatenation of two string literals is folded into
one literal.
Strings remain reference objects, whose equality of text is their own \EXP{=}
(\secref{revival-object-kinds}).
Every source of the team that speaks of literals makes equal literals one value:
the Working Draft's ``Literals are values; they do not require evaluation''
(\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/literals.tex}, lines
25--26); the team's restart of 2012, ``All literals represent value objects''
(\nolinkurl{Documentation/Specification/Prose/Language/Expressions/literals.tick},
line 29); and the team's compiled test
\nolinkurl{ProjectFortress/compiler_regressions/seqv.fss}, line 22, which
expects \hbox{\rm``\STR{cat}''} to be strictly equivalent to
\hbox{\rm``\STR{cat}''}.
Java, Kotlin, Scala and Clojure, whose strings are Java strings, give equal
literals one object.
The concatenation is the Working Draft's own sentence, which neither of the
team's implementations built.
A reversal would have each evaluation of a string literal construct a new
string, so that two literals of one text are never strictly equivalent, and
would leave a concatenation of literals to be computed when it is evaluated.
The full reasoning is in \nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/literals-2026-11-08.md},
sections 6.1 and 6.4, in the revival's repository.
\item[Effect] On both implementations
\EXP{\hbox{\rm``\STR{ab}''} \sequiv \hbox{\rm``\STR{ab}''}} is \VAR{true}, as
is one literal evaluated twice, and so are
\EXP{(\hbox{\rm``\STR{foo}''} \| \hbox{\rm``\STR{bar}''}) \sequiv \hbox{\rm``\STR{foobar}''}}
and \EXP{(\hbox{\rm``\STR{a}''} \| \hbox{\rm`\STR{b}'}) \sequiv \hbox{\rm``\STR{ab}''}},
where the interpreter answered \VAR{false}, and so did the strict equivalence
that the compiled code gives the values of \library\ (row~945 of the revival's
gap ledger); the compiled path's own library answers by text (below).
A string built at run time is not strictly equivalent to an equal literal,
unless the library's operation answers the string it was given, as a substring
that spans its whole string does on both implementations.
The literals of \library\ are strings of this kind too, so that an empty
substring, which \library\ answer with their literal \hbox{\rm``''}, is strictly
equivalent to \hbox{\rm``''}.
The interpreter interns the text of each literal, and compares two flat strings
strictly by the identity of the Java strings that they carry; the compiled code
loads each literal as the Java Virtual Machine's interned string, and its strict
equivalence compares the Java strings that two of its string values carry by
identity.
The concatenation is folded in the desugaring that both implementations run
before the static check.
The compiled path's own library, until \library\ replace it, answers its own
\EXP{\sequiv} on two strings by their text (row~908).
\item[Original text] None: the Working Draft does not say whether two string
literals of the same text are one object.
The passages that this change builds stand, in
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/literals.tex}, line 57,
``A string literal has type \TYP{String}.'', and in
\nolinkurl{Specification-1.0-frozen/basic/expressions/constant.tex}, lines
75--79:
\begin{quote}
The only operation on literals that produces a new literal (as opposed
to a constant) is concatenation by using the operator \EXP{\|}.
One may concatenate mixed string and
character literals, producing a string literal.
For example, ``\txt{foo}''\EXP{\|}''\txt{bar}'' yields ``\txt{foobar}''.
\end{quote}
\item[Route C] The same under route~C.
\end{description}

\subsection{Passages not yet revised}
\seclabel{revival-notrevised}

No passage that instantiation exclusion or the reorganized numeric types
contradict is known to be unrevised.
The rule by which a generic call over two different integer types
infers the narrowest type that both coerce into is written into
\chapref{type-inference} together with its implementation
(\secref{revival-inference}); the passages that chapter left unrevised, the
sentences of \chapref{multiple-dispatch} by which static parameters were
inferred before the applicability of a declaration was checked and before
declarations were compared, the implicit bound of \secref{type-param}, and the
rule of \chapref{overloaded-declarations} on a naked type parameter of bound
\TYP{Any}, are revised with the overloading chapters (\secref{revival-overloading},
\secref{revival-bound}).
The instance of a \KWD{nat} or \KWD{int} parameter of a declaration that a call
reaches only by dispatch, where nothing fixes it at run time, is not described;
the rule of \secref{resolving-overloading} is for type parameters (row~446 of the
revival's gap ledger).
The sentence of \secref{where-clauses} that a \KWD{where}-clause variable may
not appear as a static argument in an \KWD{extends} clause also refuses a
variable that a constraint fixes to one type; whether it is to be narrowed is
not settled.
The Meet Rule for dotted methods (\secref{more-specific-rule}) asks for a
declaration on the intersection of two parameter types; whether declarations
that together cover the intersection satisfy it too is not settled.
The type of a static call whose argument's static type stands between two
traits with \KWD{comprises} clauses, where its most specific declarations, or a
declaration more specific than them that the call may run, have static
parameters, is not given (\secref{revival-meet}).
Whether two parameter types exclude each other when the only static arguments
at which they could meet are ones that a bound rules out is not settled: the
compiled type checker takes them to exclude where the static argument that the
two parameter types force is a named type that fails the bound, and where a
static parameter that stands at the top of their meet, read as within its
declared bounds, leaves the meet empty, as for row~713's pair, which the
interpreter refuses at load; it reads no bound of a static parameter that
stands inside a static argument (\secref{overloading-static-params}).
Whether a \KWD{nat} parameter, which may appear wherever a variable of type
\EXP{\mathbb{N}32} can (\secref{natparams}), may be a component of a range is
settled (\secref{revival-inference}): it converts to \EXP{\mathbb{Z}32} as an
integer numeral does, so the static ranges of \secref{ranges} and the examples
whose ranges a \KWD{nat} parameter bounds stand as written.
Before the number chapters were revised, this section said:
\begin{quote}
The number chapters, \chapref{lib:numbers-basic} (whose integers are \secref{basic-integers})
and \chapref{lib:numbers-advanced}, are not yet revised.
Their nested tower, in which each integer type is a subtype of the rational
one above it and each carries its algebra at its own type, and the
conditional self-extension of \TYP{RationalQuantity}, are refused by
instantiation exclusion, as the compiled type checker refuses the
library's nested tower today.
They will be revised together with the reorganization of the library's
numeric types that route~A decides, and not before, because a
specification ahead of its library is as much an open discrepancy as one
behind it.
Until then the rule refuses the tower these chapters describe.
\end{quote}

\subsection{Route C, the alternative not taken}
\seclabel{revival-routec}

The team's 2012 patent applications (US~8,843,887 and US~8,898,632;
Chase, Steele, Naden, Hilburn, and Luchangco) relax the rule for a
self-typed parameterized trait: a type may be a subtype of several of its
instantiations when they form a chain.
The revival built that rule whole as an experiment and measured it.
It closes the unsoundness that instantiation exclusion guards against only
together with a rule on return types over every level of a chain and a
change in how a call chooses among generic methods specialised to
different instantiations: 188 lines of code in five files of the compiled
type checker and code generator.
Reversing route~A for route~C would also need a method per level of a
chain in the code generator, work in the interpreter, a way past a stack
overflow of the type checker on the self-typing clauses, and seven library
declarations, besides undoing the reorganization of the library's numeric
types.
Route~C rescues none of the \KWD{where}-clause examples of
\secref{where-clauses} nor the \TYP{Nothing} of \secref{convenience-types},
each of which places one type below infinitely many instantiations, which
no chain contains.
Under route~C the numeric chapters could have kept the nesting of their
widths, and their original text, quoted in \secref{revival-numbers} and the
entries after it and kept in the Working Draft of February 2011, is route~C's
for that nesting; route~C rescues neither the refined number types nor
\TYP{RationalQuantity} (\secref{revival-numbers}).
The record of the experiment is
\nolinkurl{explorations/reviews/mie-probes/route-c-experiment.md} in the
revival's repository.
\fussy
